 A TES OF A

 2

ABSTRACT
This report develops and evaluates an initial sectional population balance equation (PBE) framework
for modeling particle transport, transformation, and wall interactions in liquid nuclear systems,
with emphasis on molten-salt reactor applications. The formulation tracks suspended particulate
inventories, dissolved precursor species, and deposited surface inventories within a mass-conserving
control-volume structure. Particle populations are discretized over a sectional size grid so that size-
dependent processes, including aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition,
and resuspension, can be represented explicitly. The framework supports insoluble particles,
soluble precipitating species, and heterogeneous mixed aggregates, allowing component-resolved
mass conservation and effective particle properties to be maintained as material transfers between
dissolved, suspended, and deposited states.
The model is verified through conservation and limiting-case tests and then exercised in performance
studies involving noble-metal particles, CrF2 precipitation, heterogeneous particle mixtures, and
carrier-salt variations. The noble-metal studies show that particle growth and deposition are
controlled by coupled aggregation, breakup, turbulent transport, carrier viscosity, and wall-transfer
behavior. The CrF2 studies demonstrate the importance of dissolved–particulate coupling, where
precipitation, growth, and dissolution determine how much material enters the particulate phase
before transport and deposition occur. The heterogeneous-particle and carrier-salt studies show
that mixed aggregation and carrier properties can substantially alter particle size, mobility, and
deposition. Overall, the results demonstrate that molten-salt particle behavior should be modeled
as a coupled inventory-evolution problem rather than by a single effective removal coefficient. The
report identifies the experimental data needed to validate the present closures and outlines future
extensions for radionuclide decay, liquid–gas exchange, and solid–gas aerosol dynamics.

1. INTRODUCTION AND SCOPE

This report presents an initial theoretical framework, sectional numerical formulation, and imple-
mentation architecture for modeling the transport and evolution of trace solid particle populations
suspended in liquid media. The governing equations belong to a family of population balance
equations (PBEs), which describe the time evolution of particle size distributions under transport,
interaction, transformation, and removal processes. In this report, the particle size distribution is
represented sectionally, meaning that the continuous particle size domain is divided into discrete
size bins and the particle inventory in each bin is evolved in time.
The framework is intended for liquid-phase particulate systems in which particles may:
 • move with the carrier liquid and through size-dependent drift processes;
 • aggregate with other particles to form larger particles;
 • break into smaller fragments;
 • nucleate from dissolved precursors;
 • grow or dissolve through exchange with dissolved species;
 • deposit on solid surfaces;
 • resuspend from deposited surface inventories.
The implemented formulation advances particle mass inventories in each size section. Particle
number concentrations are reconstructed from the mass inventory when needed for number-based
kinetic processes such as aggregation. The framework also supports multicomponent particles,
where the mass of each tracked material component is stored in each size bin. This permits
calculation of effective particle density and allows component-wise mass conservation through
aggregation, breakup, deposition, and resuspension.

1.1. Applicability to Molten Salt Reactors

Molten salt reactor systems provide a motivating application to develop a PBE particle dynamics
routine because particle formation, growth, aggregation, and deposition can affect the distribution
of important materials throughout a circulating salt loop. Several particle sources and transport
pathways are of particular interest.
Corrosion-product transport. Structural materials in contact with molten salt may release alloy
constituents into solution. These dissolved species can remain soluble, precipitate as solid particles,
or become incorporated into existing aggregates. Chromium-bearing species are of particular

 16

interest because chromium release and precipitation can affect both materials performance and salt
chemistry.
Noble metal transport. Noble metals and poorly soluble fission products may form suspended
particles. These particles can agglomerate into larger particles, deposit on surfaces, or remain
mobile in the circulating salt. Size-resolved modeling is needed because settling, deposition,
breakup, and resuspension all depend on particle size.
Surface accumulation and remobilization. Deposited particles can accumulate on walls, heat-
transfer surfaces, or internal structures. Resuspension can later return this material to the flow.
Explicit deposited-inventory tracking is therefore useful for estimating material holdup and remo-
bilization.
Multicomponent particle composition. Particles in molten salt may be mixtures of noble metals,
oxides, fluorides, corrosion products, and other material classes. A multicomponent sectional
representation allows these materials to be tracked through aggregation, breakup, deposition, and
resuspension while preserving component masses.

1.2. Document Organization

The report is organized to move from physical theory to numerical implementation:

 • Chapter 2 presents the continuous population balance equation, defines the particle size
 distribution, and develops the continuous source terms for aggregation, breakup, nucleation,
 growth, dissolution, deposition, resuspension, and future decay coupling.
 • Chapter 3 develops the sectional discretization, including mass-based bin inventories, mul-
 ticomponent effective density, aggregation and breakup redistribution, dissolved coupling,
 deposited inventory, conservation properties, and precomputed sectional geometry.
 • Chapter 4 gives the prescriptive numerical formulation for coupled suspended particle,
 dissolved-species, and deposited-inventory balances in liquid control volumes.
 • Chapter 5 describes the implementation architecture, including control-volume topology,
 population modularity, source-term closures, dissolved-species coupling, deposited-inventory
 bookkeeping, caches, and reusable workspaces.
 • Chapter 6 describes verification tests for conservation, analytical aggregation limits, breakup
 redistribution, dissolved coupling, deposited-inventory exchange, multicomponent behavior,
 and transport.
 • Chapter 7 illustrates model performance for particle size and deposition model sensitivities
 for soluble, insoluble and hetergeneous particles.
 • Chapter 8 discusses future model extensions, testing and validation efforts.
 • Chapter 9 summarizes the document’s conclusions.

 17

The appendices provide supporting material on control-volume formulation, aerosol-versus-liquid
particle dynamics, interparticle force models, analytical verification solutions, physical constants,
and notation. It should be emphasized that although this document presents a thorough framework
and model evaluation, the final implementation and form of such models in any code is able to
evolve over time. Therefore, the framework and models can be considered a first iteration of a PBE
sectional method for solid-liquid dynamics for nuclear applications.

 18

2. THE CONTINUOUS POPULATION BALANCE EQUATION

This chapter presents the continuous population balance equation (PBE) framework used to describe
suspended solid particle populations in liquids. The continuous formulation provides the physical
basis for the sectional discretization developed in Chapter 3 and the implemented control-volume
formulation described in later chapters. The model represents particle transport, aggregation,
breakup, nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, and resuspension.

For particles suspended in liquids, the PBE describes the evolution of a particle size distribution
under the combined effects of transport and internal particle processes. The carrier liquid advects
and disperses particles, while particle properties such as size, density, composition, and surface
interaction behavior determine settling, aggregation, deposition, and breakup. Interparticle forces
may strongly influence whether particle encounters lead to attachment. Therefore, the aggregation
kernel is separated into a collision-frequency component and a collision-efficiency component.
This distinction is important for liquid systems in which particle surface chemistry, wetting be-
havior, ionic structure near surfaces, and particle–particle interaction potentials can strongly affect
aggregation rates.

2.1. The Distribution Function

In the simplest representation, the particle size distribution function 𝑛(𝑣, x, 𝑡) is defined so that

                                              𝑛(𝑣, x, 𝑡) 𝑑𝑣                                        (2.1)

is the number concentration of particles with volumes in the interval [𝑣, 𝑣 + 𝑑𝑣] at spatial position
x and time 𝑡 [1, 2, 3]. Here 𝑣 is particle volume, x is position, 𝑡 is time, and 𝑑𝑣 is an infinitesimal
volume interval. The number concentration is taken per unit carrier-liquid volume.

For spherical particles,
 𝜋 3 4𝜋 3 𝑚
                                     𝑣=     𝑑 =   𝑟 =    ,                                         (2.2)

where 𝜌 𝑝 is the particle material density.

where 𝜙 is dimensionless when interpreted as a particle volume fraction. These moment-like

balance formulations [1, 2].

Particles may contain more than one material constituent. In molten salt applications, for example,
particles may contain corrosion products, noble metals, precipitated salt species, oxide or oxyflu-
oride films, graphite fragments, or mixed aggregates. A fully general composition-resolved distri-
bution would require a multidimensional distribution over particle size and constituent masses. For
practical modeling, material constituents may instead be grouped into components. Such reduced
descriptions are consistent with common population-balance and aerosol mixing-state representa-
tions in which particle size and composition may both be treated as internal coordinates or grouped
state variables [3, 4].

Let

                                     U = {𝑢 1 , 𝑢 2 , . . . , 𝑢 𝑀 }                              (2.6)

denote the set of 𝑀 material species that may be present in particles. These may be partitioned
into 𝐶 components,

                   U = 𝐶1 ∪ 𝐶2 ∪ · · · ∪ 𝐶𝐶 ,              𝐶𝑎 ∩ 𝐶 𝑏 = ∅       for 𝑎 ≠ 𝑏,         (2.7)

where 𝐶𝑎 is the set of material species assigned to component 𝑎.

Each component or component group may be associated with a particle distribution,

                                           𝑛 (𝑎) (𝑣, x, 𝑡),                                      (2.8)

where the superscript 𝑎 denotes the component index.

For a system with 𝐶 components, the total particle distribution may be written

Intra-component aggregation occurs when particles within the same component collide and form a

 20

larger particle assigned to that same component:
 𝛼𝑎𝑎
                     Particle (𝑎) (𝑣 1 ) + Particle (𝑎) (𝑣 2 ) −−−→ Particle (𝑎) (𝑣 1 + 𝑣 2 ).         (2.10)

Here 𝑣 1 and 𝑣 2 are parent-particle volumes, and 𝛼𝑎𝑎 is the collision efficiency for component 𝑎
aggregating with itself. Aggregation terms of this form are rooted in the Smoluchowski coagulation
framework and its population-balance extensions [5, 2, 6].

Inter-component aggregation occurs when particles from different components collide:
 𝛼𝑎𝑏
                     Particle (𝑎) (𝑣 1 ) + Particle (𝑏) (𝑣 2 ) −−−→ Particle (𝑐) (𝑣 1 + 𝑣 2 ).         (2.11)

Here 𝛼𝑎𝑏 is the collision efficiency for components 𝑎 and 𝑏, and 𝑐 is the product component. The
product component depends on the selected representation. In some models, mixed products are
assigned to a dedicated mixed population. In others, internal component masses are tracked directly
within a multicomponent particle population [3, 4].

The interaction behavior may be summarized by a collision-efficiency matrix,

                                              𝛼   𝛼12          · · · 𝛼1𝐶

Collision efficiencies are commonly used to represent the fraction of hydrodynamic or transport-
driven encounters that result in attachment, sticking, or aggregation, and therefore provide a compact
way to encode component-dependent aggregation behavior [7, 1, 8].

Important limiting cases include:

 • non-interacting components, where 𝛼𝑎𝑏 = 0 for 𝑎 ≠ 𝑏;

 • fully interacting components, where 𝛼𝑎𝑏 ≈ 𝛼𝑎𝑎 for all pairs;

 • selective heteroaggregation, where only specified unlike-component pairs interact.

2.2. The General Transport Equation

For a single particle population, the continuous population balance equation is
 𝜕𝑛
                                  + ∇ · (u 𝑝 𝑛) = ∇ · (𝐷 𝑝 ∇𝑛) + 𝑆(𝑣, x, 𝑡),                           (2.13)
 𝜕𝑡
where u 𝑝 (𝑣, x, 𝑡) is the particle velocity, 𝐷 𝑝 (𝑣, x, 𝑡) is the particle diffusivity, and 𝑆(𝑣, x, 𝑡) is the
net source term due to particle processes. This advection–diffusion–source form is a standard
continuous population-balance representation for particulate and aerosol systems [2, 3, 1, 9].

 21

For component 𝑎, the corresponding multicomponent transport equation is

 𝜕𝑛 (𝑎)    
 + ∇ · u (𝑎)
 𝑝 𝑛 (𝑎)
 = ∇ · 𝐷 (𝑎)
 𝑝 ∇𝑛 (𝑎)
                                                                      + 𝑆 (𝑎) (𝑣, x, 𝑡),            (2.14)
 𝜕𝑡
where the superscript 𝑎 identifies the component. Such component-resolved equations are a reduced
form of more general multidimensional population balances in which size, composition, or other
internal particle coordinates may be tracked explicitly [3, 4, 10].

The source term may include intra-component aggregation, inter-component aggregation, breakup,
nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, resuspension, and externally imposed sources. For a
single population, the source term can be written schematically as

 𝑆(𝑣) = 𝑆agg (𝑣) + 𝑆break (𝑣) + 𝑆nuc (𝑣) + 𝑆growth (𝑣) − 𝑆diss (𝑣) − 𝑆dep (𝑣) + 𝑆resusp (𝑣), (2.15)

where each term denotes the continuous source or sink associated with the indicated process.
Aggregation and breakup terms are commonly treated through coagulation–fragmentation pop-
ulation balances, while nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, and resuspension introduce
additional birth, death, and transport mechanisms depending on the physical system of interest
[2, 3, 6, 11, 12, 7].

2.2.1. Particle Velocity Field

The particle velocity is represented as the carrier-fluid velocity plus drift terms such as settling:

                                       u 𝑝 (𝑣) = u 𝑓 + usettle (𝑣),                                 (2.16)

where u 𝑓 (x, 𝑡) is the carrier-fluid velocity. This decomposition is commonly used in aerosol and
particle-laden-flow transport models, with drift velocities accounting for gravitational, inertial, or
other force-driven particle motion relative to the carrier fluid [1, 9, 3].

For small spherical particles in the Stokes regime,

 (𝜌 𝑝 − 𝜌 𝑓 )𝑑 2𝑝 𝑔
                                      usettle =                        ẑ,                          (2.17)
 18𝜇
where 𝜌 𝑓 is the carrier-fluid density, 𝑑 𝑝 is particle diameter, 𝑔 is gravitational acceleration, 𝜇 is the
carrier-fluid dynamic viscosity, and ẑ is the unit vector in the settling direction. The Stokes settling
expression follows from balancing gravitational buoyancy-corrected weight with low-Reynolds-
number viscous drag on a sphere; corrections are needed outside the creeping-flow regime or when
slip, finite-inertia, or non-Newtonian effects become important [1, 13, 14, 15].

 22

2.2.2. Particle Diffusivity

The particle diffusivity may be written as

                                      𝐷 𝑝 (𝑣) = 𝐷 Br (𝑣) + 𝐷 turb ,                                  (2.18)

where 𝐷 Br is the Brownian diffusivity and 𝐷 turb is an effective turbulent diffusivity. Such an effec-
tive diffusivity representation separates molecular or Brownian particle diffusion from unresolved
turbulent dispersion in continuum transport models [1, 9, 3].

For a spherical particle in the continuum liquid regime, the Brownian diffusivity follows the
Stokes–Einstein relation,
 𝑘 𝐵𝑇
                                             𝐷 Br =            ,                                     (2.19)
 3𝜋𝜇𝑑 𝑝

where 𝑘 𝐵 is Boltzmann’s constant. This relation connects thermal fluctuations to viscous drag and
is the standard continuum-limit diffusion coefficient for spherical Brownian particles [16, 1, 13].

2.3. Source Terms in the Population Balance Equation

2.3.1. Aggregation

Aggregation occurs when two particles collide and remain attached. The continuous aggregation
source term is
 ∫ ∫ ∞
 1 𝑣 ′ ′ ′ ′ ′
        𝑆agg (𝑣) =     𝛽(𝑣 − 𝑣 , 𝑣 ) 𝑛(𝑣 − 𝑣 ) 𝑛(𝑣 ) 𝑑𝑣 − 𝑛(𝑣)     𝛽(𝑣, 𝑣′) 𝑛(𝑣′) 𝑑𝑣 ′, (2.20)
 2 0 0

where 𝛽(𝑣𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) is the aggregation kernel for particles of volumes 𝑣 𝑖 and 𝑣 𝑗 . The first term is
the birth of particles of volume 𝑣 by aggregation of smaller particles. The second term is the
loss of particles of volume 𝑣 by aggregation with any other particle. The factor 1/2 prevents
double counting of unordered particle pairs. This birth–death form is the standard Smoluchowski
coagulation contribution used in continuous population-balance formulations [5, 2, 6, 3].

The aggregation kernel is written as

                                  𝛽(𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) = 𝛼(𝑣𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) 𝛽0 (𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ),                      (2.21)

where 𝛽0 (𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) is the collision-frequency or encounter kernel and 𝛼(𝑣𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) is the collision effi-
ciency, also called the sticking probability. This factorization separates transport-driven particle
encounters from the probability that an encounter results in attachment, a common treatment in
aerosol, colloid, and aggregation models [1, 7, 9, 8].

 23

For liquid-style formulations, the encounter kernel may include Brownian, gravitational, turbulent
shear, and turbulent inertial terms:

                                    𝛽0 = 𝛽Br + 𝛽grav + 𝛽ts + 𝛽ti ,                             (2.22)

where 𝛽Br is the Brownian contribution, 𝛽grav is the gravitational differential-settling contribution,
𝛽ts is the turbulent shear contribution, and 𝛽ti is the turbulent inertial contribution. These mech-
anisms are commonly combined additively as encounter-rate contributions in practical particle
aggregation models [1, 9, 17, 18].

The Brownian contribution is

where 𝑟𝑖 = 𝑑𝑖 /2 and 𝑟 𝑗 = 𝑑 𝑗 /2. Brownian aggregation follows from diffusive relative motion of
particles and is the classical diffusion-limited contribution to the Smoluchowski coagulation kernel
[5, 16, 13, 1].

The gravitational differential-settling contribution is

                             𝛽grav (𝑑𝑖 , 𝑑 𝑗 ) = 𝜋(𝑟𝑖 + 𝑟 𝑗 ) 2 𝑢 𝑠,𝑖 − 𝑢 𝑠, 𝑗 ,               (2.24)

where 𝑢 𝑠,𝑖 and 𝑢 𝑠, 𝑗 are the settling speeds of particles 𝑖 and 𝑗. This term represents sweep-out
collisions caused by relative settling velocities between unequal-size particles [1, 9].

The turbulent shear contribution is

where 𝜀 is the turbulent kinetic energy dissipation rate and 𝜈 = 𝜇/𝜌 𝑓 is the kinematic viscosity.

where 𝑢′𝑖 and 𝑢′𝑗 are characteristic turbulent fluctuation velocities associated with particles 𝑖 and

2.3.2. Collision Efficiency and Solvent-Mediated Interparticle Forces

The aggregation kernel is written as an encounter rate multiplied by a collision efficiency,

                                                𝛽𝑖 𝑗 = 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 𝛽𝑖0𝑗 .                               (2.27)

The encounter kernel 𝛽𝑖0𝑗 gives the rate at which particles 𝑖 and 𝑗 are brought into near contact by
Brownian motion, differential settling, or turbulence. The collision efficiency 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 is the probability
that a near-contact event produces a persistent aggregate. It therefore contains the interfacial
physics that determines whether the liquid film between two approaching particles can be drained,
displaced, or ruptured [1, 7, 13, 19, 20].

Let ℎ denote the surface-to-surface separation between two particles. The pair interaction energy
is written as

 Φ𝑖 𝑗 (ℎ) = Φ𝑖vdW el solv
                                           𝑗 (ℎ) + Φ𝑖 𝑗 (ℎ) + Φ𝑖 𝑗 (ℎ).                          (2.28)

Here Φ𝑖vdW
 𝑗 is the van der Waals interaction across the liquid, Φ𝑖el𝑗 is the Coulombic or electrostatic
contribution, and Φ𝑖solv
 𝑗 is the solvent-mediated interaction associated with wetting, capillary forces,
interfacial ordering, and liquid-layer drainage [7, 21, 22, 20, 23, 24].

In dilute electrolytes, the standard model for the first two terms is Derjaguin–Landau–Verwey–
Overbeek theory, or DLVO theory:

 Φ𝑖DLVO
 𝑗 (ℎ) = Φ𝑖vdW el
                                                 𝑗 (ℎ) + Φ𝑖 𝑗 (ℎ).                               (2.29)

DLVO theory describes the competition between van der Waals attraction and electrostatic double-
layer repulsion. A large positive barrier in Φ𝑖 𝑗 suppresses aggregation, while a small or absent
barrier gives rapid, nearly diffusion-limited aggregation [21, 22, 7].

The connection between the pair potential and the collision efficiency is given by the Fuchs stability
ratio. For Brownian encounters,
 1
                                                 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 =          ,                               (2.30)
 𝑊𝑖 𝑗

with
 ∫ ∞  
 𝐵𝑖 𝑗 (𝑟) exp Φ𝑖 𝑗 (𝑟)/(𝑘 𝐵𝑇)
                          𝑊𝑖 𝑗 = 𝑅𝑖 𝑗                                             𝑑𝑟,            (2.31)
 𝑅𝑖 𝑗 𝑟2

where 𝑅𝑖 𝑗 = 𝑟𝑖 + 𝑟 𝑗 , 𝑟 = 𝑅𝑖 𝑗 + ℎ, and 𝐵𝑖 𝑗 (𝑟) accounts for hydrodynamic retardation as the liquid
drains from the gap. If the interaction potential contains a dominant barrier ΔΦ𝑖 𝑗 , the Fuchs
expression reduces to the activated form
  

 𝑘 𝐵𝑇

up to a prefactor associated with the barrier width and hydrodynamic retardation [13, 7, 1].

For ionic liquids like molten salts, the controlling barrier is often not the classical diffuse-double-
layer barrier. The ion concentration is so large that the Debye length is molecular in scale,
and a mean-field double-layer description no longer captures the dominant near-surface structure.
Instead, the liquid adjacent to a solid surface forms ordered ionic layers whose mobility depends
on the chemistry of the material–salt interface. When two particles approach, aggregation requires
this interfacial liquid to drain, rearrange, or detach from the opposing surfaces. The corresponding
solvent-mediated barrier can be much larger than the van der Waals and Coulombic contributions:

 ΔΦ𝑖solv
 𝑗 ≫ ΔΦ𝑖vdW
 𝑗  , ΔΦ𝑖solv el
                                                              𝑗 ≫ ΔΦ𝑖 𝑗 .                        (2.33)

In this regime,

 ΔΦ𝑖 𝑗 ≃ ΔΦ𝑖solv
 𝑗 , (2.34)

and the collision efficiency becomes

 ΔΦ𝑖solv
 !
 𝑗
                                     𝛼𝑖 𝑗 ≈ 𝛼0 exp −                   .                         (2.35)
 𝑘 𝐵𝑇

Thus, for strongly structured or chemically anchored interfacial layers, 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 is controlled primarily by
the solvent-mediated wetting force rather than by the classical DLVO terms [7, 21, 22, 24, 25, 26].

This observation provides a direct route from wetting measurements to the collision efficiency. For
a solid material 𝑖 in liquid ℓ, a measured contact angle 𝜃 𝑖 gives the work of adhesion through the
Young–Dupré relation,

                                       𝑊𝑖ℓad = 𝛾ℓ (1 + cos 𝜃 𝑖 ) ,                               (2.36)

where 𝛾ℓ is the liquid surface tension. The work of adhesion is an energy per unit area and
measures the strength of the material–salt wetting interaction. Only the fraction of this wetting
energy associated with an immobile or anchored interfacial layer contributes to the barrier opposing
particle contact [26, 27, 28, 29, 30, 31]. This is represented by

 Γ𝑖anch = 𝜙𝑖anch𝑊𝑖ℓad , 0 ≤ 𝜙𝑖anch ≤ 1, (2.37)

where 𝜙𝑖anch is an anchoring factor. A mobile physisorbed layer has 𝜙𝑖anch ≈ 0, while a chemically
anchored layer has 𝜙𝑖anch ≈ 1.

For a particle pair, the effective anchoring energy per unit area is

The attenuation length 𝜆solv is not determined by the contact angle alone. The contact angle

 𝜂= .

It measures the surface-to-surface distance in units of the solvent attenuation length. Thus, 𝜂 = 0
corresponds to contact, 𝜂 = 1 corresponds to a separation of one attenuation length, and larger

This scaling separates the range of the interaction, represented by 𝜆solv , from the dimensionless

oscillation period gives 𝑑layer , and the fitted dimensionless profile gives 𝜓.

Structural measurements can also constrain 𝜓. If reflectivity or scattering measurements provide
the near-surface density perturbation, then the decay and oscillation of the density profile can be
used to select the functional form of 𝜓. In this interpretation, 𝜆 solv sets the range of the interfacial
ordering, 𝑑layer sets the layering period, and 𝜓 describes how that ordering is expressed as a normal
force between approaching surfaces. The force-based route is preferred when available, because
it measures the quantity appearing directly in Eq. (2.39); structural measurements are most useful

for constraining the range and shape of the interaction when direct force measurements are difficult
[25, 24, 20, 7].

2.3.3.      Breakup

Breakup occurs when hydrodynamic or mechanical stresses overcome the cohesive strength of a
particle or aggregate. The continuous breakup source term is
 ∫ ∞
                      𝑆break (𝑣) =     𝑏(𝑣|𝑣 ′)𝑎(𝑣 ′)𝑛(𝑣 ′) 𝑑𝑣′ − 𝑎(𝑣)𝑛(𝑣),          (2.54)
 𝑣

where 𝑎(𝑣) is the breakup frequency and 𝑏(𝑣|𝑣 ′) is the daughter fragment distribution. The first term
represents the birth of particles of volume 𝑣 by fragmentation of larger particles, and the second
term represents the loss of particles of volume 𝑣 by breakup. This birth–death structure is the
standard population-balance representation of fragmentation or breakup processes [2, 3, 6, 37].
A representative Kolmogorov–Hinze maximum stable diameter is
   3/5
 𝛾br
                                  𝑑max = 𝐶KH                               𝜀 −2/5 ,                  (2.55)
 𝜌𝑓

where 𝐶KH is a model constant and 𝛾br is an effective cohesive or interfacial stress scale written
in surface-tension units. Particles smaller than 𝑑max are stable with respect to turbulent breakup
in this model. This scaling expresses the balance between turbulent inertial stresses and cohesive
or interfacial stresses and is commonly used as a closure for breakup in turbulent multiphase
dispersions [37, 3, 38, 39].
For particles larger than 𝑑max , a representative breakup frequency may be written
 ! 1/3 " #
 𝜀 𝛾br
                          𝑎(𝑑 𝑝 ) = 𝐶1                  exp −𝐶2                                  ,   (2.56)
                                         𝑑 2𝑝                              𝜌 𝑓 𝜀 2/3 𝑑 5/3
 𝑝

where 𝐶1 and 𝐶2 are closure constants. This expression captures the expectation that breakup
becomes more likely for large particles and high turbulent dissipation rates and less likely for

 30

stronger aggregates. Similar frequency closures are widely used in population-balance descriptions
of agitated dispersions and turbulent breakup, with model constants calibrated to the relevant
material and flow conditions [37, 2, 3].

2.3.4. Nucleation

Nucleation is the formation of new particles from a supersaturated dissolved precursor state.
In the continuous PBE, nucleation appears as a source concentrated at the critical nucleus size
[2, 3, 40, 41]:

                                      𝑆nuc (𝑣) = 𝐽𝛿(𝑣 − 𝑣 ∗ ),                                (2.57)

where 𝐽 is the nucleation rate and 𝑣 ∗ is the critical nucleus volume. Two formulations are retained:
a saturation-based form and a reaction-activity-based form.

For saturation-based nucleation, the supersaturation ratio is [11, 12, 42]

2.3.5. Growth and Dissolution

Growth and dissolution represent continuous size change due to mass exchange between dissolved
and particulate phases. In the continuous PBE, these processes appear as a flux in size space
[2, 3, 52, 40]:
 𝜕
                           𝑆growth (𝑣) − 𝑆diss (𝑣) = −      [𝐺 𝑣 (𝑣)𝑛(𝑣)] ,                 (2.89)
 𝜕𝑣

 𝑑𝑣

 𝑑𝑡

                                          𝐺 𝑣 = 4𝜋𝑟 2 𝐺 𝑟 ,                                 (2.91)

In saturation-based form, growth is driven by the difference 𝐶 − 𝐶eq . A combined diffusion and

 𝑉𝑚 (𝐶 − 𝐶eq )

These limiting forms correspond to growth controlled respectively by transport of dissolved growth

                                         Δ𝐺 rxn = 𝑅𝑇 ln Ω,                                         (2.97)

 Δ𝐺 rxn

 𝑁𝐴
This activity-based driving force is the thermodynamic analogue of supersaturation in precipitation,
crystallization, and mineral-growth models [11, 50, 12].

A representative reaction-activity growth law may be written as
  
 rxn 1 Δ𝜇 𝑔
                           𝐺𝑟 =         𝐷𝑚        𝑣 𝑚 + 𝑘 𝑔 𝑣 𝑚 |Δ𝜇| ,                             (2.99)

where 𝑘 𝑔 is a surface-reaction kinetic coefficient and 𝑔 is a reaction-order exponent. The sign of 𝐺 𝑟rxn
is understood to follow the sign of Δ𝜇: when Δ𝜇 > 0, the net radial rate is taken as positive and the
particle grows; when Δ𝜇 < 0, the net radial rate is taken as negative and the particle shrinks. Such
laws combine diffusion-controlled and interface-controlled contributions, with kinetic coefficients
treated as closure parameters when molecular attachment and surface-reaction mechanisms are
uncertain [12, 54, 53, 41].

If both diffusion and reaction resistance are retained, the activity-based driving force may be
converted into an equivalent interfacial concentration difference and inserted into the combined-

 36

resistance form. The dissolved-species balance receives a source term equal in magnitude and
opposite in sign to the integrated particulate growth or dissolution contribution [2, 3, 41, 51].

2.3.6. Deposition and Resuspension

Deposition removes suspended particles from the carrier liquid and transfers them to a surface
inventory. A continuous deposition sink may be written [1, 9, 7, 55]
 𝐴𝑠
                                     𝑆dep (𝑣) = −      𝑣 𝑑 (𝑣)𝑛(𝑣),                                (2.102)
 𝑉
where 𝐴𝑠 is the available surface area, 𝑉 is the control-volume volume, and 𝑣 𝑑 (𝑣) is the deposition
velocity.
The effective deposition velocity may include gravitational settling, Brownian diffusion, turbulent
transport, thermophoresis, diffusiophoresis, and particle–surface attachment:

                                       𝑣 𝑑,eff (𝑣) = 𝜖att (𝑣) 𝑣 𝑑 (𝑣),                             (2.103)

where 𝜖 att is the particle–surface attachment efficiency. It is the probability that a particle transported
to the surface remains attached rather than rebounding, sliding, detaching, or being returned to the
suspended phase. Thus, 𝜖 att plays the same role for deposition that the collision efficiency 𝛼𝑖 𝑗
plays for particle–particle aggregation in Section 2.3.2: 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 reduces a particle–particle encounter
kernel to an aggregation kernel, whereas 𝜖att reduces a transport-limited particle–surface deposition
velocity to an effective deposition velocity [19, 7, 1, 55].
As with 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 , the attachment efficiency may be interpreted as an interfacial sticking probability,

 0 ≤ 𝜖att ≤ 1.

A value 𝜖att = 1 corresponds to perfect sticking after transport to the surface, while 𝜖 att < 1
indicates that only a fraction of particle–surface encounters lead to permanent attachment. In
liquid systems, 𝜖att may depend on particle size, particle material, particle surface state, wall
material, temperature, and liquid composition through the same types of wetting, solvent-mediated,
electrostatic, dispersion, and capillary interactions that influence 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 [7, 21, 22, 26, 19].
Resuspension returns deposited particles to the suspended phase:

                                   𝑅resusp (𝑣) = 𝑘 resusp (𝑣)𝑀dep (𝑣).                             (2.104)

A representative resuspension coefficient may be written in activated form as
 " #
 𝐸 ad (𝑣)
                          𝑘 resusp (𝑣) = 𝑘 0,resusp exp −                       ,                  (2.105)
 𝑓turb 𝜌 𝑓 𝑢 2∗ 𝑑 3𝑝

where 𝑘 0,resusp is a resuspension attempt frequency or prefactor with units of inverse time, 𝐸 ad (𝑣) is
the particle–surface adhesion energy that must be overcome for detachment, 𝑓turb is a dimensionless

 37

factor representing the efficiency with which turbulent fluctuations supply detachment work, 𝜌 𝑓
is the fluid density, 𝑢 ∗ is the friction velocity, and 𝑑 𝑝 is the particle diameter. The denominator
represents a turbulent work scale formed from the wall stress scale 𝜌 𝑓 𝑢 2∗ , the particle projected
area scale 𝑑 2𝑝 , and a displacement scale of order 𝑑 𝑝 . This type of closure expresses resuspension
as competition between hydrodynamic forcing and adhesion or contact-mechanics energy barriers,
consistent with particle-impact and sticking/rebound models [26, 32, 36, 56, 33].

2.4. Soluble and Insoluble Particle Populations

Insoluble particles have negligible mass exchange with the dissolved phase. Their evolution is
controlled by transport, aggregation, breakup, deposition, and resuspension. Soluble particles
additionally exchange mass with the dissolved phase through nucleation, growth, and dissolution.
This distinction is consistent with population-balance formulations in which particle number-density
evolution is coupled to species balances when nucleation, growth, dissolution, or precipitation
processes exchange material between dissolved and particulate phases [2, 3, 41, 51].
For dissolved species concentration 𝐶𝑠 , a representative dissolved balance is
 𝜕𝐶𝑠
                      + ∇ · (u 𝑓 𝐶𝑠 ) = ∇ · (𝐷 𝑚 ∇𝐶𝑠 ) − 𝑅nuc − 𝑅growth + 𝑅diss ,              (2.106)
 𝜕𝑡
where 𝐷 𝑚 is the molecular diffusivity, 𝑅nuc is the dissolved sink from nucleation, 𝑅growth is the
dissolved sink from growth, and 𝑅diss is the dissolved source from dissolution. Such coupled
species-balance and population-balance representations are commonly used for crystallization,
precipitation, and nanoparticle-formation systems where solute depletion and particle growth are
dynamically linked [41, 51, 54, 11].
The soluble particle balance is

            𝜕𝑛 (𝑆)                                           (𝑆)    (𝑆)
                   + ∇ · (u 𝑝 𝑛 (𝑆) ) = ∇ · (𝐷 𝑝 ∇𝑛 (𝑆) ) + 𝑆agg + 𝑆break + 𝑆nuc + 𝑆growth     (2.107)
 𝜕𝑡
 (𝑆) (𝑆)
                                                                  −𝑆diss − 𝑆dep  + 𝑆resusp ,   (2.108)

where the superscript 𝑆 denotes soluble. This equation combines transport with the standard
birth, death, and internal-coordinate flux terms used for aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth,
dissolution, deposition, and resuspension in particulate population balances [2, 3, 6, 12].
Mixed systems may contain both soluble and insoluble particles, with possible heteroaggregation,
heterogeneous nucleation, surface growth, and competition for dissolved precursor mass. In such
cases, component- or composition-resolved population descriptions can be used to represent mixed
aggregates, particle mixing state, heterogeneous nucleation sites, and material transfer among
dissolved and particulate inventories [3, 4, 48, 53].

 38

3. SECTIONAL DISCRETIZATION OF THE POPULATION BALANCE
 EQUATION

This chapter develops the sectional discretization used to convert the continuous population balance
equation from Chapter 2 into a finite set of equations for particle inventories. The emphasis here
is on the mathematical structure of the sectional method: the size grid, the definition of section-
averaged quantities, the mapping of continuous source terms to discrete bins, and the conservation
properties of the resulting finite-dimensional system.

Later chapters build on this foundation. Chapter 4 gives the prescriptive control-volume equation
set and liquid-phase closure relations used in the model. Chapter 5 describes how the resulting
sectional operators are implemented in software. The present chapter is therefore limited to the
numerical discretization of the continuous PBE and avoids implementation-specific details except
where they clarify the definition of the discrete operators.

3.1. Volume Grid and Section-Averaged Quantities

The continuous particle volume domain is divided into 𝐾 finite sections. Section 𝑘 spans the
interval
                                     [𝑣 𝑘−1/2 , 𝑣 𝑘+1/2 ],
where 𝑣 𝑘−1/2 and 𝑣 𝑘+1/2 are the lower and upper section boundaries. The section width is

                                    Δ𝑣 𝑘 = 𝑣 𝑘+1/2 − 𝑣 𝑘−1/2 .                                 (3.1)

Sectional discretizations of the internal particle-size coordinate are a standard approach for con-
verting continuous population-balance equations into finite-dimensional systems while retaining
the principal moments or inventories of the distribution [40, 57, 58, 2, 3].

A geometric grid is used for most calculations because particle sizes often span several orders of
magnitude. In volume space, the grid may be defined by

                                        𝑣 𝑘+1/2 = 𝑣 1/2 𝑞 𝑘 ,                                  (3.2)

where
   1/𝐾

  1/2

spherical particles, the representative diameter is

Multicomponent or composition-resolved population balances commonly use additional internal

In the implemented model, mass is the primary conserved sectional variable, while number con-
centration is reconstructed when required by number-based kinetic processes such as aggregation.
This convention is used throughout the remainder of this report and is consistent with sectional
methods that emphasize conservation of particle volume or mass while using representative pivots
to evaluate number-based rate expressions [57, 58, 40, 3].

3.2. Derivation of the Sectional Equations

The starting point is the continuous population balance equation for a single particle population:
 𝜕𝑛
                               + ∇ · (u 𝑝 𝑛) = ∇ · (𝐷 𝑝 ∇𝑛) + 𝑆(𝑣, x, 𝑡),                       (3.13)
 𝜕𝑡
where u 𝑝 is the particle velocity, 𝐷 𝑝 is the particle diffusivity, and 𝑆 is the sum of local source
terms. This advection–diffusion–source form is the standard continuous population-balance equa-
tion for particulate systems, with the source term collecting processes such as aggregation, breakup,
nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, and resuspension [2, 3, 1, 9].

Integrating Eq. (3.13) over section 𝑘 gives
 𝜕𝑁 𝑘
                                + ∇ · (u 𝑝,𝑘 𝑁 𝑘 ) = ∇ · (𝐷 𝑝,𝑘 ∇𝑁 𝑘 ) + 𝑆 𝑘 ,                  (3.14)
 𝜕𝑡
where
 ∫ 𝑣 𝑘+1/2
                                    𝑆𝑘 =                      𝑆(𝑣, x, 𝑡) 𝑑𝑣.                    (3.15)
 𝑣 𝑘−1/2

This integration over finite intervals of the internal coordinate is the basis of sectional or discrete
population-balance methods [40, 57, 58, 2]. The section-averaged velocity and diffusivity are
evaluated at the pivot volume:

                              u 𝑝,𝑘 = u 𝑝 ( 𝑣¯ 𝑘 ),              𝐷 𝑝,𝑘 = 𝐷 𝑝 ( 𝑣¯ 𝑘 ).          (3.16)

 41

Using pivot or representative properties is a common closure in fixed-pivot and related sectional
methods, where each section is treated as locally monodisperse for evaluating size-dependent
coefficients [57, 40, 3].
For a spatially homogeneous control volume, the transport terms vanish and the sectional equation
reduces to
 𝑑𝑁 𝑘
                                                    = 𝑆𝑘 .                                      (3.17)
 𝑑𝑡
Homogeneous sectional balances of this form are frequently used to analyze local particle kinetics
independently of spatial transport [2, 3, 57].
The local source term is then decomposed into the physical mechanisms defined in Chapter 2:
 agg growth/diss dep resusp
 𝑆 𝑘 = 𝑆 𝑘 + 𝑆 break
 𝑘 + 𝑆 nuc
                                             𝑘 + 𝑆𝑘                        − 𝑆𝑘 + 𝑆𝑘        .   (3.18)

Such decompositions into aggregation, fragmentation, nucleation, growth, dissolution, and removal
terms are standard in population-balance models, with each term discretized consistently over the
sectional grid [2, 3, 6, 40].
The remainder of this chapter defines the sectional form of each term. The detailed liquid-phase
closure equations used to evaluate the rates are deferred to Chapter 4.

3.3. Aggregation: Sectional Discretization

The continuous aggregation source has the birth–death form
 ∫ ∫ ∞
 1 𝑣 ′ ′ ′ ′ ′
        𝑆agg (𝑣) =         𝛽(𝑣 − 𝑣 , 𝑣 )𝑛(𝑣 − 𝑣 )𝑛(𝑣 ) 𝑑𝑣 − 𝑛(𝑣)     𝛽(𝑣, 𝑣′)𝑛(𝑣 ′) 𝑑𝑣 ′ .      (3.19)
 2 0 0

The first term creates particles of size 𝑣 from smaller particles. The second term removes particles
of size 𝑣 when they collide with any other particle. This is the standard Smoluchowski coagulation
source term used in continuous population-balance models [5, 2, 6, 3].
Using the pivot approximation, the aggregation death term for section 𝑘 is
 𝐾
 ∑︁

                                     𝐷𝑘      = 𝑁𝑘             𝛽𝑘 𝑗 𝑁 𝑗 ,                        (3.20)

where

The symbol 𝐷 𝑘 denotes a death or removal term and is distinct from the diffusivity 𝐷 𝑝 . Evaluation
of size-dependent coagulation kernels at representative pivots is a standard closure in sectional and
fixed-pivot discretizations [40, 57, 58, 3].

 42

When particles from sections 𝑙 and 𝑚 aggregate, the product volume is

                                          𝑣 prod,𝑙𝑚 = 𝑣¯ 𝑙 + 𝑣¯ 𝑚 .                         (3.22)

Because this product volume will not generally equal a pivot volume, the product is assigned to
neighboring receiving bins. If

then the interpolation weights are
 𝑣 prod,𝑙𝑚 − 𝑣¯ 𝑘

 𝑣¯ 𝑘+1 − 𝑣¯ 𝑘
and

                                          𝜂𝑙𝑚,𝑘 = 1 − 𝜂𝑙𝑚,𝑘+1 .                             (3.24)

These coefficients are selected so that

                                𝜂𝑙𝑚,𝑘 𝑣¯ 𝑘 + 𝜂𝑙𝑚,𝑘+1 𝑣¯ 𝑘+1 = 𝑣 prod,𝑙𝑚 .                   (3.25)

Thus the sectional assignment conserves product volume, and therefore particle mass for a fixed
material density. This conservative interpolation of newly formed aggregate volume is the key idea
of fixed-pivot and adjustable-sectional methods for preserving selected moments of the particle
distribution [57, 58, 40, 2].

The aggregation birth term is
 𝐾 𝐾

The factor 1/2 avoids double counting of unordered collision pairs, as in the continuous Smolu-

 agg agg agg
                                          𝑆𝑘     = 𝐵𝑘 − 𝐷 𝑘 .                               (3.27)

For multicomponent particles, aggregation rates are computed from total sectional number con-
centrations. The component masses from the parent particles are added and redistributed using the
same coefficients 𝜂𝑙𝑚𝑘 . This conserves each tracked component independently. Component-wise
conservation is consistent with multicomponent or composition-resolved population-balance mod-
els in which aggregation changes particle size while preserving the inventories of tracked material
constituents [3, 4, 10].

3.4. Breakage: Sectional Discretization

The continuous breakage source is
 ∫ ∞

where 𝑎(𝑣) is the breakage frequency and 𝑏(𝑣|𝑣′) is the fragment distribution. This birth–death form
is the standard fragmentation contribution in coagulation–fragmentation and population-balance
models [2, 3, 6, 37].
The sectional death term is

 𝐷 break

where 𝑎 𝑘 = 𝑎( 𝑣¯ 𝑘 ). Evaluating the breakage frequency at the section pivot is consistent with the
fixed-pivot approximation used for other size-dependent kernels and rate coefficients [40, 57, 3].

 𝑆 break

Redistribution matrices of this type provide a conservative sectional representation of daughter-
fragment distributions and are commonly used to discretize breakage or fragmentation terms in
PBEs [2, 3, 40, 6].
For the default symmetric binary breakage rule, a parent particle of volume 𝑣¯ 𝑗 produces two
daughters of volume
 𝑣¯ 𝑗
                                           𝑣 daughter, 𝑗 =               .                   (3.32)
 2
The daughter volume is mapped to the sectional grid using the same mass-conserving interpolation
concept used for aggregation products. For a valid fragment distribution,
 ∑︁
                                            𝑏 𝑘 | 𝑗 = 2,                                  (3.33)
 𝑘

and
 ∑︁

 44

The first condition preserves the expected number of daughter fragments for symmetric binary
breakage, while the second preserves the parent particle volume, and therefore mass for fixed density.
Moment-conserving constraints of this kind are central to sectional and fixed-pivot population-
balance discretizations [57, 58, 40, 2].

For multicomponent particles, the default assumption is that breakage does not fractionate compo-
sition. Each daughter inherits the parent composition, so component mass fractions are conserved
during the breakage event. This assumption is consistent with composition-resolved sectional mod-
els in which breakup redistributes particle mass across size sections while preserving the tracked
material inventories [3, 4, 10].

3.5. Nucleation

The continuous nucleation source is represented as insertion at a critical nucleus volume 𝑣 ∗ :

                                       𝑆nuc (𝑣) = 𝐽𝛿(𝑣 − 𝑣 ∗ ),                                  (3.35)

where 𝐽 is the nucleation rate. Representing nucleation as a birth source at the critical or initial
nucleus size is a common treatment in population-balance models of crystallization, precipitation,
and particulate formation [2, 3, 40, 41].

In the sectional model, nucleated particles are inserted into the section 𝑘 ∗ that contains 𝑣 ∗ :

 𝐽, 𝑘 = 𝑘 ∗ ,
 
 nuc
 𝑆𝑘 = (3.36)
 0, 𝑘 ≠ 𝑘 ∗ .

This bin-insertion procedure is the sectional analogue of the continuous delta-function source and
is consistent with discrete population-balance methods for nucleation and growth [40, 57, 58, 51].
In mass form, the corresponding particle source is

                                                𝐽 𝜌 𝑝 𝑣∗, 𝑘 = 𝑘 ∗,
 
 𝑀¤ 𝑘

 0, 𝑘 ≠ 𝑘 ∗.

If the critical nucleus volume lies below the smallest represented section, the source is inserted into
the smallest bin by convention.

Nucleation also couples the particulate inventory to the dissolved inventory. The corresponding
dissolved-phase sink is treated in Chapter 4, where the liquid-phase thermodynamic closure is
specified. This coupling between a species balance and a particle population balance is standard
in crystallization and precipitation models because nucleation transfers dissolved material into the
particulate phase [41, 11, 12, 54].

3.6. Growth and Dissolution

In the continuous PBE, growth and dissolution appear as transport in particle-volume space:
 𝜕
                               𝑆growth/diss (𝑣) = −          [𝐺 𝑣 (𝑣)𝑛(𝑣)] ,                  (3.38)
 𝜕𝑣
where 𝐺 𝑣 (𝑣) = 𝑑𝑣/𝑑𝑡 is the volumetric growth rate. This internal- coordinate flux form is the
standard population-balance representation of continuous particle growth or dissolution [2, 52, 3,
11].

Integrating over section 𝑘 gives
 growth/diss
                                   𝑆𝑘              = 𝐹𝑘−1/2 − 𝐹𝑘+1/2 ,                        (3.39)

where the size-space flux is

                                   𝐹𝑘+1/2 = 𝐺 𝑣 (𝑣 𝑘+1/2 )𝑛(𝑣 𝑘+1/2 ).                        (3.40)

This conservative flux-balance form follows directly from integrating the growth-advection term
over the finite section and is commonly used in sectional population-balance discretizations [40,
58, 57, 3].

A donor-cell approximation may be used to express this boundary flux in terms of sectional
quantities:
 𝑁𝑘 𝑁 𝑘+1
                           𝐹𝑘+1/2 = 𝐺 +𝑣,𝑘+1/2            + 𝐺 −𝑣,𝑘+1/2        ,               (3.41)
 Δ𝑣 𝑘 Δ𝑣 𝑘+1
with

               𝐺 +𝑣,𝑘+1/2 = max 0, 𝐺 𝑣,𝑘+1/2 ,              𝐺 −𝑣,𝑘+1/2 = min 0, 𝐺 𝑣,𝑘+1/2 .
  
 (3.42)

The upwind or donor-cell choice selects the section upstream in volume-space advection: the
lower-volume section for positive growth and the higher-volume section for negative growth or
dissolution. This gives a conservative and directionally consistent discretization of growth and
dissolution fluxes [2, 3, 40, 58].

This flux form is the conservative sectional interpretation of growth and dissolution. The im-
plemented model may alternatively realize growth and dissolution through binwise mass-transfer
source terms. In that case, the source must still be interpreted as a discrete realization of the
same particulate–dissolved exchange process and must preserve the corresponding mass balance
[41, 51, 54, 53].

 46

3.7. Deposition and Resuspension

Deposition removes suspended particle mass from a section and transfers it to a surface-bound
deposited inventory:
 dep
 𝑑𝑀𝑘 dep resusp
                                               = 𝑅𝑘 − 𝑅𝑘             .                      (3.43)
 𝑑𝑡
This inventory-exchange representation is consistent with aerosol and colloid deposition models in
which suspended particles are removed from the carrier phase and accumulated on surfaces, with
possible later detachment or resuspension [1, 9, 7, 55].

The suspended-particle contribution is the equal and opposite exchange:
 dep resusp
 𝑆 surf
                                      𝑘    = −𝑅 𝑘 + 𝑅 𝑘              .                      (3.44)

This form emphasizes that resuspension is not an independent source of new particles. It is a
conservative return pathway from the deposited inventory to the suspended inventory. The detailed
deposition and resuspension rate closures are specified in Chapter 4. Conservative exchange
between suspended and deposited inventories is important when deposition, attachment, rebound,
detachment, and resuspension are modeled as coupled particle–surface processes [19, 7, 26, 36,
33].

3.8. Conservation and Positivity

The sectional redistribution coefficients are constructed to preserve mass. For aggregation,
 ∑︁ ∑︁
 agg agg
                                           𝐵𝑘    =       𝐷𝑘 ,                                (3.45)
 𝑘 𝑘

when no mass leaves the represented grid. For breakage,
 ∑︁ ∑︁
 𝐵break
 𝑘 = 𝐷 break
 𝑘 . (3.46)
 𝑘 𝑘

Mass- or volume-conserving redistribution is a central requirement of sectional and fixed-pivot
population-balance methods for aggregation and breakage, because aggregation changes particle
number while conserving particle volume or mass, and breakage redistributes parent mass among
daughter fragments [57, 40, 58, 2, 3].

For multicomponent populations, the same conservation condition applies componentwise. When
dissolved and deposited inventories are included, conservation applies to the combined inventory:
 𝑑  suspended 
 𝑀 + 𝑀 dissolved + 𝑀 deposited = 0 (3.47)
 𝑑𝑡

 47

in the absence of boundary transport and external sources. This combined inventory statement ex-
presses the material balance coupling among suspended particles, dissolved precursor species, and
deposited material in population-balance models with nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition,
and resuspension [2, 3, 41, 51].
All physical inventories are nonnegative:
 dep
                      𝑀𝑘 ≥ 0,          𝑀𝑘,𝑐 ≥ 0,      𝐶𝑠 ≥ 0,       𝑀𝑘      ≥ 0.              (3.48)

Maintaining nonnegative number, mass, concentration, and deposited inventories is a basic con-
sistency requirement for numerical population-balance models and for conservative sectional dis-
cretizations of particulate systems [2, 3, 57].

3.9. Summary

This chapter defined the sectional discretization of the continuous population balance equation.
The continuous particle-volume domain was partitioned into discrete sections, each represented by
a pivot volume and diameter. Aggregation, breakage, nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition,
and resuspension were expressed as sectional source terms. The key numerical requirement is
conservative mapping of continuous size-change events onto the finite grid.
The next chapter uses this sectional structure to define the control-volume liquid-phase equation set
and the closure relations used to compute the source and transport terms in practical simulations.

 48

4. PRESCRIPTIVE NUMERICAL FORMULATION FOR THE
 COUPLED LIQUID-PHASE SYSTEM

Chapter 2 introduced the continuous population balance equation and the physical mechanisms that
govern particle evolution in a liquid carrier phase. Chapter 3 converted the continuous particle-
size coordinate into a sectional representation and established the conservative mapping rules for
aggregation, breakup, growth, dissolution, deposition, resuspension, and related inventory-transfer
processes. The purpose of the present chapter is to assemble those results into the semi-discrete
balance system advanced by the numerical solver.

This chapter specifies how their contributions are combined in a control-volume formulation for
suspended particles, dissolved species, and deposited surface inventories. In this sense, the chapter
provides the operational mathematical model: the state variables, the coupled balance equations,
the residual structure, and the conservation requirements imposed on the assembled system.

4.1. State Variables, Source-Term Convention, and Residual Form

The sectional discretization developed in Chapter 3 produces a finite set of particle inventories
for each particle population, size section, and control volume. In the coupled liquid-phase sys-
tem considered here, those sectional particle inventories evolve together with dissolved-species
inventories and surface-deposited particle inventories. The purpose of this section is to define the
notation used for those quantities and to establish the source-term convention used throughout the
remainder of the chapter. Coupled sectional population-balance and species-balance formulations
of this type are commonly used for particulate systems in which transport, aggregation, breakup,
nucleation, growth, dissolution, and deposition processes are represented by finite-dimensional
inventory balances [2, 3, 57, 41].

Let 𝑖 = 1, . . . , 𝑁𝑉 denote the control-volume index, 𝑝 = 1, . . . , 𝑁 𝑃 the particle-population index,
and 𝑘 = 1, . . . , 𝐾 𝑝 the size-section index for population 𝑝. The volume of control volume 𝑖 is
denoted by 𝑉𝑖 . The representative volume and diameter of section 𝑘 in population 𝑝 are denoted
by 𝑣 𝑝,𝑘 and 𝑑 𝑝,𝑘 , respectively.

For a single-component particle population, the primary suspended particle unknown is the sectional
mass inventory

                                              𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 ,                                           (4.1)

 49

which is the total suspended mass of population 𝑝 in section 𝑘 and control volume 𝑖. For a
multicomponent population, the primary unknowns are the component masses

                                  𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑐,𝑖 ,        𝑐 = 1, . . . , 𝑁𝑐,𝑝 ,                        (4.2)

where 𝑐 indexes the particulate components. The total suspended mass in a multicomponent bin is
then
 𝑁
 ∑︁𝑐, 𝑝

                                        𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 =                    𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑐,𝑖 .                  (4.3)
 𝑐=1

Using mass or volume inventories as primary sectional variables is consistent with conservative
sectional methods, particularly when multicomponent particles or material-transfer processes must
preserve tracked constituent inventories [57, 58, 3, 4].

Dissolved chemical species are represented by concentrations

                                     𝐶𝑠,𝑖 ,         𝑠 = 1, . . . , 𝑁 𝑆 ,                          (4.4)

where 𝑠 indexes the dissolved species. When a dissolved-species balance is written in conser-
vative form, the corresponding inventory in control volume 𝑖 is 𝑉𝑖 𝐶𝑠,𝑖 , possibly multiplied by a
conversion factor when the conserved material basis differs from the concentration units. Cou-
pling dissolved concentrations to particulate inventories is standard in crystallization, precipitation,
and nanoparticle-formation PBEs because nucleation, growth, and dissolution exchange material
between the dissolved and particulate phases [41, 51, 54, 11].

Surface-deposited material associated with particle population 𝑝 and section 𝑘 is represented by
 dep
                                                𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 .                                         (4.5)

This quantity denotes material that has left the suspended phase but remains tracked as a surface
inventory within control volume 𝑖. It is therefore part of the modeled material inventory unless an
additional irreversible wall-loss or surface-reaction model is specified. Treating deposited material
as a separate inventory is useful for maintaining a conservative exchange between suspended and
surface-bound particles when deposition and resuspension are both active [1, 9, 7, 19].

The numerical state vector is written abstractly as
 𝑇

Here Msusp denotes the collection of all suspended particle mass variables, C denotes the collection
of all dissolved-species variables, and Mdep denotes the collection of all deposited surface invento-
ries. The ordering of these entries in x is not important for the mathematical formulation, provided
that the same ordering is used for the residual vector.

A distinction is made between volumetric source densities and control-volume inventory rates. A
lower-case or explicitly volumetric source density, denoted here by 𝑆, has units per unit volume per
unit time. For example,

 𝑆 (𝑚)
                                                  𝑝,𝑘,𝑖                                            (4.10)

denotes the volumetric source density contributed by mechanism 𝑚 to suspended population 𝑝,
section 𝑘, and control volume 𝑖. Uppercase 𝑄 denotes the corresponding inventory-rate contribution
appearing directly in the semi-discrete balance equation:

 𝑄 (𝑚) (𝑚)
                                            𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 𝑉𝑖 𝑆 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 .                                   (4.11)

Thus 𝑄 (𝑚)
 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 has units of mass per time when 𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 is a mass inventory. This source-density versus
inventory- rate distinction is useful for maintaining dimensional consistency in finite-volume and
sectional population-balance formulations [2, 3, 57].

Transport terms are written separately from local source terms. The quantity 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 denotes the
mass flux of suspended particles of population 𝑝 and section 𝑘 through face 𝑓 . Its units are mass
 (𝑠)
per time. Similarly, 𝐹𝐶, 𝑓 denotes the transport flux of dissolved species 𝑠 through face 𝑓 , expressed
on the inventory basis used in the dissolved balance. With this convention, an internal face
contributes equal and opposite inventory rates to the two adjacent control volumes. Conservative
flux accounting is essential for maintaining global material balances in spatially discretized transport
equations coupled to population-balance source terms [3, 1, 9].

 51

The local contribution of a physical mechanism 𝑚 is represented by an inventory-rate vector
  
                                   Q (𝑚) = Q (𝑚) x, z, 𝜽 (𝑚) ,                             (4.12)

where 𝜽 (𝑚) denotes the closure parameters for that mechanism. The vector Q (𝑚) contains the
contributions of mechanism 𝑚 to all affected inventories. For example, an aggregation contribution
modifies only suspended particle bins, whereas a growth or dissolution contribution modifies both
suspended particle inventories and dissolved-species inventories. Organizing the model as a sum
of mechanism-specific source vectors is consistent with population-balance treatments in which
aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth, dissolution, and deposition are discretized as separate
birth, death, flux, or inventory-exchange operators [2, 3, 6, 40].
The complete residual is the sum of the transport contribution and the enabled local mechanism
contributions:
 ∑︁
                                R (x, 𝑡; 𝜽) = Qtr +       Q (𝑚) .                        (4.13)
 𝑚∈M

Here M is the set of active local mechanisms, such as aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth,
dissolution, deposition, resuspension, chemical reaction, and external sources. The semi-discrete
system advanced by the time integrator is therefore
 𝑑x
                                               = R (x, 𝑡; 𝜽) .                                  (4.14)
 𝑑𝑡
This residual form is the natural finite-dimensional representation of a sectional population-balance
model after discretization of the internal particle-size coordinate and any spatial transport operators
[40, 57, 58, 3, 10].
Equations (4.6)–(4.14) establish the notation used in the remainder of this chapter. The state vector
contains the tracked inventories; the reconstruction operator provides the quantities required by
closure models; 𝑄 denotes inventory-rate source terms; 𝐹 denotes transport fluxes; and R denotes
the assembled right-hand side supplied to the time-integration method.

4.2. Suspended Particle Balance

For population 𝑝, section 𝑘, and control volume 𝑖, the suspended particle mass balance is
 𝑑𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 ∑︁
 susp
                                             = −           𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 + 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 .                  (4.15)
 𝑑𝑡 𝑓 ∈𝜕𝑖

The first term represents transport through the faces bounding the control volume. The second term
is the net local source due to particle-particle interactions, particle–solute exchange, interaction
with surfaces, and any imposed external source. This conservative finite-volume inventory form is
consistent with sectional population-balance models in which spatial transport is coupled to local
birth, death, exchange, and removal mechanisms [2, 3, 57, 40].

 52

The local suspended-particle source is written as
 susp agg gd
 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 + 𝑄 br nuc surf ext
                                         𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 + 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 + 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 + 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 + 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 .      (4.16)

Here the superscripts denote aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth/dissolution, surface ex-
change, and external addition or removal, respectively. Each contribution is evaluated using
the physical closures described in Chapter 2 and the conservative sectional mappings defined
in Chapter 3. Decomposing the local source into mechanism-specific operators is standard in
population-balance formulations, where coagulation, fragmentation, nucleation, growth, dissolu-
tion, deposition, and external forcing are discretized as separate contributions to the semi-discrete
balance [2, 3, 6, 41].

For a multicomponent particle population, Eq. (4.15) is applied componentwise:

This condition ensures that the component-resolved and total-mass forms of the population balance

For single-component populations, 𝜌 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 is the material density. For multicomponent populations,
𝜌 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 is the effective density obtained from the component masses and component densities, as
defined in Chapter 3. Reconstructing number concentrations from conserved mass or volume
inventories is common in sectional methods when number-based kernels, such as aggregation
kernels, must be evaluated from mass-conservative state variables [57, 58, 3].

4.3. Dissolved-Species Balance

Dissolved species provide the reservoir from which soluble particle populations can nucleate and
grow, and to which particles can return material through dissolution or chemical reaction. For
dissolved species 𝑠 in control volume 𝑖, the balance is

                                  𝑉𝑖           = −           𝐹𝐶, 𝑓 + 𝑄 𝑠,𝑖 .                     (4.21)

 (𝑠)
The transport flux 𝐹𝐶,  𝑓 accounts for advective and diffusive motion of the dissolved species through
the control-volume network. The local source 𝑄 diss𝑠,𝑖 is determined by reactions and particle–solute
exchange. Coupled dissolved-species and particle-population balances are standard in crystalliza-
tion, precipitation, and nanoparticle-formation models because dissolved precursor depletion and
particulate birth or growth occur simultaneously [41, 51, 54, 11]:

The sign convention is chosen so that particle growth or nucleation appears as a sink in the dissolved
balance, while dissolution appears as a source. Stoichiometric coefficients or molecular-weight
conversions are included in the definitions of the 𝑄 𝑠,𝑖 terms when dissolved species and particle
masses are represented in different units. This source-term convention enforces the material transfer
between dissolved and particulate inventories required by mass-conservative PBEs [2, 3, 41].

The particle and dissolved balances are therefore not independent. They are coupled through equal-
and-opposite inventory transfers. For a conserved material exchanged only between dissolved and

This deposited-inventory balance represents deposition and resuspension as surface exchange pro-

Equations (4.24) and (4.25) show that deposition and resuspension are bookkeeping transfers
between two tracked inventories. They do not create or destroy material unless an additional
surface reaction or external removal pathway is included. This conservative exchange structure is
consistent with particle-deposition and filtration models in which transport to a surface is followed
by attachment, rebound, detachment, or resuspension [7, 55, 19, 26].

4.5. Transport Through the Control-Volume Network

Transport couples the local inventory equations across the control-volume network. For suspended
particles, the face flux may include advection with the carrier liquid, dispersive or Brownian
transport, and size-dependent drift:
 adv diff drift
                                𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓    = 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 + 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 + 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 .                           (4.28)

This decomposition corresponds to the usual particle-transport mechanisms in aerosol and par-
ticulate population balances, where particles are carried by the fluid, dispersed by Brownian or
turbulent diffusion, and drift relative to the carrier phase under forces such as gravity [1, 9, 3].

 55

The detailed expressions for the mobility, diffusivity, and drift velocity are closure-dependent. The
numerical requirement is that the face contribution be conservative for internal faces. If face 𝑓
connects control volumes 𝑖 and 𝑗, then
 𝑑𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 𝑑𝑀 𝑝,𝑘, 𝑗

 𝑑𝑡 𝑓 𝑑𝑡 𝑓

The same structure applies to component-resolved particle fluxes. Conservative face fluxes ensure
that internal transport redistributes material between neighboring control volumes without changing
the global inventory, which is essential for finite-volume discretizations of transport-coupled PBEs
[2, 3, 1].
 (𝑠)
Dissolved species are transported by the corresponding dissolved flux 𝐹𝐶, 𝑓 , which may include
advective and molecular or turbulent diffusive contributions. For an internal face,
 𝑑𝐶𝑠,𝑖 (𝑠) 𝑑𝐶𝑠, 𝑗 (𝑠)

 𝑑𝑡 𝑓 𝑑𝑡 𝑓

Boundary faces are treated separately through prescribed inflow, outflow, wall-loss, or no-flux
conditions. The boundary treatment defines whether the modeled domain is closed or open with
respect to a given inventory. The same conservative internal-face accounting used for suspended
particles applies to dissolved-species balances coupled to nucleation, growth, dissolution, and
reaction source terms [3, 41, 51].

4.6. Aggregation and Breakup Contributions

Aggregation and breakup modify the particle size distribution without exchanging material with
the dissolved or deposited inventories. Their role in the present chapter is therefore to redistribute
suspended mass among the sectional bins while preserving the appropriate material inventory.
These processes are commonly represented as conservative coagulation–fragmentation operators
in population-balance models [2, 3, 6].

The aggregation contribution is obtained from the sectional birth–death operator defined in Chap-
ter 3 using the aggregation kernel supplied by the selected physical closure. In compact form,
  
 agg 𝐾𝑝 𝐾𝑝
                      𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = A 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 {𝑁 𝑝,𝑙,𝑖 }𝑙=1 , {𝛽 𝑝,𝑙𝑚,𝑖 }𝑙,𝑚=1 , {𝜂 𝑝,𝑙𝑚𝑘 } ,     (4.31)

where 𝜂 𝑝,𝑙𝑚𝑘 denotes the conservative product-assignment coefficient for aggregation of sections 𝑙
and 𝑚 into section 𝑘. The aggregation kernel 𝛽 𝑝,𝑙𝑚,𝑖 may include Brownian, differential-settling,
turbulent, and interaction-potential corrections, but those constitutive details are part of the closure
model rather than the residual assembly. This operator is the finite-dimensional sectional analogue
of the Smoluchowski aggregation source, with product volumes mapped to grid sections using
moment- or mass-conserving interpolation [5, 40, 57, 58].

 56

For a closed single-population aggregation event, total suspended particle mass is conserved:

Componentwise conservation is required for multicomponent or composition-resolved population
balances, where aggregation changes the particle-size distribution but must preserve each tracked
material constituent [3, 4, 10].

Breakup is represented similarly. The selected breakup closure provides a breakage frequency
and a fragment distribution, while the sectional operator maps the resulting fragments to the size
grid:

coefficient from parent section 𝑗 to receiving section 𝑘. The breakup operator is the sectional form
of the fragmentation birth–death term, with the redistribution matrix enforcing conservation of
parent mass among the daughter fragments [2, 3, 37, 6]. For a closed breakup process,
 ∑︁

                                                𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 0.                                      (4.35)

Heteroaggregation between different particle populations is treated as a coupled version of Eq. (4.31).
The source populations receive loss terms, and the configured product population receives the corre-
sponding mass. The receiving population and component mapping are part of the model definition.
Conservation is imposed on the combined set of populations participating in the interaction. This
is a reduced sectional representation of multicomponent or mixed-particle aggregation, where the
population labels and component mappings encode the product identity and material inventories
[3, 4, 7].

4.7. Nucleation, Growth, and Dissolution Contributions

Nucleation, growth, and dissolution couple the suspended particle inventories to the dissolved
species. Unlike aggregation and breakup, these processes do not merely redistribute mass over
particle size; they transfer material between phases or chemical representations. Such coupled
particle-population and dissolved-species balances are standard in crystallization, precipitation,
and nanoparticle-formation models where nucleation and growth consume dissolved precursor and
dissolution returns material to solution [2, 3, 11, 41].

 57

The nucleation contribution inserts new particle mass into the sectional grid according to the critical
size and insertion rule described in Chapter 3. The closure supplies the nucleation rate and the
composition of the newly formed particle phase. In residual form,
  
 𝑄 nuc
 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 =  N  𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 {𝐶 𝑠,𝑖 }, z𝑖 , 𝜽 nuc
 𝑝 , (4.36)

with an associated dissolved-species contribution
  

The stoichiometric coefficients 𝝂 nuc define the conversion between dissolved species and the pre-
cipitated particle material. This source-pair construction enforces the material transfer associated
with nucleation: a birth source in the particle population and a corresponding sink in the dissolved
inventory [40, 41, 51, 12].

Growth and dissolution are represented by a net particle-side exchange term 𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 . Positive values
increase suspended particle mass, while negative values remove particle mass and return it to the
dissolved inventory:

or as binwise mass-transfer terms, but in either case the particle-side and dissolved-side updates

maps define the conserved quantity. This statement is the local material-balance constraint for
coupled species-balance and population-balance models with nucleation, growth, and dissolution
[11, 41, 51, 54].

4.8. Deposition and Resuspension Contributions

Deposition and resuspension couple suspended particles to deposited surface inventories. The
deposition rate is determined by the local suspended concentration, the available surface area, and

 58

the selected deposition closure. Rather than repeating the deposition-velocity correlations from
Chapter 2, the present formulation uses the closure output directly:
  
 dep dep
                             𝑄 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = D 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 , z𝑖 , 𝐴𝑠,𝑖 , 𝜽 𝑝 .                 (4.41)

This operator represents the transfer of suspended material to a surface-associated inventory and
may include transport to the surface, particle–surface attachment efficiency, and size-dependent
deposition velocity effects [1, 9, 7, 19].

Similarly, resuspension is represented by

 𝑝 . (4.42)

The resuspension operator returns material from the deposited inventory to the suspended particle
population and may depend on hydrodynamic forcing, adhesion energy, surface roughness, particle

Here 𝑄 ext ext
 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 and 𝑄 𝑠,𝑖 denote externally imposed additions or removals that are not generated by the
internal mechanisms of the population balance. They may represent prescribed injection, purge,
sampling, filtration, chemical feed, imposed loss, or other user-specified source and sink processes.
Positive values add material to the corresponding inventory in control volume 𝑖, while negative
values remove material. These terms need not conserve the modeled inventory unless they are
paired with corresponding sources or sinks in another tracked inventory or boundary reservoir.

These equations are the prescriptive numerical formulation. They define the quantities that must
be assembled during each residual evaluation and make explicit which terms act only within the
suspended particle distribution, which terms couple particles to dissolved species, and which terms
exchange material with surfaces.

The formulation is constructed so that conservation can be checked at the operator level and at the
full-system level. For a closed control-volume network with no boundary fluxes and no external
sources, the total material inventory is
