Internal transport faces also conserve mass by construction because the same flux is subtracted
from one control volume and added to the neighboring control volume.
For multicomponent populations, the preceding identities hold for each component when the mech-
anism is composition preserving. When a mechanism changes composition, the transformation or
stoichiometric map defines the appropriate conserved material basis.

4.11. Positivity and Depletion Limits

The physical inventories in the model are nonnegative:
 dep
                  𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 ≥ 0,        𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑐,𝑖 ≥ 0,           𝐶𝑠,𝑖 ≥ 0,   𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 ≥ 0.       (4.52)

The semi-discrete equations are therefore subject to the usual positivity requirements for reactive
transport systems. Negative inventories may arise numerically if explicit time integration, stiff
exchange terms, or aggressive source terms remove more material from an inventory than is present
over a time step.
The formulation admits several equivalent approaches for preserving nonnegativity. Source terms
may be depletion limited so that a process cannot remove more material than is available. Stiff
exchange processes may be treated implicitly or with positivity-preserving integrators. Very
small negative values caused by roundoff may be projected to zero without changing the intended
conservation properties. Regardless of the particular numerical strategy, the conservation identities
in Section 4.10 should be evaluated after applying any limiting or projection step so that positivity
enforcement does not introduce unintended material loss or gain.

4.12. Residual Evaluation

A residual evaluation begins from the current packed state x(𝑡). The suspended, dissolved, and
deposited inventories are unpacked, and the reconstruction operator Z evaluates the derived quan-
tities required by the closure laws. Transport fluxes are then evaluated over the control-volume
network, and local source terms are evaluated independently in each control volume. The individual
contributions are accumulated into the suspended, dissolved, and deposited residual arrays and then
repacked into the derivative vector 𝑑x/𝑑𝑡.
This procedure corresponds directly to Eq. (4.13). The transport part of the residual couples neigh-
boring control volumes, while the local source part couples size sections, populations, dissolved
species, and deposited inventories within each control volume. The separation is useful because

 61

transport depends primarily on network topology and face properties, whereas local source terms
depend primarily on the sectional state and local thermophysical conditions.

4.13.       Summary

This chapter assembled the sectional PBE model into a complete semi-discrete liquid-phase balance
system. The unknowns are suspended sectional particle masses, dissolved-species concentrations,
and deposited surface inventories. The residual is constructed from transport, aggregation, breakup,
nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, resuspension, chemical reaction, and external source
contributions.
The chapter intentionally avoids repeating the continuous closure derivations of Chapter 2 or the
sectional redistribution derivations of Chapter 3. Instead, it establishes how those pieces enter the
numerical balance equations. The resulting formulation provides the bridge between the theoretical
model and the implementation architecture described in Chapter 5.

 62

5. COMPUTATIONAL EVALUATION OF THE SECTIONAL
 POPULATION BALANCE

Chapters 3 and 4 define the sectional population-balance model and its semi-discrete conservation
form. The purpose of this chapter is to state how that model is evaluated in a computational residual.
At each residual call, the implementation receives a packed state vector, reconstructs the physical
quantities required by the closure laws, evaluates the enabled local and transport mechanisms, and
returns the time derivative in the same packed ordering.

The central operation is
 𝑑𝑥
                                               = 𝑅(𝑥, 𝑡; Θ),                                      (5.1)
 𝑑𝑡
where 𝑥 is the packed vector of active inventories and Θ collects the fixed data and closure parameters
needed by the model. These include the section grid, control-volume geometry, material properties,
carrier-phase properties, mechanism flags, boundary data, and empirical closure coefficients. The
residual 𝑅 is not a new physical model; it is the computational assembly of the balances defined in
Chapter 4.

5.1. Residual Evaluation Through the Closure Graph

The residual evaluation is organized as an unpack–evaluate–pack operation:
 unpack closure graph pack
                            𝑥 −−−−−−→ C𝑥 −−−−−−−−−−→ C𝑥¤ −−−−→ 𝑅.                                (5.2)

Here C𝑥 is the set of state-dependent channels populated from the solver vector, and C𝑥¤ is the set of
source-rate channels produced by the closure graph. The same operation may be written compactly
as
                                   𝑅(𝑥, 𝑡; Θ) = 𝑃 G [𝑈 (𝑥), 𝑡; Θ] ,                              (5.3)
where 𝑈 unpacks the state vector, G evaluates the closure graph, and 𝑃 packs the assembled
derivative channels back into solver-vector ordering.

The closure graph evaluates quantities in dependency order. Reconstruction closures compute
derived fields such as total bin mass, number concentration, effective density, particle diameter,
diffusivity, and deposition properties. Mechanism closures then evaluate aggregation, breakup,
nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, resuspension, transport, and prescribed external sources
as needed. This organization keeps state-vector bookkeeping separate from the physical source
calculations.

 63

5.2. State Vector Layout

The state vector is stored as a sequence of population blocks, followed by any dissolved or deposited
inventories that are advanced as primary states. For 𝑁 𝑃 particle populations, the suspended-particle
portion may be written as
 𝑇
                                    𝑥 susp = 𝑥 (1) , 𝑥 (2) , . . . , 𝑥 (𝑁 𝑃 ) ,
 
 (5.4)
where 𝑥 ( 𝑝) contains the active sectional unknowns for population 𝑝.

For a single-component population, each entry represents the suspended mass inventory 𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖
in section 𝑘 and control volume 𝑖. For a multicomponent population, each entry represents
the component inventory 𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑐,𝑖 . Let 𝐾 𝑝 be the number of size sections, 𝑁𝑐,𝑝 the number of
components, 𝑁𝑉 the number of control volumes, and 𝑜 𝑝 the starting offset of population 𝑝. A
convenient linear index is

                       ℓ( 𝑝, 𝑖, 𝑘, 𝑐) = 𝑜 𝑝 + (𝑖 − 1)𝐾 𝑝 𝑁𝑐,𝑝 + (𝑘 − 1)𝑁𝑐,𝑝 + 𝑐.                  (5.5)

The precise ordering is not mathematically important, but the same ordering must be used for
unpacking, residual assembly, and packing.

Dissolved species and deposited inventories may be appended in separate blocks. If deposited
material is treated as a side inventory rather than as a primary ODE variable, the suspended
residual still includes deposition and resuspension, but the deposited inventory is updated by a
separate conservative exchange step.

5.3. Channel Representation of the Working State

The packed vector is convenient for the time integrator, but the closure models require physically
named fields. The implementation therefore uses channels: named arrays with an index set, units,
and a role in the residual evaluation. A channel may be denoted schematically as
 
                                       C𝑞 = 𝑞, I𝑞 , [𝑞], role𝑞 ,                             (5.6)

where 𝑞 is the stored quantity, I𝑞 is its index set, [𝑞] denotes its units, and role𝑞 identifies whether
the channel stores a state variable, reconstructed property, mechanism rate, or assembled residual
contribution.

During unpacking, state-vector entries are copied into the appropriate state channels. For a multi-
component population,
                                   C𝑀𝑐 ( 𝑝, 𝑘, 𝑐, 𝑖) = 𝑥ℓ( 𝑝,𝑖,𝑘,𝑐) ,                         (5.7)
while for a single-component population,

                                       C𝑀 ( 𝑝, 𝑘, 𝑖) = 𝑥ℓ( 𝑝,𝑖,𝑘,1) .                             (5.8)

 64

Reconstruction closures then compute fields needed by multiple mechanisms. The total bin mass,
component fractions, and effective density use the definitions given in Chapters 3 and 4. One
implementation-specific conversion is the number concentration,
 𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖
 𝑁 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = eff
 , (5.9)
 𝜌 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 𝑣 𝑝,𝑘 𝑉𝑖

where 𝑉𝑖 is the carrier-liquid volume of control volume 𝑖. This conversion is needed because the
evolved state is a mass inventory, while several closures are naturally written in terms of particle
number concentration.
After mechanism rates are accumulated, the derivative channels are packed back into the residual.
For a multicomponent population,
 tot
                                    𝑅ℓ( 𝑝,𝑖,𝑘,𝑐) = C𝑀¤ ( 𝑝, 𝑘, 𝑐, 𝑖),                        (5.10)

and for a single-component population,

Thus, the solver advances 𝑥, while the closure graph operates on channels with explicit physical
meaning.

5.4. Local Source Evaluation

Local sources are evaluated independently within each control volume before transport coupling
is applied. For control volume 𝑖, the local closure graph maps the local state and reconstructed-
property channels to local source-rate channels:

 G𝑖local : C𝑥,𝑖 −→ C𝑥,𝑖
 local
 ¤ . (5.12)

Aggregation and breakup are treated as birth–death mechanisms. For 𝑞 ∈ {agg, br},

                                      𝑀¤ 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 𝐵 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 − 𝐷 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 .

Here 𝐵 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 is the birth contribution and 𝐷 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 is the death contribution. The serif symbol 𝐷 is
used for death terms throughout the document. The detailed aggregation and breakup formulas are
those already defined in the earlier chapters.
Other local mechanisms transfer material between inventories. Nucleation, growth, and dissolution
exchange material between dissolved and particulate states. Deposition and resuspension exchange
material between suspended and deposited states. Each closure writes signed increments to the
affected derivative channels, and the enabled local contributions are accumulated as

where Qlocal is the set of enabled local mechanisms. For multicomponent populations, composition-
preserving mechanisms distribute rates according to reconstructed component fractions, while
composition-changing mechanisms use the specified stoichiometric or component-transfer map.

5.5. Population Coupling and Product Assignment

Populations may be evaluated independently unless a mechanism transfers material between them.
Heteroaggregation is the main example. If particles from populations 𝑎 and 𝑏 form a product
assigned to population 𝑟, the coupling closure removes material from the source populations and
adds it to the product population:

 𝑀¤ 𝑎,𝑘,𝑖

conserve the appropriate material basis across all participating populations:

5.6. Transport and Inventory Exchange

Transport couples neighboring control volumes. For an internal face 𝑓 connecting control volumes
𝑖 and 𝑗, the same face flux is applied with opposite signs to the two neighboring residual entries:

 𝑀¤ 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖
 tr
 ← 𝑀¤ 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖
 tr
                                         − 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 ,         𝑀¤ 𝑝,𝑘,
 tr ¤ tr
                                                                     𝑗 ← 𝑀 𝑝,𝑘, 𝑗 + 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 .   (5.17)

The same conservative update pattern is used for dissolved species, with the dissolved-species flux
replacing 𝐹𝑝,𝑘, 𝑓 . Boundary faces are the only transport faces that can change the total inventory of
the modeled domain.

Deposited inventories may be stored either as primary state variables or as side inventories. In both
cases, deposition and resuspension must be assembled as conservative suspended–surface exchange
terms:
 susp/surf dep
                                     𝑀¤ 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖     + 𝑀¤ 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 0                               (5.18)
for deposition and resuspension alone. If the deposited inventory is part of the state vector, its
derivative is packed into 𝑅. If it is a side inventory, the exchange is applied outside the primary
ODE vector while preserving the same material balance.

5.7. Pre-Computation, Cache Structure, and Kernel Storage

The preceding sections described the evaluation of the sectional residual as a sequence of maps from
the packed state vector to structured working fields, local source terms, transport contributions,
and finally the assembled derivative vector. The efficiency and reproducibility of that evaluation
depend strongly on which quantities are recomputed at every residual call and which quantities are
constructed once and reused. The present section defines that separation.

The sectional method contains two distinct classes of auxiliary data. The first class depends
only on the fixed sectional grid and on the chosen redistribution rules. These quantities are
independent of the evolving solution and can therefore be precomputed during model construction.
The second class depends on the current thermodynamic, hydrodynamic, or compositional state.
These quantities must be evaluated during the residual calculation, but they can often be stored
temporarily or reused when the state on which they depend has not changed. In this chapter, the
term pre-computation cache refers to the first class, while kernel storage refers to the saved pairwise
kernel data used during repeated source-term evaluations.

For a population 𝑝 with 𝐾 𝑝 sections, the static sectional cache contains the grid quantities
 grid  𝐾 𝑝
                                      C𝑝        =         𝑑 𝑝,𝑘 , 𝑣 𝑝,𝑘 , 𝑟 𝑝,𝑘 𝑘=1 ,           (5.19)

where 𝑑 𝑝,𝑘 , 𝑣 𝑝,𝑘 , and 𝑟 𝑝,𝑘 are the representative diameter, volume, and radius of section 𝑘. These
quantities are used throughout the residual evaluation, but they are not functions of x(𝑡). They
should therefore be generated once and treated as read-only data during time integration.

Aggregation requires additional pairwise geometric quantities. For each ordered or symmetric pair
of source sections (𝑙, 𝑚), the product volume is
 prod
                                             𝑣 𝑝,𝑙𝑚 = 𝑣 𝑝,𝑙 + 𝑣 𝑝,𝑚 .                           (5.20)

is the aggregation assignment cache. Its purpose is not to introduce a new approximation beyond the

is used each time the residual is evaluated. Without this cache, the implementation would repeatedly
search the sectional grid for every pairwise product volume during every residual call. With the
cache, the pairwise loop can use the stored receiver and weights directly.
The same idea applies to breakup. If the breakup rule maps a parent section 𝑗 to daughter sections
𝑘, the corresponding redistribution coefficients are stored as

For simple binary breakup, only a small number of receiving sections may be nonzero for each
parent section. For a general fragment distribution, Π br𝑝, 𝑗 𝑘 may be dense. In both cases, the cached
object is the discrete representation of the selected redistribution rule.
The complete static pre-computation cache for population 𝑝 may therefore be written as
 n o

Only the active portions of this cache are required. For example, a calculation with breakup disabled
need not construct or store C𝑝br . Likewise, a calculation without aggregation does not require the
pairwise aggregation assignment data.
Kernel storage is separate from the static sectional cache. The aggregation kernel depends on
pairwise section properties, but it may also depend on local carrier conditions such as temperature,
pressure, viscosity, fluid density, turbulent dissipation rate, ionic strength, or interparticle interaction
parameters. Let the local set of kernel-driving conditions in control volume 𝑖 be denoted
  𝑇
                                   𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 =     𝑇𝑖 , 𝑃𝑖 , 𝜇𝑖 , 𝜌 𝑓 ,𝑖 , 𝜀𝑖 , . . . .                  (5.26)

The pairwise kernel matrix for population 𝑝 in control volume 𝑖 is then
  
 grid agg
                                 𝜷 𝑝,𝑖 = K 𝑝 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 ; C𝑝 , 𝜽 𝑝 ,                                      (5.27)

where   𝐾𝑝
                                          𝜷 𝑝,𝑖 = 𝛽 𝑝,𝑙𝑚,𝑖 𝑙,𝑚=1 .
The function K 𝑝 denotes the selected kernel model, including any enabled Brownian, differential-
settling, turbulent, or interaction corrections. The present chapter is concerned only with when this
matrix is evaluated and stored, not with rederiving the closure equations that define its entries.
A saved kernel matrix is useful when the kernel-driving conditions remain unchanged over repeated
residual evaluations. For each population and control volume, the kernel cache stores both the most

 68

recently evaluated kernel matrix and the state at which it was evaluated:
 n o

                                    C𝑝,𝑖   = 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖      , 𝜷 𝑝,𝑖    .                           (5.28)

At a subsequent residual evaluation, the current kernel-driving state 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 is compared with 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖
If the difference is below the prescribed tolerance, the stored matrix is reused:

 save save
                            𝜷 𝑝,𝑖 = 𝜷 𝑝,𝑖  ,       𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 − 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖  ≤ 𝜀ker .                     (5.29)

If the difference exceeds the tolerance, the kernel matrix is recomputed and the saved entry is
overwritten:
  
 save grid agg save
                      𝜷 𝑝,𝑖  ← K 𝑝 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 ; C𝑝 , 𝜽 𝑝 ,         𝝌 𝑝,𝑖  ← 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 .           (5.30)

This cache-validity test must include every quantity on which the kernel depends. If the selected
kernel depends only on fixed bin geometry and constant carrier properties, the saved kernel may
remain valid for the entire calculation. If it depends on evolving temperature, pressure, turbulence,
dissolved chemistry, or interaction potentials, then those quantities must be part of 𝝌 𝑝,𝑖 . A kernel
should not be reused when an omitted dependency has changed. In that case, the safe algorithm is
to recompute the kernel during each residual evaluation.
The saved kernel matrix and the static aggregation assignment cache serve different purposes during
the aggregation calculation. The saved kernel contains the collision frequency for each interacting
pair of sections, whereas the assignment cache contains the destination of the product mass. For a
fixed control volume, the aggregation contribution can be viewed computationally as
  
 ¤ agg agg
                              M 𝑝,:,𝑖 = A 𝑀 𝑝,:,𝑖 , 𝑁 𝑝,:,𝑖 , 𝜷 𝑝,𝑖 , C𝑝 .                   (5.31)

 agg
The kernel matrix determines how frequently each bin pair collides, while C𝑝 determines where
the resulting product mass is placed on the sectional grid. Keeping these objects separate makes
the residual evaluation both faster and less error-prone.
Precomputed geometric factors may also be stored to accelerate kernel formation itself. Many
pairwise kernels contain repeated combinations of particle radii, such as

                                        𝑅𝑙𝑚 = 𝑟 𝑝,𝑙 + 𝑟 𝑝,𝑚 ,                                  (5.32)

or powers of this sum. Others require inverse radii, hydrodynamic correction factors, or pairwise
sums of diffusivities. These geometric factors depend only on the sectional grid and can be placed
in the static cache. Then kernel evaluation reduces to combining the cached geometric factors with
the current local carrier and interaction parameters.
Runtime work arrays form a third category of reusable data. Unlike the static assignment cache,
their contents are not valid across arbitrary residual evaluations. They hold temporary values such
as local bin masses, number concentrations, birth rates, death rates, or partial source accumulators.
Their purpose is to avoid repeated memory allocation and to make each residual evaluation follow

 69

the same sequence of operations. Mathematically, these arrays have no independent meaning after
the residual evaluation is complete.
The resulting data hierarchy is therefore

 static cache : grid geometry, assignment maps, redistribution weights,
 kernel storage : saved pairwise kernels and their validity state,
 runtime work arrays : temporary reconstructed fields and source accumulators. (5.33)

This hierarchy is the computational counterpart of the mathematical separation established in
Chapters 3 and 4. Chapter 3 defines the sectional grid and redistribution rules, which become
static cached data. Chapter 4 defines the residual terms, which are evaluated using the current
reconstructed state and, where valid, saved kernel matrices. The present chapter specifies how
those objects are organized so that the same semi-discrete equations can be evaluated repeatedly
by the time integrator.
The main benefit of this organization is that expensive operations are performed only when their
inputs have changed. Sectional product assignments do not change with time and are therefore
precomputed once. Pairwise kernels may change only when their local driving conditions change
and can therefore be saved conditionally. Temporary arrays change at every call but can reuse
allocated storage. This structure reduces computational cost without altering the governing balance
equations or the conservation properties of the sectional method.
Because this section concerns computational evaluation rather than model verification, it does not
introduce additional conservation tests or postprocessing diagnostics. Those topics are addressed
in the following chapter. The role of the cache and kernel-save procedure is narrower: it ensures
that the residual defined in Chapter 4 is evaluated efficiently, deterministically, and with consistent
sectional product assignment at every time step.

Only the parameter groups associated with enabled mechanisms are required for a given calculation.
If 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 is prescribed directly, then mechanistic 𝛼s are not needed for aggregation. If resuspension

a mechanistic closure is enabled, the corresponding row in Table 5-1 identifies the quantities that
must be supplied, tabulated, or computed from auxiliary correlations.

6. VERIFICATION

This chapter describes verification tests for the sectional population balance implementation. The
purpose of verification is to confirm that the numerical formulation correctly represents the intended
equations, preserves conservation properties, maintains nonnegative inventories, and reproduces
analytical or limiting-case solutions where available. The emphasis here is on demonstrating
consistency with the mathematical structure developed in Chapters 2 through 5 rather than on
validating specific closure parameters against experiment.
The verification program is designed to confirm that the implemented framework reproduces the in-
tended mathematical behavior of the sectional population balance model. The primary emphasis is
on conservation and consistency. In particular, verification must demonstrate that particulate mass
is conserved under aggregation and breakup, that combined particulate and dissolved inventories
are conserved under nucleation, growth, and dissolution, and that combined suspended and de-
posited inventories are conserved under deposition and resuspension. In addition to these inventory
checks, the verification suite examines whether bin-to-bin redistribution is performed correctly,
whether multicomponent effective density and component inventories are reconstructed consis-
tently, whether transport across the control-volume network remains conservative, and whether
the use of precomputed maps, caches, and reusable workspaces changes computational efficiency
without altering the mathematical result.

6.1. Mass Conservation Tests

6.1.1. Aggregation Conservation

Aggregation should conserve total particulate mass while reducing total particle number. For a
closed, homogeneous system with aggregation as the only active mechanism,

 • interpolation weights conserve total mass;

6.4. Nucleation, Growth, and Dissolution Verification

6.4.1. Nucleation Insertion

Nucleation is verified by checking that new particle mass is inserted into the bin corresponding to
the critical nucleus size 𝑣 ∗ . If 𝑣 ∗ lies below the minimum grid size, the mass should be inserted
into the smallest bin. For a nucleation rate 𝐽, the inserted particulate mass rate is

                                            𝑀¤ nuc = 𝐽𝑚 ∗𝑉,                                  (6.15)

6.4.2. Saturation-Based Growth and Dissolution

For saturation-based growth, the sign of the growth rate is verified:

                                      𝐶 > 𝐶eq ⇒ 𝑚¤ 𝑘 > 0,                                  (6.16)

The dissolved inventory must receive the equal and opposite mass source.

6.4.3. Reaction-Activity-Based Growth and Dissolution

For reaction-activity-based growth and dissolution, the sign of the thermodynamic driving force is
verified:

                                       Ω > 1 ⇒ Δ𝜇 > 0,                                     (6.18)

for growth-favored conditions, while undersaturated conditions should produce dissolution. The
particle-side and dissolved-side source terms are checked for equal and opposite mass transfer.

6.6. Control-Volume Transport Verification

6.6.1. Uniform Field Preservation

For a uniform particle concentration field in an incompressible closed flow, advection should
preserve uniformity. The test initializes

 𝐶 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 𝐶 𝑝,𝑘,0 for all control volumes 𝑖. (6.24)

With no local sources, the concentration should remain spatially uniform.

6.6.2. Conservative Transport

For a closed control-volume network with no local sources, total mass must be conserved:
 𝑑 ∑︁
                                               𝑀 𝑝,𝑘,𝑖 = 0.                                   (6.25)
 𝑑𝑡 𝑖

This verifies that face fluxes are equal and opposite between neighboring control volumes.

6.6.3. Diffusive Smoothing

For diffusion-only transport, an initially nonuniform concentration profile should smooth mono-
tonically while conserving total mass. The numerical solution is checked for:
 • nonnegative concentrations;
 • decreasing spatial variance;
 • conservation of total mass.

6.6.4. Settling Direction and Density Dependence

Settling verification checks that the settling velocity scales as

The test confirms that larger particles settle faster than smaller particles, and that particles with
density closer to the carrier-liquid density settle more slowly.

6.8. Regression Tests

In addition to one-time verification cases, the implementation should include a regression-test suite
that is rerun whenever the code, closure logic, or data structures are modified. The purpose of
these tests is to detect unintended changes in model behavior by comparing new results against
previously accepted reference solutions. In this sense, regression testing is not a separate form of
theory verification, but a practical mechanism for ensuring that an already verified implementation
remains stable as the code evolves.
A regression case is defined by a prescribed initial condition, an enabled subset of physics, and a
fixed set of closure parameters. The resulting solution is then compared against a stored reference
trajectory or a set of reference diagnostics. For aggregation-only evolution, for example, the
regression check should track the total suspended particulate mass
 ∑︁
                                     𝑀susp (𝑡) =        𝑀𝑘 (𝑡),                              (6.30)
 𝑘

and the total particle number
 ∑︁
                                      𝑁total (𝑡) =            𝑁 𝑘 (𝑡).                        (6.31)
 𝑘

The expected behavior is that 𝑀susp (𝑡) remains constant while 𝑁total (𝑡) decreases in time. For
breakup-only evolution, the same diagnostics are useful, but the expected trend is different: partic-
ulate mass remains constant, while the total particle number generally increases as large particles

 81

fragment into smaller ones. Thus, the regression suite should compare

                                      𝑀susp (𝑡) = 𝑀susp (0),                                   (6.32)

while also checking that the binwise distribution shifts toward smaller sizes in a manner consistent
with the stored reference case. For cases involving dissolved-particle exchange, the relevant
regression diagnostic is the combined inventory

 𝑀tot (𝑡) = 𝑀particulate (𝑡) + 𝑀dissolved (𝑡), (6.33)

which should remain constant in a closed system when only nucleation, growth, and dissolution are
active. A supersaturated growth case should show decreasing dissolved inventory and increasing
particulate inventory, while an undersaturated dissolution case should show the reverse. For
deposition and resuspension, the regression quantity of interest is the combined suspended and
deposited inventory,

                              𝑀surf-coupled (𝑡) = 𝑀susp (𝑡) + 𝑀dep (𝑡),                        (6.34)

where
 ∑︁

                                     𝑀dep (𝑡) =            𝑀𝑘 (𝑡).                             (6.35)
 𝑘

In a closed system with only surface exchange active, Eq. (6.34) should remain constant. For
multicomponent cases, the regression suite should preserve not only total mass but also component-
wise inventories. For component 𝑐,
 ∑︁
                                    𝑀𝑐 (𝑡) =         𝑀𝑘,𝑐 (𝑡),                              (6.36)
 𝑘

should remain unchanged under pure aggregation and breakup when no component-specific source
or sink is active. For multi-control-volume transport, the regression tests should store total system
mass,
 ∑︁ ∑︁
                                    𝑀sys (𝑡) =           𝑀𝑘,𝑖 (𝑡),                              (6.37)
 𝑖 𝑘

together with representative control-volume inventories and, where useful, selected face-flux di-
agnostics. In a closed transport-only case, Eq. (6.37) should remain constant while the spatial
distribution evolves according to the reference solution.

In practice, a regression-test record should include a compact but informative set of diagnostic
quantities rather than a full raw state dump. The most useful stored quantities are typically total
suspended mass, total deposited mass, total dissolved mass, total particle number, component
masses, selected sectional inventories, and conservation residuals such as
                                               |𝑀ref (𝑡) − 𝑀test (𝑡)|
                                 𝜖cons (𝑡) =                          ,                        (6.38)
 𝑀ref (0)

 82

where 𝑀ref (𝑡) is the stored reference diagnostic and 𝑀test (𝑡) is the value produced by the current
run. The regression suite is then passed if all selected diagnostics remain within the prescribed
tolerances over the full test window. Thus, the regression tests serve as the implementation-level
counterpart to the analytical and conservation checks described earlier in this chapter. They provide
a systematic way to ensure that future code changes do not alter the verified behavior of aggregation,
breakup, dissolved-species coupling, deposited-inventory exchange, multicomponent tracking, or
transport conservation.

6.9. Verification Summary

The verification suite is designed to confirm that the sectional implementation preserves the key
mathematical and physical properties of the model. In particular, it should demonstrate that mass
is conserved under aggregation and breakup, that combined dissolved and particulate mass is
conserved under nucleation, growth, and dissolution, and that combined suspended and deposited
mass is conserved under deposition and resuspension. It should also confirm that multicomponent
aggregation and breakup preserve individual component inventories, that the numerical solution
reproduces available analytical aggregation limits, and that particle and dissolved-species transport
remains conservative across the control-volume network. In addition, verification should show that
the use of precomputed maps, caches, and reusable workspaces changes computational cost without
changing the mathematical result, and that the numerical implementation maintains nonnegative
physical inventories throughout the simulation.

 83

 84

7. MODEL PERFORMANCE

The calculations reported in this chapter were organized around three classes: noble-metals (insol-
uble particles), CrF2 (soluble particles), and a heterogeneous aggregation/nucleation that combines
noble metals and CrF2 . These studies were constructed to exercise the major architectural elements
of the liquid-phase sectional PBE implementation under application-relevant conditions. In all
cases, the model advanced sectional particle mass inventories in time, reconstructed number con-
centrations for rate evaluation, and coupled the particle phase to breakup, deposition, resuspension,
and dissolved-species source terms. The three sweep classes differed in their active populations and
parameter variations, but they were built to share as much structure as possible so that differences
in behavior could be interpreted in terms of physics and chemistry.

7.1. Model Test Background

All three sweep classes used the same two-control-volume recirculating-loop representation. The
domain was divided into CV1 and CV2, each with a of volume 1.0 m3 . The baseline thermodynamic
state was defined by a pressure of 101, 325 Pa in both control volumes, zero vapor pressure, and a
temperature field of 960 K. The available wall surface areas were 0.25 m2 , respectively. The wall-
friction velocities were 0.08 m/s, the wall-orientation factors were unity in both control volumes,
and the baseline turbulent dissipation rates were 5.0×10−3 m2 /s3 . The two volumes were connected
by a pair of internal recirculating faces, one directed from CV1 to CV2 and one from CV2 to CV1,
each with face area 1.0 m2 , characteristic transport length 1.0 m, and nominal recirculation flow
rate 100 L/min, corresponding to 1.667 × 10−3 m3 /s.

The baseline carrier treatment in these calculations corresponded to a liquid FLiBe-like salt with
constant thermophysical properties in the reference configuration. The baseline dynamic viscosity
was 10 × 10−3 Pa s, the baseline liquid density was 1948 kg/m3 , and the thermal conductivity used
by the transport and deposition closures was 1.0 W/(m K). Where dissolved-species coupling was
active, the representative molecular diffusivity scale was 1.0 × 10−9 m2 /s. These quantities were
held fixed in the reference calculations and then selectively varied in the corresponding sweeps
where carrier-property sensitivity was of interest.

The noble-metal, CrF2 , and heterogeneous aggregation studies were each implemented as one-at-a-
time sensitivity sweeps about their respective base configurations. In each case, a single parameter
was varied while all other model inputs were held at their baseline values. Across the three
study classes, the active sweep parameters spanned thermodynamic conditions, carrier properties,
transport rates, breakup parameters, aggregation-efficiency parameters, dissolved-species initial
inventories, reaction-activity parameters.

 85

The subsequent 𝑁 × 𝑁 Cartesian-product sweeps generalize the one-at-a-time sensitivity studies by
varying selected parameter groups simultaneously rather than perturbing each input independently
about the baseline state. In this formulation, each sweep dimension corresponds to a physically
meaningful model control, and the Cartesian product of the selected parameter levels produces a
structured set of coupled cases. This allows the calculation to probe interaction effects that cannot
be observed in a single-parameter sweep, such as the combined influence of aggregation efficiency,
transport mobility, turbulent mixing, and breakup resistance on the evolving sectional distribution.
The parameters chosen for the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweeps were not arbitrary; they were selected from the
one-at-a-time studies as the inputs that produced the largest perturbations in the model response.
Consequently, the Cartesian sweeps focus computational effort on the most influential mechanisms
and provide a more demanding test of the model under combined, high-sensitivity perturbations.

Taken together, these three sweep classes provide a consistent performance-test set for the present
model. The noble-metal calculations emphasize multipopulation aggregation, mixed-population
routing, breakup, and wall exchange. The CrF2 calculations emphasize dissolved-particle coupling
and reaction-activity source terms. The heterogeneous aggregation calculations combine both
features and therefore provide the most demanding performance exercise of the current implemen-
tation. Because all three studies share the same underlying sectional PBE formulation and the same
two-control-volume recirculating-loop structure for the production cases, they provide a coherent
basis for the performance and scaling discussion that follows.

7.2. Noble Metals

7.2.1. Model Sweep Parameters

The noble-metal sweeps tracked three interacting particulate populations representing Mo, Rh, and
a mixed MoRh aggregate class. The Mo and Rh populations were treated as single-component
solid populations, while the mixed MoRh population was treated as a multicomponent population
carrying explicit component masses for Mo and Rh so that unlike-particle aggregation could be
routed into a composition-resolved product population. The baseline material properties for these
populations were specified directly in the base configuration. The Mo population used a solid
density of 10280 kg/m3 , molecular weight 0.09595 kg/mol, and representative surface-tension-
like parameter 𝜎 = 0.07, together with material interaction parameters 𝐴𝐻 = 3.5 × 10−19 J
and 𝜓0 = 0.01. The Rh population used a solid density of 12400 kg/m3 , molecular weight
0.10291 kg/mol, the same nominal 𝜎 value, and material interaction parameters 𝐴𝐻 = 5.0×10−20 J
and 𝜓0 = 0.01. The mixed MoRh population used a representative base density of 11340 kg/m3 ,
molecular weight 0.09943 kg/mol, and a two-component internal composition model with equal
initial Mo and Rh mass fractions specified for the multicomponent bookkeeping structure. All three
populations used the same geometric sectional grid, with 15 diameter bins spanning 5.0 × 10−9 to
1.0 × 10−6 m. In the baseline initial condition, the Mo and Rh populations were each seeded in the
smallest size bin in both control volumes, while the MoRh mixed population was initialized with
zero suspended mass and was populated only through the allowed aggregation pathways.

 86

The allowed population-interaction structure was an important part of the noble-metal test problem.
Self-interactions were enabled for Mo, Rh, and MoRh_mix, and cross interactions between Mo
and Rh, Mo and MoRh_mix, and Rh and MoRh_mix were all routed into the mixed MoRh_mix
population. This made the noble-metal study a genuine multipopulation aggregation problem
rather than a set of independent single-population calculations. In addition, breakup, deposition,
and resuspension were enabled in the baseline problem, while dissolved-species coupling was
disabled. The global breakup block used a binary symmetric fragmentation model with baseline
parameters 𝜎 = 0.07, 𝐶1 = 4.81 × 10−3 , 𝐶2 = 0.08, and 𝐶KH = 0.725. The global resuspension
block used a Hamaker constant of 5.0 × 10−20 J, a roughness length of 4.0 × 10−10 m, an attempt
frequency of 1.0 × 104 s−1 , and a turbulent enhancement factor of 20.0. The baseline agglomeration
block assigned unit values to the global collision-efficiency multiplier 𝛼, the kernel multiplier 𝛽,
and the mechanism-specific aggregation multipliers for Brownian, gravitational, turbulent-shear,
and turbulent-inertial aggregation.

The noble-metal one-at-a-time sweep varied the two-control-volume temperature over 850 K, 990 K,
and 1050 K, and varied the turbulence dissipation field over 1.0 × 10−3 m2 s−3 , 5.0 × 10−3 m2 s−3 ,
and 2.0 × 10−2 m2 s−3 . The carrier properties were perturbed using dynamic viscosities of 5.0 ×
10−3 Pa s, 1.0 × 10−2 Pa s, and 2.0 × 10−2 Pa s, and densities of 1700 kg m−3 , 1948 kg m−3 , and
2200 kg m−3 . The loop volumetric flow rate was swept over 50.0 × 10−3 /60.0 m3 s−1 , 100.0 ×
10−3 /60.0 m3 s−1 , and 200.0 × 10−3 /60.0 m3 s−1 . Breakup behavior was examined by varying
the surface tension over 0.05 N m−1 , 0.07 N m−1 , and 0.10 N m−1 , the dimensionless breakup
coefficient 𝐶1 over 1.0 × 10−3 , 4.81 × 10−3 , and 1.0 × 10−2 , the dimensionless coefficient 𝐶2 over
0.02, 0.08, and 0.20, and the dimensionless Kelvin–Helmholtz coefficient 𝐶KH over 0.50, 0.725,
and 1.00. Aggregation sensitivity was assessed by applying the same perturbations simultaneously
to the Mo, Rh, and MoRh_mix populations: the dimensionless global agglomeration factors 𝛼 and
𝛽, together with the dimensionless Brownian, gravitational, turbulent-shear, and turbulent-inertial
aggregation multipliers, were each swept over 0.1, 1.0, and 10.0.

In addition to the one-at-a-time studies, the noble-metal model was exercised with a focused
Cartesian-product, or 𝑁 × 𝑁, sweep to evaluate coupled parameter interactions among the inputs
most relevant to particle growth and deposition. Unlike the broader one-at-a-time study, this sweep
was restricted to six two-level parameter groups and used only global aggregation controls. The
varied parameters were the global aggregation-efficiency multiplier 𝛼, the global aggregation-kernel
multiplier 𝛽, the carrier dynamic viscosity 𝜇, the control-volume turbulent dissipation rate 𝜖, the
breakup surface-tension parameter 𝜎, and the breakup prefactor 𝐶1 . The aggregation-efficiency
multiplier was swept over 𝛼 = {0.5, 1.0}, while the aggregation-kernel multiplier was swept over
𝛽 = {0.75, 1.25}. The carrier dynamic viscosity was varied over 𝜇 = {5.0×10−3 , 2.0×10−2 } Pa s.
The turbulent dissipation rate was applied uniformly across the two control volumes using paired
values of 𝜖 = [1.0 × 10−3 , 1.0 × 10−3 ] m2 s−3 and 𝜖 = [3.0 × 10−2 , 3.0 × 10−2 ] m2 s−3 . Breakup
behavior was varied through the surface tension 𝜎 = {0.05, 0.15} N m−1 and the dimensionless
breakup prefactor 𝐶1 = {1.0 × 10−3 , 1.0 × 10−2 }. This product design allowed aggregation
strength, carrier viscous damping, turbulent mixing intensity, and breakup resistance to be varied
simultaneously, thereby testing how coupled growth-promoting and growth-limiting mechanisms
influence the noble-metal particle-size distribution and deposition response.

The updated noble-metal deposition sweep retained the two-control-volume Mo–Rh–MoRh_mix

 87

base problem, while focusing on parameters most relevant to particle growth and wall transfer.
Six two-level parameter groups were varied simultaneously: the global aggregation-efficiency
multiplier 𝛼 = {0.01, 1.0}, the global aggregation-kernel multiplier 𝛽 = {0.5, 2.0}, the carrier
dynamic viscosity 𝜇 = {5 × 10−3 , 2 × 10−2 } Pa s, and paired control-volume turbulent dissipation
rates of 𝜖 = [10−3 , 10−3 ] m2 s−3 and 𝜖 = [5 × 10−2 , 5 × 10−2 ] m2 s−3 . Breakup behavior was
varied through the surface tension 𝜎 = {0.03, 0.12} N m−1 and the dimensionless prefactor
𝐶1 = {10−3 , 10−2 }. These input cases spanned weak and strong aggregation, low and high viscous
damping, low and high turbulent mixing, and weak and strong breakup resistance.

7.2.2. Particle Size

Rankings for the single-parameter tests are show in in Figure 7-1. The single-parameter sweeps
show that the parameters producing a strong, systematic change in particle size is the global
wetting/sticking parameter. Increasing the sticking parameter from 0.01 to 0.5 to 1.0 shifts the final
cumulative number-fraction distributions toward larger diameters and increases the number-mean
diameter histories. This indicates that the dominant size-change mechanism is agglomeration-
driven depletion of the fine-particle number population. As sticking efficiency increases, a larger
fraction of particle-particle encounters produces successful attachment; therefore, small particles
are consumed more rapidly, the number distribution migrates to larger bins, and the characteristic
particle diameter increases. Across comparable salt–temperature groups, increasing the sticking
parameter raises the number-mean diameter scale by roughly a factor of 2–4. The same trend
appears in the final mean occupied bin index and largest-bin metrics: stronger sticking shifts the
number distribution upward in bin space and accelerates the accumulation of mass in the coarsest
resolved bin.
By contrast, temperature and carrier properties have comparatively weak effects on noble-metal
particle size in the single-parameter sweeps. Increasing temperature from 850 K to 923 K to 1000 K
produces only modest increases in the particle-size metrics at fixed salt and sticking parameter. The
direction is physically consistent because higher temperature generally reduces carrier viscosity
and increases particle mobility, but the magnitude of the change is small compared with the change
caused by sticking efficiency.
Among the one-at-a-time noble-metal sweeps, the parameters that produced the weakest particle-
size perturbations were the control-volume temperature, the carrier density, and the loop volumetric
flow rate. Changing the temperature field produced only secondary changes in the final particle-
size metrics, indicating that the temperature-dependent mobility changes were not strong enough
to substantially reorganize the size distribution. Similarly, sweeping the carrier density had only a
modest influence on particle size compared with the aggregation controls, because density mainly
modifies gravitational and inertial transport pathways rather than directly changing the probability
that collisions form larger agglomerates. The loop flow-rate sweep also produced relatively weak
size changes; its main role was to alter inter-volume exchange and residence behavior rather than
directly drive coarsening. These weak-response parameters therefore acted as indirect transport
modifiers, in contrast to the aggregation-efficiency parameters and selected breakup parameters,
which more directly controlled the balance between coalescence-driven growth and fragmentation-
driven size suppression.

The single-parameter results therefore show both the important and unimportant controls. The
sticking parameter has the largest effect because it directly controls the conversion of collisions
into agglomerates. Carrier properties have a secondary effect through carrier viscosity and density,
but it produces only modest shifts in final number distributions and largest-bin mass fraction.
Temperature has the weakest effect among the single-sweep variables; it slightly changes transport
properties but does not substantially change the final particle-size structure. In practical terms,
changing the salt or temperature alone is not an effective way to strongly control noble-metal
particle diameter over the tested range. The single-parameter ranking is

 sticking efficiency > salt-dependent carrier properties > temperature.

 Figure 7-2. Results of the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep on particle size. Each sub-box details from top-left depicts
 high-low, high-high, low-high, low-low for the two given parameters.

The focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep provides a more direct mechanistic ranking of the parameters that control
noble-metal particle growth (Figure 7-2). This sweep varied the global aggregation-efficiency
multiplier 𝛼, the global aggregation-kernel multiplier 𝛽, the carrier-fluid dynamic viscosity 𝜇, the

 90

turbulent dissipation rate 𝜖, the breakup surface-tension parameter 𝜎, and the breakup prefactor
𝐶1 . The strongest growth controls in the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep are 𝛼 and 𝛽. Increasing 𝛼 increases the
probability that a collision results in successful sticking, while increasing 𝛽 increases the magnitude
of the aggregation kernel. Therefore, when 𝛼 and 𝛽 increase, fine-particle number is removed more
rapidly and the number-mean diameter increases. The high-𝛼, high-𝛽 cases therefore define the
strongest-growth block. The low-𝛼, low-𝛽 cases define the weakest-growth block because collision
events are less frequent or less effective.
Carrier viscosity is the next important parameter. Increasing 𝜇 from 0.005 to 0.02 Pa s suppresses
Brownian diffusivity, settling mobility, and turbulence-assisted collision transport. As a result,
high-𝜇 cases show slower fine-particle depletion and smaller final number-mean diameters. Lower-
𝜇 cases promote particle mobility and increase the frequency of effective encounters, so the size
distribution moves upward through the bins more efficiently. Unlike temperature, the explicit
viscosity variation in the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep produces a clearer mechanistic effect because it directly
changes the transport terms that feed aggregation.
The breakup parameters have a measurable but secondary effect compared with 𝛼, 𝛽, and 𝜇.
Increasing 𝜎 from 0.05 to 0.15 stabilizes larger particles against breakup, allowing coarse particles
to persist and increasing the largest-bin mass fraction. Increasing 𝐶1 from 10−3 to 10−2 increases
the breakup frequency, fragments large aggregates, repopulates smaller bins, and suppresses the
final particle size. These effects are most visible in the largest-bin mass fraction and cumulative
distribution diagnostics because those plots directly show whether mass remains in the coarse tail
or is redistributed back into smaller particles. However, the breakup parameters do not dominate
the sweep unless they are paired with weak aggregation or high turbulence. When aggregation is
strong, breakup modifies the terminal size distribution but does not fully prevent coarsening unless
the breakup settings are also unfavorable.
The parameter with the least direct and least monotonic effect on particle size is 𝜖. Increasing 𝜖
from 10−3 to 3 × 10−2 m2 s−3 affects both sides of the population balance. It can increase turbulent
collision rates, which promotes agglomeration and increases particle size, but it can also increase
turbulent breakup, which fragments coarse particles and decreases particle size. Because these
two effects oppose one another, 𝜖 does not produce a simple monotonic particle-size trend across
the sweep. In high-𝛼, high-𝛽, high-𝜎, low-𝐶1 cases, increased 𝜖 can enhance growth if turbulent
collisions dominate. In low-𝜎, high-𝐶1 cases, increased 𝜖 can suppress growth if turbulent breakup
dominates. Therefore, 𝜖 is best interpreted as a regime-dependent modifier rather than a primary
independent size-control parameter.
Several parameters therefore have little or no practical effect on particle size over the tested ranges.
In the single-parameter sweeps, temperature has only a weak effect on the final number spectrum
and mean occupied bin index compared with the sticking parameter. In the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep, 𝜖
has no consistent monotonic effect because its aggregation-enhancing and breakup-enhancing
roles partially cancel. Breakup parameters can matter, but they are not the leading controls
unless aggregation is weak or turbulence makes fragmentation competitive. These weak-sensitivity
results are important because they indicate that noble-metal particle size is not equally sensitive
to all transport-property changes. The model responds most strongly to parameters that directly
control collision success and aggregation rate, while parameters that only perturb carrier properties
or act through competing mechanisms produce smaller or conditional changes.

 91

The growth-favorable parameter combination is

                       𝛼 ↑,      𝛽 ↑,      𝜇 ↓,        𝜎 ↑,        𝐶1 ↓,     𝑇↑.

Under these conditions, collisions are frequent, sticking probability is high, fine-particle number
is depleted rapidly, and the aggregates that form are resistant to breakup. The growth-suppressing
parameter combination is

                       𝛼 ↓,      𝛽 ↓,      𝜇 ↑,        𝜎 ↓,        𝐶1 ↑,     𝑇↓.

Under these conditions, collisions are less effective, particle mobility is reduced, and large particles
that form are more readily fragmented.

 Table 7-1. Ranking of scanned parameters controlling noble-metal particle size.
 Rank Parameter Growth-favorable direction Observed importance
  1 𝛼 Increase Strong, direct increase in collision sticking
  2 𝛽 Increase Strong, direct increase in aggregation rate
  3 𝜇 Decrease Moderate/strong effect through particle mobility
  4 𝜎 Increase Secondary effect through coarse-particle survival
  5 𝐶1 Decrease Secondary effect through reduced fragmentation
  6 𝜖 Conditional Weak/non-monotonic
  7 𝑇 Increase Weakest single-sweep size effect

The overall ranking from the focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep is

                                   𝛼 ≈ 𝛽 > 𝜇 > 𝜎 ≈ 𝐶1 > 𝜖 > 𝑇 .

When the single-parameter carrier/temperature results are included, sticking efficiency remains the
dominant control, salt-dependent viscosity is secondary, and temperature has only a minor influence.
Overall, noble-metal particle size is controlled primarily by agglomeration-driven depletion of fine-
particle number. Parameters that increase collision effectiveness and preserve coarse aggregates
increase the final particle size, while parameters that suppress mobility, reduce sticking, or enhance
fragmentation keep the distribution finer. Parameters such as temperature, salt identity, and 𝜖
should be treated as secondary or conditional size controls over the ranges tested because they do
not produce the same systematic size shifts as 𝛼, 𝛽, and 𝜇.

7.2.3. Particle Deposition

The noble-metal deposition sensitivities were evaluated first using the single-parameter salt, tem-
perature, and wetting/sticking sweeps, and then using a focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep of deposition-relevant
transport parameters. Unlike the particle-size sweeps, which is controlled primarily by agglom-
eration efficiency, the deposition response is controlled primarily by parameters that increase
wall-directed particle transport. The single-parameter sweep shows that the largest systematic
deposition changes are associated with salt-dependent carrier properties and temperature, while the

 92

 No b l e M et al Par am et er - Pai r Ef f ec t i v e M as s Tr an s f er Res p o n s e

  Figure 7-3. Results of the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep on mass transport. Each sub-box details from top-left depicts
 high-low, high-high, low-high, low-low for the two given parameters.

wetting/sticking parameter has only a secondary effect on deposition. This distinction is important
because the wetting/sticking parameter primarily changes the suspended particle-size distribu-
tion, whereas deposition depends on the ability of gravity, thermophoresis, diffusiophoresis, and
turbulence to move particles from suspension to the wall.

The wetting/sticking parameter has comparatively little direct effect on deposition in the single-
parameter sweep. Increasing the sticking parameter from 0.01 to 0.5 to 1.0 strongly affects noble-
metal particle size by promoting agglomeration, but it only weakly changes the final deposition
metrics. Its effect on deposition is indirect: stronger sticking produces larger particles, which can
slightly increase gravitational settling and can shift the particle-size regime sampled by the wall-

 93

transfer models. However, the wetting/sticking parameter does not directly increase the wall-transfer
coefficients. Therefore, changing wetting/sticking changes deposition much less than changing salt
identity or temperature. The same parameter that dominates noble-metal particle size does not
dominate noble-metal particle deposition.

The mechanism-resolved single-parameter results show that turbulence-driven deposition is the
dominant wall-removal pathway. Gravity is the next most important contribution, thermophoresis
is smaller but non-negligible. The turbulence contribution is largest in high viscosity. The
gravity contribution follows the same broad salt and temperature trends, but it is more sensitive
to the availability of coarse particles and to the density and viscosity combinations that control
settling. Thermophoresis also increases in hotter, lower-viscosity salts, but it remains smaller than
turbulence. The single-parameter mechanism ranking is

 turbulence > gravity > thermophoresis.

The focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 deposition sweep provides a clearer ranking of the wall-transport controls, as
seen in Figure 7-3. This sweep varied temperature 𝑇, friction velocity 𝑢 ∗ , thermal-gradient forcing
∇𝑇, viscosity 𝜇, and density 𝜌. The final total deposition rate spans approximately 2 × 10−8 to
1 × 10−7 kg s−1 ,which corresponds to an approximately fivefold variation in terminal deposition
rate. The peak total deposition rate reaches approximately 2 × 10−5 kg s−1 showing that deposition
is strongest during an early transient when the suspended particle inventory and transport driving
forces are largest. The final effective mass-transfer coefficient spans approximately 5 × 10−7 to
2 × 10−6 m s−1 , indicating an approximately fourfold variation in terminal wall-transfer efficiency
across the scanned deposition parameter space.

The dominant parameter in the 𝑁 × 𝑁 deposition sweep is the friction velocity 𝑢 ∗ . Increasing
𝑢 ∗ from 0.02 to 0.2 increases near-wall turbulent transport and raises the deposition-rate scale
by roughly an order of magnitude in the individual run histories. In low-𝑢 ∗ cases, the total
deposition-rate histories remain on the order of 10−6 kg s−1 . In high-𝑢 ∗ cases, the rate scale
increases to approximately 10−5 kg s−1 . Therefore, increasing 𝑢 ∗ increases turbulent mixing and
particle delivery to the wall, resulting in the strongest systematic increase in total deposition rate
and effective mass-transfer coefficient. This makes 𝑢 ∗ the primary practical control on noble-metal
deposition in the transport-focused sweep.

Thermal-gradient forcing is the next major deposition control when it is active. This mechanism
is important because it provides a directed wall-loss pathway that does not require particles to
be large enough for gravitational settling to dominate. However, thermophoresis is conditional
on the imposed thermal gradient; when the gradient is absent, this contribution is essentially
inactive. Its magnitude is also modified by viscosity, temperature, and the suspended particle-size
distribution.

Density primarily affects the gravitational contribution. Increasing 𝜌 from 1700 to 2200 kg m−3 in-
creases gravitational settling, especially for larger particles. Therefore, high-density cases produce
larger gravity deposition rates when coarse suspended particles are available. However, density is
not the dominant global deposition parameter because gravity is not the largest mechanism in most
cases. Its effect is strongest in cases with appreciable coarse-particle mass and weaker in cases

 94

where the suspended inventory is dominated by smaller particles or where turbulence dominates
the total wall flux.

Viscosity generally suppresses deposition. Increasing 𝜇 from 0.005 to 0.02 Pa s increases drag,
decreases particle mobility, and reduces settling and near-wall transport efficiency. Therefore,
high-𝜇 cases generally have lower gravitational and turbulence-mediated deposition rates than
comparable low-𝜇 cases. Viscosity is important because it modifies several deposition mechanisms
at once, but in the 𝑁 × 𝑁 deposition sweep its effect is secondary to 𝑢 ∗ and to the activation of
directed transport mechanisms such as thermophoresis.

Several parameters have little or no practical effect on deposition over the tested ranges. In
the single-parameter sweep, wetting/sticking has only a weak direct effect on deposition, even
though it strongly affects particle size. It changes deposition only indirectly by changing the
particle-size distribution available for gravity or turbulence to remove. Density has little effect on
non-gravitational deposition mechanisms and only becomes important when coarse particles are
available for settling. Temperature also has a secondary effect compared with 𝑢 ∗ , because it mainly
changes transport properties rather than directly imposing wall-directed particle motion. Thus, the
weak-sensitivity parameters are not irrelevant, but their influence depends on whether they couple
to an active deposition pathway.

The deposition-favorable parameter combination is

                            𝑇 ↑,      𝑢 ∗ ↑,     ∇𝑇 ,      𝜌 ↑,      𝜇↓.

Under these conditions, turbulent near-wall delivery is strong, thermophoresis provides directed
transport toward the colder wall, condensation adds diffusiophoretic forcing, gravitational settling
is enhanced, and viscous drag is reduced. The strongest deposition cases are therefore the high-
temperature, high-𝑢 ∗ , active-∇𝑇, active-condensation, high-density, low-viscosity combinations.

The deposition-suppressing parameter combination is

                         𝑇 ↓,       𝑢 ∗ ↓,     ∇𝑇 = 0,        𝜌 ↓,      𝜇↑.

Under these conditions, turbulent wall delivery is weak, thermophoretic and diffusiophoretic drift
are absent, gravitational settling is reduced, and viscous drag is increased. The lowest deposition
cases are therefore the cool, low-𝑢 ∗ , no-gradient, no-condensation, low-density or high-viscosity
cases.

 Rank Parameter Deposition-favorable direction Primary effect
  1 𝑢∗ Increase Strongly increases turbulent wall delivery
  2 ∇𝑇 Increase Activates thermophoretic deposition
  3 𝜌 Increase Increases gravitational settling of coarse particles
  4 𝜇 Decrease Reduces drag and increases particle mobility
  5 𝑇 Increase Indirectly improves
  6 𝜖 Increase Weak indirect effect through particle-size changes

 95

The overall ranking from the transport-focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep is

                                     𝑢 ∗ > ∇𝑇 > 𝜌 ≈ 𝜇 > 𝑇 > 𝜖,

with wetting/sticking treated as a weak indirect contributor to deposition. Mechanistically, noble-
metal deposition is maximized when turbulent transport, thermophoretic drift, condensation-driven
drift, and gravitational settling act together. Deposition is minimized when wall-directed forcing
is weak or absent, especially in low-𝑢 ∗ , no-gradient, no-condensation cases where particles remain
suspended and the wall-transfer coefficient is small.

7.3. Chromium Fluoride

7.3.1. Model Sweep Parameters

The CrF2 performance calculations used the same two-control-volume recirculating framework and
one-at-a-time sensitivity philosophy introduced in the preceding noble-metal study. Instead, the
CrF2 study extends that framework to a single reactive CrF2 particle population coupled to dissolved
Cr2+ . The shared nonchemical perturbations were retained where applicable to provide a direct
comparison with the noble-metal calculations, but the distinctive feature of the CrF2 sweep is the
addition of chemistry-dependent controls for initial dissolved Cr2+ , nucleation, growth, dissolution,
solubility, activity, diffusivity, and reaction enthalpy. As a result, the CrF2 cases test not only the
previously described transport, agglomeration, deposition, resuspension, and breakup behavior, but
also the coupling between dissolved-species availability and the evolving precipitated particle-size
distribution. It should be noted that the CrF2 modeled here is a representative reaction and not of
actual CrF2 formation that occurs in molten salt systems.
The key differences between the CrF2 sweep and the noble-metal sweep arose from the presence
of dissolved-species chemistry and reaction-activity-driven particle formation and removal. The
initial dissolved Cr2+ inventory was varied over 0.25 kg m−3 , 0.50 kg m−3 , and 1.00 kg m−3 in the
two control volumes, while the activity of fluorine was kept constant at one, representing the ready
availability of fluorine in a fluoride molten salt. The shared reaction-activity thermochemical defini-
tion was then swept through the nucleation pathway and mirrored programmatically into the growth
and dissolution closures where appropriate. Within this shared chemistry block, the dimensionless
nucleation solubility-product reference parameter 𝐾sp,ref was varied over 10−8 , 10−7 , and 10−6 , the
interfacial free-energy parameter 𝛾 was varied over 0.10 N m−1 , 0.20 N m−1 , and 0.40 N m−1 , the
dissolved-species diffusivity parameter 𝐷 𝑚 was varied over 3.0 × 10−10 m2 s−1 , 1.0 × 10−9 m2 s−1 ,
and 3.0 × 10−9 m2 s−1 , and the reaction enthalpy Δ𝐻rxn was varied over −2.0 × 104 J mol−1 ,
0 J mol−1 , and 2.0 × 104 J mol−1 . The activity-value specification for the reaction-activity closure
was also varied through a set of five dimensionless cases that independently altered the Cr2+ activity
coefficient and the constant fluoride activity contribution, namely 𝛾act = 0.5, 1.0, 2.0. In addition
to these shared thermochemical variations, the growth closure retained independent kinetic sen-
sitivity through the growth prefactor 𝑘 𝑔 , which was varied over 10−7 , 10−6 , and 10−5 , with units
dependent on the selected growth-order exponent 𝑔, and the dimensionless growth-order exponent
𝑔, which was varied over 0.5, 1.0, and 2.0. The dissolution closure was likewise given its own

 96

independent sensitivity study, with dimensionless dissolution 𝐾sp,ref varied over 10−6 , 10−5 , and
10−4 , dissolution 𝑘 𝑔 varied over 10−7 , 10−6 , and 10−5 , again with units dependent on the dissolution
reaction-order exponent, and the dimensionless dissolution reaction-order exponent varied over 0.5,
1.0, and 2.0. Thus, whereas the noble-metal study primarily emphasized the coupled effects of
transport, aggregation, breakup, deposition, and resuspension on insoluble particle populations, the
CrF2 study added a second layer of sensitivity associated with dissolved precursor loading, thermo-
dynamic driving force, activity corrections, and kinetic exchange between dissolved and particulate
inventories. This made the CrF2 sweep the principal test bed for performance changes associated
with precipitation chemistry and dissolved–particulate coupling in the present implementation.
The selected inputs to investigate particle size growth sensitivity were the population agglomeration-
efficiency multiplier 𝛼, varied over 0.5 and 1.0; the agglomeration-kernel multiplier 𝛽, varied over
0.75 and 1.25; the initial dissolved Cr2+ inventory, varied over 0.25 and 1.00 kg/m3 in the two
control volumes; the reaction-activity nucleation interfacial parameter 𝛾, varied over 0.10 and
0.40; the loop recirculation flow rate, varied over 50 and 200 L/min; and the control-volume
temperature field, varied over 850 and 1123 K. As implemented in the sweep driver, the nucleation
reaction-activity chemistry was also enforced consistently across the coupled nucleation, growth,
and dissolution closures wherever those fields were intended to remain shared, so that each product-
sweep case represented a physically coherent perturbation of the CrF2 precipitation system rather
than a set of independently altered submodels. This particular 𝑁 × 𝑁 design was chosen because
these six parameters directly control the balance among thermodynamic driving force, nucleation
barrier height, dissolved precursor availability, aggregation intensity, and residence-time effects,
all of which strongly influence how rapidly particulate mass is created and transferred toward larger
sizes. Consequently, the CrF2 particle-size sweep provided a targeted way to identify combinations
of chemistry and transport conditions that favor rapid particle growth, larger final number-mean di-
ameters, and stronger computational stiffness associated with tightly coupled dissolved–particulate
source terms.

7.3.2. Particle Size

The CrF2 sweep results (Figure 7-4) showed that particle-size metrics were substantially more
sensitive than the bulk phase-partition metrics. Across the one-at-a-time studies, the final suspended
mass, final dissolved inventory, and final deposited mass often remained within a relatively narrow
range, while the number-based measures of particle size changed more visibly. In particular, the
final number-mean diameter and related particle-size diagnostics showed that the sweep primarily
altered how suspended CrF2 mass and number were distributed across particle sizes rather than
how much total CrF2 ended in the suspended, dissolved, or deposited states. The final number-
based distributions remained strongly weighted toward the fine-particle end, while the mass-based
distributions were much more strongly shifted toward larger particles. This indicates that the system
frequently ended with many small particles by number but with a large fraction of suspended
particulate mass carried by comparatively large particles.
Among the one-at-a-time sweep parameters, the clearest effects on particle size came from the
aggregation and reaction-driven particle-formation parameters. Increasing the global aggregation-
efficiency multiplier 𝛼 increased the effectiveness of particle attachment during collisions and

therefore favored larger final particles. Increasing the aggregation-kernel multiplier 𝛽 had the same
qualitative effect by increasing the overall aggregation rate and accelerating transfer of particulate
mass toward coarser particles. The mechanism-specific aggregation multipliers followed the same
pattern. Increasing the Brownian, gravitational, turbulent-shear, or turbulent-inertial aggregation
factors strengthened the corresponding collision pathways and increased coarsening, while reducing
those multipliers preserved a larger fine-particle population and lowered the final number-mean
diameter. In the number-based metrics, these aggregation-parameter changes produced some of the
largest shifts in final particle size because they act directly on the balance between particle-number
loss and coarse-particle growth.

The reaction-activity parameters also produced strong particle-size sensitivity, although their effects
operated through the competition between new-particle formation and growth of existing particles.
Increasing the initial dissolved chromium inventory increased the amount of precursor available
for precipitation, but the sign of the size response depended on whether that additional dissolved
mass primarily fed growth of existing particles or intensified production of new fine particles.
Increasing the nucleation interfacial parameter 𝛾 strengthened the nucleation barrier and altered
the rate at which new particles were formed, thereby shifting the balance between sustained
fine-particle production and subsequent coarsening. Increasing the reference solubility parameter
weakened the net precipitation driving force, increased the final dissolved inventory, and reduced
the extent of suspended-particle growth, thereby tending to suppress coarsening. Changes in
growth and dissolution prefactors and reaction-order exponents further modified particle size by
changing how rapidly particulate mass could be transferred into or out of the suspended phase once
thermodynamic driving was established. These chemistry-related effects were most apparent in the
final number-mean diameter and related size-distribution diagnostics, confirming that the sweep
sensitivity resided primarily in redistribution across particle sizes rather than in large changes to
total mass.

By contrast, many of the transport-property and operating-condition variations produced only
modest changes in final particle size. Changes in carrier viscosity, carrier density, loop recirculation
rate, wall-friction velocity, turbulent dissipation rate, breakup constants, and deposition-related
transport inputs did affect the transient response, but they did not generally produce the strongest
shifts in final CrF2 number-mean diameter when varied one at a time. These parameters altered
residence times, inter-control-volume mixing, breakup activity, and wall-transfer rates, but their
influence on the final particle-size state was usually weaker than that of the aggregation multipliers
and the reaction-activity parameters that directly control particle formation and coarsening. The
resulting interpretation is that the CrF2 particle-size response was governed mainly by the balance
between particle birth, growth, and aggregation rather than by strong variation in the final bulk
phase partitioning.

The focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 particle-size sweep clarified which combinations of parameters most strongly
favored larger or smaller suspended CrF2 particles. In that product sweep, the varied parame-
ters were the aggregation-efficiency multiplier 𝛼, the aggregation-kernel multiplier 𝛽, the initial
dissolved chromium inventory, the reaction-activity nucleation interfacial parameter 𝛾, the loop
recirculation flow rate, and the uniform control-volume temperature. The resulting trends were
physically consistent across the size-based diagnostics, as showed in Figure 7-5. Increasing 𝛼
from 0.5 to 1.0 increased the effectiveness of collisions and therefore favored larger final particles.

 Figure 7-5. Results of the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep on CrF2 particle size. Each sub-box details from top-left depicts
 high-low, high-high, low-high, low-low for the two given parameters.

 Reference: median final mean radius = 1.032 μm

Increasing 𝛽 from 0.75 to 1.25 strengthened the aggregation kernel and likewise favored larger final
sizes by accelerating transfer of particulate mass into coarser particles. Increasing 𝛾 from 0.10
to 0.40 favored smaller final particles because stronger nucleation sustained a larger fine-particle
population and lowered the final number-mean diameter. Increasing the initial dissolved chromium
inventory from 0.25 to 1.00 kg/m3 could either increase final particle size, when the additional pre-
cursor primarily supported growth of existing particles, or decrease final size when it instead drove
a stronger nucleation burst and preserved a larger suspended fine-particle population. Increasing
temperature from [930, 930] to 990 K generally favored larger final particles when the hotter state
promoted net growth relative to persistent fine-particle retention, while increasing loop flow rate
from 50 to 200 L/min altered the size response indirectly through residence-time redistribution and
stronger inter-control-volume mixing.

Larger final particles were favored by higher aggregation efficiency, higher aggregation-kernel
magnitude, lower effective fine-particle production, and conditions in which the dissolved precursor

 100

inventory was converted preferentially into growth of existing particles rather than into repeated
formation of new small particles. Smaller final particles were favored by stronger nucleation and
by conditions that preserved a high suspended particle count at small size. Quantitatively, the
coarse-particle-favoring cases occupied the upper end of the resolved number-mean diameter range
and showed stronger signatures of coarsening, whereas the fine-particle-favoring cases retained
larger suspended particle numbers and more strongly fine-weighted number distributions. The
combined interpretation of the one-at-a-time and 𝑁 × 𝑁 studies is therefore that CrF2 particle
size is controlled primarily by the competition between fine-particle production and coarse-particle
growth: parameters that strengthen aggregation increase final particle size, while parameters that
strengthen sustained small-particle production decrease it.
The 𝑁 ×𝑁 results therefore define the combinations of conditions that favor coarse or fine suspended
CrF2 populations. The growth-favorable parameter combination is

                                  𝛼 ↑,       𝛽 ↑,         𝛾 ↓,     𝑇 ↑,

with the dissolved Cr2+ inventory favoring growth when the available precursor is consumed
primarily by growth of existing particles rather than by repeated nucleation of new fine particles.
Under these conditions, collisions are more effective, the aggregation kernel is stronger, fine-particle
number is depleted more rapidly, and dissolved chromium is transferred preferentially into coarser
suspended particles. The corresponding growth-suppressing parameter combination is

                                  𝛼 ↓,      𝛽 ↓,          𝛾 ↑,    𝑇↓.

together with dissolved-inventory conditions that sustain nucleation and preserve a large number
concentration of small particles. Under these conditions, particle collisions are less effective at
producing larger agglomerates, new fine particles continue to replenish the lower-size bins, and
the final number-mean diameter remains smaller. The loop recirculation rate acted mainly as a
conditional transport modifier: changing 𝑉¤ altered residence-time distribution and inter-control-
volume mixing, but it was not a primary independent control on particle size compared with the
aggregation and reaction-activity parameters. Overall, the focused product sweep shows that CrF2
particle size is governed primarily by the competition among particle birth, growth of existing
particles, and aggregation-driven depletion of fine-particle number.

 Table 7-3. Ranking of scanned parameters controlling CrF2 particle size.
 Rank Parameter Growth-favorable direction Observed importance
  1 𝛼 Increase Strong, direct increase in collision effectiveness
  2 𝛽 Increase Strong, direct increase in aggregation rate
  3 𝛾 Decrease Affects fine-particle production
  4 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 Conditional Feeds growth or nucleation
  5 𝑇 Increase Affects reaction and transport properties
  6 𝑉¤ Conditional Indirect effect of residence time and mixing

The overall ranking from the focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep is

                                    𝛼 ≈ 𝛽 > 𝛾 > 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 > 𝑇 > 𝑉¤ .

 101

Thus, the strongest coarsening cases are those that combine high aggregation efficiency, high
aggregation-kernel strength, and reaction conditions that favor growth of existing particles over
sustained nucleation. The finest final distributions occur when aggregation is weak and the chem-
istry continues to maintain a large population of newly formed small particles. Parameters such as
temperature and loop flow rate should therefore be interpreted as secondary or conditional particle-
size controls over the ranges tested, whereas 𝛼, 𝛽, and 𝛾 define the dominant size-response structure
for the CrF2 product sweep.

7.3.3. Particle Deposition
