 Figure 7-6. Results of the 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep on CrF2 particle deposition. Each sub-box details from top-left
 depicts high-low, high-high, low-high, low-low for the two given parameters.

The CrF2 deposition results showed substantially weaker sweep sensitivity in the final bulk deposi-
tion metrics than in the particle-size metrics. Across the one-at-a-time studies, the final deposited
mass remained relatively tightly clustered, and the total deposition-rate histories generally stayed

 102

within the same order of magnitude across the sweep. In the global summaries, the final suspended
mass, final dissolved inventory, and final deposited mass often changed only weakly even when the
suspended particle-size distribution changed more noticeably. This indicates that, over much of the
sweep space, the parameter variations altered the size and number distribution of suspended CrF2
more strongly than they altered the ultimate amount of material transferred to the walls.

Among the one-at-a-time sweep parameters, the most direct controls on deposition were the wall-
transport parameters that enter the individual deposition mechanisms. Increasing the wall-friction
velocity increased the turbulence-driven deposition contribution and therefore increased both the
instantaneous deposition rate and the effective wall-transfer coefficient. Increasing the magnitude of
the cold-wall thermal gradient increased the thermophoretic contribution and likewise increased the
total deposition rate, the normalized deposition rate, and the effective mass-transfer coefficient.

The chemistry and particle-formation parameters influenced deposition primarily through their
effect on the suspended particle population presented to the wall rather than through direct modi-
fication of the deposition closures. Increasing the initial dissolved chromium inventory increased
the available suspended CrF2 mass and therefore increased the total deposition rate and the control-
volume deposition-rate histories. Increasing the nucleation source strength changed the balance
between fine-particle production and growth, which in turn altered the relative strength of the
individual deposition mechanisms. When the suspended population remained finer, gravity-driven
deposition weakened while the relative importance of thermophoretic and turbulence-driven trans-
port increased. When the suspended population became coarser, gravity contributed more strongly
to the total wall flux. Temperature acted in a similarly indirect way. Changing the control-volume
temperature altered fluid properties, reaction driving forces, and the evolving suspended particle-
size distribution, but its overall effect on the deposition metrics was generally weaker than that
of the direct wall-transport parameters and weaker than its effect on particle-size diagnostics. As
a result, temperature changes modified deposition largely by changing the size and amount of
suspended particulate inventory available for removal rather than by producing a fundamentally
different wall-transfer regime.

The focused 𝑁 × 𝑁 deposition sweep (Figure 7-6) clarified which combinations of parameters most
strongly increased or decreased CrF2 wall transfer. In that product sweep, the varied parameters were
the initial dissolved chromium inventory, the nucleation interfacial parameter 𝛾, the control-volume
temperature, the wall-friction velocity, the control-volume thermal gradient, and the condensation
mass flux. The trends were physically consistent across deposited-mass, deposition-rate, and
effective-transfer diagnostics. Increasing the initial dissolved chromium inventory from 0.25 to
1.00 kg/m3 increased the deposition response by increasing the amount of suspended particulate
mass available for wall removal. Increasing wall-friction velocity from 0.02 to 0.20 m/s increased
the turbulence-driven deposition contribution and therefore increased total deposition rate and
effective wall-transfer efficiency. Increasing 𝛾 changed deposition indirectly through its effect on
the suspended particle population: stronger nucleation favored finer suspended particles and altered
the balance among the deposition mechanisms, while weaker nucleation allowed more coarsening
and therefore strengthened gravity-driven removal. Temperature again acted through a coupled
response involving fluid properties, suspended particle size, and reaction-driven evolution, but it
did not produce the same direct and robust changes in deposition efficiency as the thermal-gradient
and wall-friction inputs.

 103

Taken together, the deposition results define the combinations of conditions that favor strong or
weak CrF2 wall removal. The deposition-favorable parameter combination is

                                 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 ↑,    𝑢 ∗ ↑,     ∇𝑇 ↑

with the thermal gradient oriented so that thermophoresis drives particles toward the colder wall.
Under these conditions, more suspended CrF2 particulate material is available for removal, turbulent
near-wall delivery is stronger, thermophoretic drift provides a directed wall-transfer pathway. The
deposition-suppressing parameter combination is

                                  𝐶Cr2+ ,0 ↓,    𝑢 ∗ ↓,     ∇𝑇 = 0.

Under these conditions, the suspended particulate inventory is smaller, turbulent wall delivery is
weak and thermophoretic drift is absent. The nucleation interfacial parameter 𝛾 and temperature
𝑇 acted more conditionally: they changed deposition mainly by modifying the suspended particle
population and its size distribution rather than by directly changing the wall-transfer closure. Lower
effective fine-particle production and greater coarsening can strengthen gravity-driven removal,
whereas sustained fine-particle production shifts the suspended inventory toward smaller particles
and reduces the gravitational contribution. Overall, the CrF2 deposition sweeps showed that wall
removal was controlled by two coupled pathways: the amount and size of suspended particulate
material presented to the wall, and the strength of the active wall-transfer mechanisms.

 Table 7-4. Ranking of scanned parameters controlling CrF2 particle deposition.
 Rank Parameter Deposition-favorable direction Primary effect
  1 𝑢∗ Increase Strongly increases turbulence-driven wall delivery
  2 ∇𝑇 Increase Activates thermophoretic deposition
  3 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 Increase Increases particulate inventory for deposition
  4 𝛾 Conditional Changes particle size production
  5 𝑇 Conditional Modifies reaction, fluid , and particle-size state

The overall ranking from the focused deposition sweep is

                                     𝑢 ∗ ≈ ∇𝑇 > 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 > 𝛾 ≈ 𝑇 .

Thus, the strongest CrF2 deposition cases are those that combine a large suspended particulate
inventory with direct wall-transfer forcing, especially high wall-friction velocity and an active cold-
wall thermal gradient. The weakest deposition cases occur when the available suspended particulate
inventory is small and the direct wall-transfer mechanisms are inactive or weak. Mechanistically,
deposition is therefore less sensitive than particle size to many chemistry and aggregation param-
eters, because those parameters affect wall removal mainly through the suspended particle field,
whereas 𝑢 ∗ and ∇𝑇 directly control the dominant turbulent and thermophoretic transfer pathways.

 104

7.4. Heterogeneous aggregation

7.4.1. Model sweep parameters

For the one-at-a-time heterogeneous-aggregation sweep, the base model included five particle
populations: Mo, Rh, MoRhmix , CrF2 , and CrF2,mix . Only the Mo and Rh populations were
initialized with particulate mass; the mixed-metal, CrF2 , and mixed-CrF2 populations were initially
empty. In the base case, each control volume contained 0.05 kg of Mo and 0.05 kg of Rh in
the first size bin. The sweep varied the initial first-bin Mo mass over 0.00, 0.05, and 0.10 kg,
and independently varied the initial first-bin Rh mass over the same values. The base dissolved
chromium inventory was initialized with 0.80 kg/m3 of Cr2+ in CV1 and 0.20 kg/m3 in CV2.
For the one-at-a-time cases, the dissolved chromium inventory was varied using spatially uniform
values of 0.20, 0.80, and 1.00 kg/m3 in both control volumes.
Base CrF2 precipitation model used a dimensionless nucleation parameter 𝛾 = 0.20, a growth
coefficient 𝑘 𝑔 = 1.0×10−6 in model growth-rate units, and a dissolution coefficient 𝑘 diss = 1.0×10−6
in model dissolution-rate units. These parameters were varied over 𝛾 = {0.05, 0.20, 0.50}, 𝑘 𝑔 =
{5.0 × 10−7 , 1.0 × 10−6 , 5.0 × 10−6 }, and 𝑘 diss = {5.0 × 10−7 , 1.0 × 10−6 , 5.0 × 10−6 }. The base
aggregation parameters were 𝛼 = 1.0 and 𝛽 = 1.0, both dimensionless. The sweep varied these
over 𝛼 = {0.5, 1.0, 1.5} and 𝛽 = {0.75, 1.0, 1.25}.
Carrier-fluid and loop conditions were 𝑇 = 990 K in both control volumes, a loop flow rate of
𝑉¤ = 100.0 × 10−3 /60.0 m3 /s, a carrier viscosity of 𝜇 = 10.0 × 10−3 Pa s, and a carrier density of
𝜌 = 1948.0 kg/m3 . The one-at-a-time sweep varied the uniform temperature over 900, 990, and
1150 K; the loop flow rate over 50.0 × 10−3 /60.0, 100.0 × 10−3 /60.0, and 200.0 × 10−3 /60.0 m3 /s;
the carrier viscosity over 5.0 × 10−3 , 10.0 × 10−3 , and 20.0 × 10−3 Pa s; and the carrier density over
1800.0, 1948.0, and 2200.0 kg/m3 .
The particle-growth NxN sweep varied the initial dissolved Cr2+ inventory, the CrF2 –noble-metal
aggregation efficiency, the CrF2 –noble-metal aggregation multiplier, and temperature. The dis-
solved Cr2+ inventory was varied using spatially uniform two-control-volume values of 0.25 kg/m3
and 1.00 kg/m3 . The CrF2 –noble-metal aggregation efficiency was varied over 𝛼CrF2 −NM = 0.5 and
1.0 dimensionless, and the CrF2 –noble-metal aggregation multiplier was varied over 𝛽CrF2 −NM =
0.75 and 1.25 dimensionless. The temperature was varied using spatially uniform two-control-
volume values of 850 K and 1123 K. This product sweep separates source-driven effects from
heterogeneous-aggregation effects. The initial dissolved Cr2+ inventory controls the amount of
chromium-bearing material available for particle formation, while 𝛼CrF2 −NM and 𝛽CrF2 −NM control
how efficiently that material is transferred into mixed CrF2 -bearing populations.
A separate deposition-enabled NxN sweep was used to evaluate how heterogeneous aggregation
interacts with surface removal. This sweep varied temperature, wall-friction velocity, thermal
gradient, carrier viscosity, and carrier density. The temperature was varied using spatially uniform
two-control-volume values of 850 K and 1050 K. The wall-friction velocity was varied using
spatially uniform values of 𝑢 ∗ = 0.02 m/s and 0.20 m/s. The thermal gradient was varied using
spatially uniform values of ∇𝑇 = 0.0 K/m and −200.0 K/m. The carrier viscosity was varied
over 𝜇 = 0.005 Pa s and 0.020 Pa s, and the carrier density was varied over 𝜌 = 1700 kg/m3

 105

and 2200 kg/m3 . The deposition-enabled product sweep therefore identifies how particle mobil-
ity, turbulent wall transfer, thermal-gradient-driven wall transfer, and density-dependent transport
combine with heterogeneous aggregation.

7.4.2. Particle Size

The one-at-a-time results showed that particle growth is controlled by two classes of parameters:
source parameters that determine how much CrF2 -bearing particulate material exists, and aggre-
gation parameters that determine how efficiently that material is transferred into mixed and larger
particles. For total CrF2 -bearing particle growth, the strongest one-at-a-time controls are the CrF2
growth coefficient 𝑘 𝑔 , the initial dissolved Cr2+ inventory, the CrF2 nucleation parameter 𝛾, the
heterogeneous aggregation efficiency 𝛼, the aggregation multiplier 𝛽, and the dissolution coefficient
𝑘 diss . Carrier viscosity, carrier density, temperature, and flow rate are secondary modifiers.
When 𝑘 𝑔 increases, CrF2 -bearing particles grow more strongly because dissolved material is
transferred onto existing particles. This increases the number-mean diameter and shifts CrF2 -
bearing mass-fraction distributions toward larger diameters. When the initial dissolved Cr2+
inventory increases, more chromium-bearing material is available for particulate conversion, so
CrF2 and CrF2mix populations gain mass and can coarsen. When 𝛾 increases, more small CrF2 -
bearing particles are generated. This increases particle number and provides more feedstock for
mixed-particle formation, but it can keep the number-weighted size distribution finer if new-particle
formation exceeds aggregate coarsening.
When 𝛼 increases, more cross-population collisions are accepted as successful heterogeneous
aggregation events. As a result, Mo, Rh, and CrF2 -bearing particles are transferred more efficiently
into mixed populations. When 𝛽 increases, the aggregation kernel is strengthened, so mixed-
particle formation and coarsening occur faster. When 𝑘 diss increases, CrF2 -bearing particulate
material is returned to the dissolved phase, suppressing particle persistence and limiting CrF2 -
bearing growth.
The NxN particle-growth results confirm the one-at-a-time interpretation. When the initial dis-
solved Cr2+ inventory increases, the amount of CrF2 -bearing material available for particle for-
mation increases. This parameter therefore provides the strongest source-driven control in the
heterogeneous-aggregation product sweep. When the CrF2 –noble-metal aggregation efficiency
𝛼CrF2 −NM increases, cross-population collisions are more likely to produce mixed aggregates.
When the CrF2 –noble-metal aggregation multiplier 𝛽CrF2 −NM increases, the aggregation kernel
is strengthened and mixed-particle formation is accelerated. When temperature 𝑇 increases, the
coupled reaction and transport rates are modified, but temperature acts as a secondary modifier
relative to the source and aggregation parameters.
The growth-favorable parameter combination for the heterogeneous-aggregation NxN sweep is

                      𝛼CrF2 −NM ↑,      𝛽CrF2 −NM ↑,      𝐶Cr2+ ,0 ↑,    𝑇↑.

Under these conditions, more CrF2 -bearing material is available, cross-population collisions are
more effective, the aggregation kernel is stronger, and material is transferred more efficiently into

 106

larger mixed particles. The corresponding growth-suppressing parameter combination is

                       𝛼CrF2 −NM ↓,       𝛽CrF2 −NM ↓,       𝐶Cr2+ ,0 ↓,    𝑇↓.

Under these conditions, less CrF2 -bearing material is available, cross-population collisions are less
effective, mixed-particle formation is weaker, and the final suspended population remains more
strongly weighted toward smaller or unmixed particles.
Table 7-5 summarizes the qualitative ranking of the scanned NxN parameters controlling heterogeneous-
aggregation particle size. The ranking follows the same format as Tables 7-1 and 7-3: parameters
are ordered by their influence on particle-size growth, the growth-favorable direction is indicated,
and the final column identifies the physical interpretation of the response.

  Table 7-5. Ranking of scanned NxN parameters controlling heterogeneous-aggregation particle size.
 Rank Parameter Growth-favorable direction Observed importance
  1 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 Increase Increases CrF2 -bearing material.
  2 𝛼CrF2 −NM Increase Increases cross-population mixed-particle .
  3 𝛽CrF2 −NM Increase Increases mixed population aggregation.
  4 𝑇 Increase Changes reaction and transport behavior.

The overall ranking from the heterogeneous-aggregation NxN sweep is therefore

                                 𝐶Cr2+ ,0 > 𝛼CrF2 −NM ≈ 𝛽CrF2 −NM > 𝑇 .

This ranking indicates that the largest changes in particle size are obtained when sufficient CrF2 -
bearing source material is present and the cross-population aggregation parameters are high. Tem-
perature modifies the final size distribution, but over the scanned range it is a secondary control
compared with the source inventory and aggregation parameters. Thus, the strongest coarsening
cases are those that combine high dissolved Cr2+ , high aggregation efficiency, and high aggregation-
kernel strength, whereas the finest or least mixed distributions occur when source inventory and
aggregation strength are both low.

7.4.3. Particle deposition

The heterogeneous-aggregation deposition calculations were evaluated using the same framework
as the particle-size analysis. The one-at-a-time results identified the individual parameters that
most strongly controlled surface removal, while the deposition-enabled 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep identified
parameter combinations that produced the strongest and weakest deposition responses. In the one-
at-a-time deposition results, carrier viscosity 𝜇 emerged as one of the dominant controls on wall
removal. Lower viscosity reduced viscous resistance, increased particle mobility, and enhanced
transport to surfaces, leading to larger deposition rates and larger effective mass-transfer coefficients.
Higher viscosity had the opposite effect: particle motion was more strongly damped, near-wall
transport was reduced, and a larger fraction of the particulate inventory remained suspended.
Thus, decreasing the carrier viscosity favored deposition, whereas increasing the carrier viscosity
suppressed deposition.

 107

The wall-friction velocity 𝑢 ∗ directly controlled turbulent wall delivery. As the wall-friction
velocity increased, turbulent transport toward surfaces became stronger, which increased both
the deposited-mass rate 𝑀¤ dep and the effective mass-transfer coefficient 𝑘 𝑚 . As the wall-friction
velocity decreased, wall delivery weakened and suspended particles were retained for longer times.
Thus, increasing the wall-friction velocity favored deposition, whereas decreasing the wall-friction
velocity suppressed deposition.
The thermal-gradient parameter ∇𝑇 provided an additional directed wall-transfer pathway when
it was oriented toward the surface. When a wall-directed thermal gradient was active, particles
experienced stronger thermally driven transport to the wall. When the thermal gradient was zero,
this pathway was absent, and deposition was controlled by the remaining transport mechanisms.
Therefore, a wall-directed thermal gradient favored deposition, whereas the absence of a thermal
gradient suppressed deposition.
Carrier density 𝜌 modifies density contrast, particle inertia, and settling-related transport. When 𝜌
increases over the scanned range, the deposition response generally increases, although the effect
is more moderate than the viscosity and wall-friction-velocity effects. Density therefore acts as a
secondary but important deposition parameter. Temperature 𝑇 modifies the deposition response
indirectly through changes in carrier properties, reaction state, and particle-size evolution. As a
result, 𝑇 is best treated as a conditional modifier rather than as a primary independent wall-transfer
control.
Heterogeneous aggregation affects deposition indirectly by changing the particle-size distribution
presented to the wall. When aggregation transfers mass into larger mixed particles, those particles
can become more efficiently removed if they occupy size ranges that couple strongly to gravitational,
inertial, or turbulent transport. When aggregation is weak, the suspended inventory remains more
strongly weighted toward smaller or unmixed particles, and the deposition response can be reduced.
Thus, aggregation does not itself remove mass from the suspended phase, but it changes the size
and population state on which the deposition mechanisms act.
The one-at-a-time deposition-favorable parameter combination is

                            𝜇 ↓,      𝑢 ∗ ↑,    ∇𝑇 ,       𝜌 ↑,      𝑇↑ .

Under these conditions, particle mobility is high, turbulent wall delivery is strong, thermally driven
wall transfer is active, and density-dependent transport is enhanced. The one-at-a-time deposition-
suppressing parameter combination is

                          𝜇 ↑,      𝑢 ∗ ↓,     ∇𝑇 = 0,       𝜌 ↓,     𝑇↓ .

Under these conditions, viscous drag is larger, wall-directed turbulent transport is weaker, thermally
driven wall transfer is absent, and density-dependent enhancement is reduced.
The deposition-enabled 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep used the parameters 𝜇, 𝑢 ∗ , ∇𝑇, 𝜌, and 𝑇. The strongest
deposition cases occur when the favorable directions occur together. In particular, low carrier
viscosity, high wall-friction velocity, wall-directed thermal gradient, and higher carrier density
produce the largest wall-transfer tendency. The weakest deposition cases occur when high carrier
viscosity, low wall-friction velocity, zero thermal gradient, and lower carrier density occur together.

 108

Intermediate cases occur when favorable and unfavorable parameters oppose one another. For
example, low 𝜇 can increase particle mobility even if 𝑢 ∗ is low, while high 𝑢 ∗ can increase turbulent
wall transfer even when viscosity is less favorable. Similarly, wall-directed ∇𝑇 can increase
deposition in cases where the other transport parameters are only moderately favorable.
Table 7-6 summarizes the qualitative ranking of the scanned 𝑁 × 𝑁 parameters controlling
heterogeneous-aggregation particle deposition. The ranking follows the same format as the earlier
particle-size and deposition ranking tables: parameters are ordered by their influence on deposi-
tion, the deposition-favorable direction is identified, and the final column gives the primary physical
effect.

 Table 7-6. Ranking of scanned 𝑁 × 𝑁 parameters controlling heterogeneous-aggregation particle
 deposition.
 Rank Parameter Deposition-favorable direction Primary effect
  1 𝜇 Decrease Increases particle mobility.
  2 𝑢∗ Increase Increases delivery of suspended particles.
  3 ∇𝑇 Increase Thermally drives particles toward the wall.
  4 𝜌 Increase Changes particle inertia, and settling.
  5 𝑇 Conditional Modifies carrier properties.

The overall ranking from the deposition-enabled 𝑁 × 𝑁 sweep is therefore

                                        𝜇 > 𝑢 ∗ ≳ ∇𝑇 ≳ 𝜌 > 𝑇 .

In this ranking, low viscosity is the strongest deposition-favorable condition because it increases
particle mobility and reduces drag across several transport pathways. Wall-friction velocity and
wall-directed thermal gradients provide direct wall-transfer forcing and are therefore the next most
important deposition controls. Carrier density is a secondary but important modifier because it
changes density-dependent particle transport. Temperature has the weakest and most conditional
deposition effect because it acts primarily through coupled changes in carrier properties, particle-
size evolution, and reaction state.
The most deposition-favorable 𝑁 × 𝑁 combination is

                            𝜇 ↓,      𝑢 ∗ ↑,    ∇𝑇 ,       𝜌 ↑,      𝑇↑ .

Under these conditions, suspended particles are mobile, turbulent wall transfer is strong, thermally
driven transport contributes to wall removal, and density-dependent deposition is enhanced. The
most deposition-suppressing 𝑁 × 𝑁 combination is

                          𝜇 ↑,      𝑢 ∗ ↓,     ∇𝑇 = 0,       𝜌 ↓,     𝑇↓ .

Under these conditions, particle mobility is reduced, turbulent wall delivery is weak, thermally
driven wall transfer is absent, and density-dependent deposition is weaker.
Overall, the heterogeneous-aggregation deposition response is controlled by the competition be-
tween aggregate formation and wall-transfer efficiency. Aggregation determines which particle

 109

sizes and mixed populations are available for removal, while 𝜇, 𝑢 ∗ , ∇𝑇, 𝜌, and 𝑇 determine how ef-
ficiently those suspended particles are transferred to surfaces. Deposition is maximized when large
or mixed particles are produced under low-viscosity, high-wall-transfer conditions. Deposition is
minimized when particle growth is limited or when wall-transfer forcing is weak, especially under
high-viscosity, low-𝑢 ∗ , zero-gradient conditions.

7.5. Carrier Dynamics

7.5.1. Model Sweep Parameters

The carrier-dynamics study used the same base noble-metal, CrF2 , and heterogeneous configura-
tions described in the preceding sections, and therefore retained the same, population definitions,
breakup settings, deposition settings, dissolved-species structure, and solver controls already doc-
umented there. Relative to those earlier sweeps, the only parameters changed in the carrier study
were the carrier salt identity, the uniform system temperature, and the global aggregation-efficiency
multiplier 𝛼.

The varied carrier salts were BeF2 −LiF, BeF2 −LiF−UF4 , KCl−NaCl, and KCl−NaCl−UCl3 .
These salts were represented through temperature-dependent viscosity and density correlations
evaluated at the sweep temperatures, together with salt-specific constant thermal conductivities
taken fromt the Molten Salt Database - Thermophysical (MSD-TP)[59]. The temperature was
varied over 850, 923, and 1000 K, and the global aggregation-efficiency multiplier was varied over
𝛼 = {0.01, 0.5, 1.0}. These three parameter groups were combined in full Cartesian-product
noble-metal, CrF2 and hetero-aggregation carrier cases.

The temperature-dependent carrier correlations used in the sweep were implemented in simple
Arrhenius and linear forms. The dynamic viscosity was evaluated as

 110

The variation of 𝛼 in this carrier-focused study is directly tied to the earlier discussion of collision
efficiency and particle wetting. In the theoretical formulation, the full aggregation kernel is the
product of a collision-frequency term and a collision-efficiency term, and the latter represents the
probability that a hydrodynamic encounter leads to persistent attachment. In molten-salt systems,
that attachment probability depends not only on collision mechanics but also on interfacial chemistry
and the extent to which the carrier salt promotes or suppresses wetting-mediated sticking at contact.
The 𝛼 sweep was therefore used as a compact surrogate for effective carrier-dependent wetting
behavior. Smaller 𝛼 corresponds to weaker effective sticking and poorer attachment, consistent with
a salt environment that inhibits particle adhesion, while larger 𝛼 corresponds to stronger effective
sticking and more favorable wetting-mediated aggregate formation. In this sense, the carrier sweep
probes not only bulk salt-property effects through viscosity, density, and conductivity, but also the
uncertainty in how different carrier salts may alter the effective attachment efficiency of colliding
particles.

The carrier-dynamics calculations in Section 7.5 were not run on the same base loop used for the
preceding Chapter 7 sensitivity studies. For the Section 7.5 carrier-property comparisons, the base
problem was changed to a single recirculating control volume representing a 1.0 m-long square
duct with side length 0.127 m and therefore a much larger wall-area-to-volume ratio than the earlier
two-control-volume base cases. The recirculating face was driven by a nominal bulk velocity of
5.96 m/s.

This geometry and flow change was introduced so that the sectional-PBE carrier-salt calcula-
tions could be compared more directly with the mass-transfer analysis in SAND2026-12380[60].
That work evaluated deposition closures using MSRE/Kedl-type conditions with 𝑈 = 5.96 m/s,
characteristic length 𝐿 = 0.127 m, 𝜌𝑙 = 2245 kg/m3 , 𝜇𝑙 = 0.0078 Pa s, 𝑇 = 923 K, and
𝐷 = 5.1 × 10−9 m2 /s. Thus, Section 7.5 should be interpreted as a carrier-property compar-
ison study aligned with that earlier mass-transfer work, rather than as a direct continuation of
the Sections 7.2–7.4 loop baseline. The distinction is important because the wall-removal terms
are affected not only by salt chemistry, but also by the hydraulic scale and loop residence time.
Faster flow increases the Reynolds-number-dependent convective contribution to mass transfer, the
smaller characteristic dimension and larger 𝐴𝑤 /𝑉 increase the effective surface exchange per unit
liquid volume, and changing between fluoride and chloride salts changes the viscosity, density,
thermal conductivity, and diffusivity entering the turbulent-deposition, thermophoretic, settling,
and diffusion-related closures.

7.5.2. Noble Metals

The suspended noble-metal population remains broadly similar across the salt cases: the final
number distributions remain closely grouped, and the suspended mass remains concentrated toward
the largest resolved bins. Thus, the salt sweep does not indicate that changing from fluoride to
chloride carriers fundamentally changes the final noble-metal size regime. Instead, the carrier
properties mainly change the efficiency with which the existing Mo, Rh, and mixed Mo–Rh particle
population is transported to the wall.

 111

Increasing 𝛼 produces the clearest systematic shift in the suspended number distribution and
increases the rate at which noble-metal mass accumulates in the coarsest resolved bins. The
effect is not a wholesale restructuring of the distribution, but rather a modest coarsening of an
already coarse-biased suspended mass inventory. Temperature and salt identity also perturb the
final distributions, but their effect on particle size is weaker than the direct effect of 𝛼. This
is consistent with the Section 7.2 noble-metal results, where aggregation efficiency primarily
controlled coarsening, while deposition responded to both size and transport conditions.
The salt-family dependence is much clearer in the deposition metrics than in the size metrics.
The chloride salts produce larger final noble-metal deposition rates than the fluoride salts. This
occurs because the chloride property set is less viscous over the sweep range, which reduces
hydrodynamic resistance to particle motion and increases the effectiveness of Brownian, turbulent,
thermophoretic, and settling-related transport. The fluoride salts are more viscous and therefore
damp particle mobility and near-wall transport. As a result, the fluoride cases occupy the lower-
deposition portion of the sweep, while the chloride cases occupy the higher-deposition portion.
Temperature reinforces the same trend. Increasing temperature lowers the carrier viscosity and
therefore increases the mobility of the suspended noble-metal particles. The higher-temperature
cases consequently show larger wall-removal rates within each salt family. The density variation
with temperature is comparatively weaker, but it still affects the particle–fluid density contrast and
the kinematic response of the carrier. Because Mo and Rh are much denser than the carrier salts,
the density effect does not dominate the size evolution, but it remains important for gravitational
settling and for the property combinations that appear in the turbulent and thermophoretic deposition
closures.
Uranium addition changes the two salt families in different ways. In the fluoride cases, adding UF4
increases both the carrier density and viscosity relative to the binary BeF2 –LiF salt. The viscosity
increase suppresses particle mobility and reduces the tendency for rapid wall transport, while the
density increase modifies the particle–fluid density contrast. In the chloride cases, adding UCl3
primarily changes the carrier density in the property set used for this sweep, while the viscosity
behavior remains much closer to the binary KCl–NaCl case. Therefore, uranium addition should
not be interpreted as producing the same transport perturbation in both salt families. In the fluoride
carrier, uranium changes both viscous drag and density contrast; in the chloride carrier, the main
effect is through density and kinematic-property changes.
Mechanism-resolved deposition confirms that the total noble-metal wall loss is controlled primarily
by the transport environment rather than by large changes in the final suspended size spectrum.
Turbulence-driven deposition is the dominant wall-removal pathway in the salt sweep. Gravity
provides the next most important contribution, especially when aggregation has produced larger
particles that settle more efficiently. Thermophoresis is smaller than the turbulent contribution but
remains non-negligible under the imposed wall-directed thermal gradient. The overall deposition
ordering therefore follows the turbulent-transport ordering: hot chloride salts give the largest
wall-transfer rates, while cooler and more viscous fluoride salts give the smallest rates.
Overall, the noble-metal salt sweep indicates that carrier selection primarily controls wall-removal
efficiency, while wetting behavior primarily controls coarsening. Fluoride salts, especially the more
viscous uranium-bearing fluoride case, suppress transport and yield lower deposition rates. Chloride

 112

salts provide a lower-viscosity transport environment and yield larger turbulent, gravitational, and
thermophoretic wall losses. Increasing temperature strengthens these effects by further reducing
viscosity. Increasing 𝛼 promotes larger noble-metal aggregates, which can enhance size-dependent
deposition, but it does not reorder the sweep as strongly as the fluoride–chloride property difference.
The dominant conclusion is therefore that noble-metal deposition in the carrier-salt sweep is
controlled first by salt-dependent transport properties, second by temperature, and then by wetting-
controlled aggregation through 𝛼.

7.5.3. Chromium Fluoride

The chromium-fluoride salt sweep differs from the noble-metal sweep because the CrF2 population
is coupled to dissolved chromium through nucleation, growth, and dissolution. As a result, the
carrier salt affects the calculation in two ways. First, the carrier viscosity and density change the
transport of already-formed particles to the wall. Second, temperature can change the balance
between dissolved and particulate chromium by shifting the relative importance of nucleation,
growth, and dissolution. The final CrF2 size distribution therefore reflects both particle transport
and the reaction-activity model, rather than aggregation and deposition alone.
The salt-family effect is still visible in the transport response. The fluoride carriers are more
viscous than the chloride carriers, and this larger viscosity tends to damp particle mobility, reduce
settling velocities, and weaken turbulence-assisted wall transport. The chloride carriers provide
a lower-viscosity environment, so CrF2 particles experience less hydrodynamic resistance and are
transported more efficiently once they are present in the suspended phase. This produces larger
deposition tendencies in the chloride cases, especially when the particle population has shifted
toward sizes for which gravitational and turbulent deposition are effective. The fluoride cases,
by contrast, tend to retain particles in suspension more effectively because the higher viscosity
suppresses several wall-transport mechanisms.
Temperature has a more complicated effect for CrF2 than for the noble metals. For noble metals,
temperature primarily changes transport properties. For chromium fluoride, temperature also
changes the precipitation and dissolution history. Increasing temperature lowers the carrier viscosity
and can therefore increase particle mobility and deposition, but it can also shift the nucleation-
growth-dissolution balance. If the hotter case favors growth of existing particles, the suspended
population becomes coarser and the gravitational contribution to deposition increases. If the hotter
case favors dissolution or sustained fine-particle production, the number distribution can remain
finer and the gravitational contribution can decrease even though the carrier is less viscous. Thus,
the temperature sweep does not produce a single universal particle-size trend; its effect depends on
whether the final state is growth-dominated, dissolution-dominated, or nucleation-dominated.
The uranium-bearing salts modify the CrF2 trends through the same property pathways identified
in the carrier-property model, but the effect is not identical for fluorides and chlorides. In the
fluoride family, adding UF4 increases both density and viscosity relative to the binary BeF2 –LiF
carrier. The viscosity increase suppresses particle diffusion, settling, and turbulence-assisted wall
approach, while the density increase changes the particle–fluid density contrast. These effects tend
to reduce transport-driven wall removal relative to a less viscous fluoride carrier. In the chloride

 113

family, adding UCl3 primarily increases the carrier density in the property set used here, while the
viscosity behavior remains closer to the binary KCl–NaCl case. Uranium addition in the chloride
cases therefore mainly changes density contrast and kinematic response, whereas uranium addition
in the fluoride cases changes both density contrast and viscous drag.
The role of 𝛼 is also different for CrF2 than for noble metals. In the noble-metal cases, 𝛼
directly controls the coarsening of an insoluble particulate inventory. For CrF2 , 𝛼 still controls
the probability that particle–particle encounters become permanent aggregates, but the available
particle population is also being created and removed by nucleation, growth, and dissolution.
Therefore, increasing 𝛼 generally promotes coarsening, reduces the persistence of fine particles,
and increases the mass fraction in larger bins, but the magnitude of this effect depends on how
much particulate chromium exists at the time aggregation occurs. If the system remains strongly
nucleation-dominated, a high 𝛼 case can still retain a substantial fine-particle source. If the system
is growth-dominated, the same increase in 𝛼 more clearly shifts the population toward larger
aggregates.
The mechanism-resolved deposition behavior shows that CrF2 wall loss is controlled by both
the evolving size distribution and the carrier-transport environment. Gravity is the largest positive
deposition contribution for the chromium-fluoride sweep, with turbulence providing the next-largest
positive contribution. Thermophoresis is smaller than gravity and turbulence but remains positive
under the imposed cold-wall temperature-gradient convention. Diffusiophoresis remains negligible
for most of the sweep. This ordering differs from the noble-metal subsection, where turbulence was
the leading wall-removal pathway. The difference arises because the CrF2 population can grow into
a size range where settling becomes the dominant removal mechanism, while the noble-metal cases
remain more directly controlled by turbulent transport of an insoluble particulate inventory.
Overall, the chromium-fluoride salt sweep shows that carrier properties control wall transport, while
temperature and 𝛼 control both transport and the particle population presented to the wall. Chloride
salts tend to increase deposition because their lower viscosity permits stronger particle motion and
wall approach. Fluoride salts, especially the uranium-bearing fluoride, suppress deposition through
higher viscosity and stronger drag. Increasing temperature often increases mobility, but its net effect
on particle size is regime dependent because it also modifies the dissolved–particulate chromium
balance. Increasing 𝛼 promotes agglomeration under poorly wetted or non-wetting conditions, but
its effect is filtered through the ongoing nucleation-growth-dissolution dynamics. The dominant
conclusion is therefore that CrF2 deposition cannot be interpreted from carrier properties alone:
the salt controls transport, while the chromium chemistry controls how much particulate material
exists and what size distribution is available for deposition.

7.5.4. Heterogeneous Particles

The heterogeneous-particle salt sweep combines the noble-metal and chromium-fluoride behaviors
discussed in the previous two subsections. In these cases, the particulate system contains Mo,
Rh, mixed Mo–Rh aggregates, CrF2 , and mixed noble-metal–CrF2 populations. The carrier salt
therefore affects not only the transport of an already-existing particle population, but also the
extent to which different particle classes encounter one another and form mixed aggregates. As

 114

in Section 7.4, the important feature of the heterogeneous calculation is that the noble-metal and
chromium-fluoride populations are no longer isolated. Carrier properties can change the collision
environment, the persistence of fine particles, the rate of mixed-particle formation, and the size
distribution available for wall transport.

The suspended-mass response is relatively compact across the salt sweep. Changing between
fluoride and chloride salts does not produce a large change in the total particulate mass remaining
in suspension at the end of the calculation. This indicates that the salt-property variations primarily
redistribute the suspended inventory among particle classes and size bins, rather than strongly
creating or removing total particulate mass over the simulated interval. The more important
changes occur in the population structure: how much mass remains in single-component Mo, Rh,
or CrF2 particles, how much is transferred into mixed aggregates, and how efficiently the larger
mixed particles are transported toward the wall.

The fluoride–chloride distinction is clearest in the transport and deposition behavior. The fluoride
carriers are more viscous, which damps Brownian mobility, reduces relative-motion collision rates,
and suppresses settling and turbulence-assisted wall approach. These effects tend to preserve
larger numbers of smaller particles and reduce deposition efficiency. The chloride carriers are less
viscous, so particles experience less drag and move more readily through the carrier. This favors
both aggregation and deposition, especially once mixed particles have grown into sizes where
gravitational and turbulent deposition are more effective. Therefore, the chloride cases generally
represent the higher-transport and higher-deposition portion of the heterogeneous sweep, while the
fluoride cases represent the more transport-limited portion.

Temperature acts in the same direction as the fluoride–chloride viscosity contrast. Increasing
temperature reduces the carrier viscosity, which increases particle mobility and strengthens both
particle–particle encounters and particle–wall transport. In the heterogeneous cases, however, the
temperature response is more coupled than in the noble-metal-only sweep. A lower-viscosity,
higher-temperature carrier can increase the frequency of encounters among Mo, Rh, CrF2 , and
mixed particles, which promotes redistribution of mass into larger heterogeneous aggregates.
Those larger aggregates can then be removed more efficiently by size-sensitive deposition mecha-
nisms. Thus, temperature affects deposition both directly, through the carrier transport properties,
and indirectly, through the particle-size distribution produced by aggregation and heterogeneous
mixing.

Uranium addition again affects the fluoride and chloride families differently. In the fluoride family,
adding UF4 increases both carrier density and viscosity relative to the binary BeF2 –LiF salt.
The viscosity increase suppresses diffusional motion, collision transport, settling, and turbulence-
assisted deposition, while the density increase changes the particle–fluid density contrast. In the
chloride family, adding UCl3 primarily changes the carrier density in the property set used for
the sweep, while the viscosity remains much closer to the binary KCl–NaCl case. Consequently,
uranium addition in the fluoride cases changes both viscous drag and density contrast, whereas
uranium addition in the chloride cases mainly changes density contrast and kinematic response.
The uranium-bearing salts therefore cannot be interpreted as a single uniform perturbation; their
effect depends on the base salt family.

 115

The role of 𝛼 is especially important in the heterogeneous-particle sweep because it controls not only
like-particle aggregation, but also the formation of mixed aggregates. Small values of 𝛼 represent
well-wetted or strongly solvated particle surfaces, where the molten-salt film between approaching
particles remains stable enough that many collisions do not produce permanent attachment. Under
these conditions, Mo, Rh, and CrF2 particles are more likely to remain as separate populations, and
the final number of discrete particles remains higher. Large values of 𝛼 represent poorly wetted or
effectively non-wetting surfaces, where the intervening salt layer drains or ruptures more readily.
Under those conditions, collisions are more likely to produce persistent contacts, mixed aggregates
form more readily, and the population shifts toward fewer but larger particles.

This wetting interpretation follows the Chapter 2 discussion of solvent-mediated interaction barriers.
In a well-wetted system, the salt stabilizes particle surfaces and inhibits direct particle–particle
contact. In a poorly wetted or non-wetting system, the salt does not provide the same stabilizing
barrier, so agglomeration is more efficient. For the heterogeneous cases, this distinction is broader
than simple coarsening. Increasing 𝛼 also increases the degree of coupling between the noble-metal
and chromium-fluoride populations. A high-𝛼 case can convert separate Mo, Rh, and CrF2 particles
into mixed aggregates more efficiently, which changes the effective density, size, and composition
of the depositing particulate inventory.

The deposition trends should therefore be interpreted as the combined result of salt-controlled
transport and aggregation-controlled particle restructuring. Low-viscosity chloride carriers favor
larger deposited masses because they reduce drag and allow larger aggregates to reach the wall more
efficiently. High-viscosity fluoride carriers suppress that transport and tend to reduce deposition.
Increasing 𝛼 generally increases deposition by producing larger aggregates, but its effect is indirect:
𝛼 first changes the particle population, and the deposition closures then act on that changed
population. The largest deposition responses occur when both effects act together, namely in low-
viscosity salts with high aggregation efficiency. The smallest deposition responses occur when
both effects oppose deposition, namely in more viscous salts with low aggregation efficiency.

The mechanism-level behavior is consistent with this interpretation. Turbulent transport and gravi-
tational settling are the dominant positive wall-removal pathways for the depositing heterogeneous
inventory. Turbulent deposition responds strongly to the carrier flow and viscosity, while gravita-
tional settling responds strongly to particle size and particle–fluid density contrast. Thermophoresis
remains a secondary contribution under the imposed wall-directed temperature gradient. The rel-
ative importance of gravity increases when aggregation shifts mass into larger mixed particles,
whereas the relative importance of turbulent transport increases in the lower-viscosity chloride
carriers.

Overall, the heterogeneous-particle salt sweep shows that carrier properties, temperature, and
wetting behavior act together. The salt family controls the transport environment, with chloride
salts producing more mobile and more depositable particles than the more viscous fluoride salts.
Temperature strengthens transport by lowering viscosity and increasing particle mobility. Uranium
addition modifies the trends according to whether it changes viscosity, density, or both. The wetting
parameter 𝛼 controls the degree of heterogeneous aggregation, with low-𝛼 wetted systems preserv-
ing more separate particles and high-𝛼 non-wetting systems producing larger mixed aggregates.
The dominant conclusion is that heterogeneous deposition is not controlled by carrier properties

 116

alone: it is controlled by the interaction between salt-dependent transport and wetting-controlled
mixed-particle formation.
latex

7.5.5. Comparison between Noble Metals, Chromium Fluoride, and Heterogeneous
 Particles

The carrier-salt sweep provides a direct comparison of how noble-metal, CrF2 , and heterogeneous
particle populations respond to changes in the surrounding liquid carrier. Sections 7.5.2 to 7.5.4
show that the same carrier perturbation does not act identically on each particle class. For noble
metals, the carrier mainly changes the transport and wall-transfer efficiency of an already-existing
insoluble particle population. For CrF2 , the carrier affects both particle transport and the dissolved–
particulate chemical balance. For heterogeneous particles, the carrier influences transport while
also changing the rate at which distinct particle classes encounter one another and form mixed
aggregates.
The comparison of characteristic particle radii in Fig. 7-7a shows that the carrier does not impose
one common size response on all material classes. The noble-metal cases remain comparatively
grouped because the sweep transports an insoluble Mo–Rh population whose size distribution is
already established by aggregation, breakup, and wall exchange. In contrast, the CrF2 cases can
show stronger changes in characteristic size because the particulate inventory is coupled to dissolved
chromium through nucleation, growth, and dissolution. The heterogeneous cases combine both
effects: carrier properties influence the mobility of existing particles, while aggregation between
Mo, Rh, CrF2 , and mixed classes changes the resulting size distribution.
Figure 7-7b shows the corresponding effective wall-transfer coefficients. Compared with the radius
comparison in Fig. 7-7a, the wall-transfer coefficient more directly reflects the carrier-dependent
transport environment. Lower-viscosity chloride salts generally produce a more mobile suspension
and therefore favor larger effective transfer coefficients, while higher-viscosity fluoride salts damp
Brownian, turbulent, thermophoretic, and settling-related wall approach. This distinction is most
direct for noble metals, where the particles are insoluble and the carrier mainly controls how
efficiently suspended material is delivered to the wall. For CrF2 and heterogeneous particles,
the same transport effect remains present, but it is coupled to chemistry and mixed-aggregation
pathways.
The viscosity dependence in Fig. 7-8a helps separate transport-driven effects from chemistry-driven
effects. Increasing viscosity increases hydrodynamic resistance and reduces particle mobility. For
noble metals, this mainly limits particle–particle encounters and particle–wall transport, so the size
response is generally weaker than the deposition response. For CrF2 , however, viscosity is only one
part of the response because the particle population can be created or removed through dissolved-
species exchange. For heterogeneous particles, viscosity affects both the collision frequency among
different particle classes and the subsequent ability of the resulting aggregates to reach the wall.
The more direct relationship between viscosity and wall transfer is shown in Fig. 7-8b. The
effective coefficient 𝑘 eff decreases when viscous damping suppresses diffusion, turbulent transport,

and settling-related motion toward surfaces. This trend explains why the chloride carriers tend
to occupy the higher-transport portion of the sweep, while the fluoride carriers tend to be more
transport limited. The result is consistent with the deposition model because the surface sink scales
with the deposition velocity, and the deposition velocity depends on carrier properties through
Brownian diffusion, turbulent transport, thermophoresis, and gravitational settling.
Comparison between the effective wall-transfer coefficient and characteristic particle radius in
Fig. 7-9a emphasizes that carrier effects and particle-size effects are coupled. Small particles
are influenced more strongly by Brownian and near-wall diffusive transport, while larger particles
become more sensitive to settling, turbulent deposition, and inertial transport. Therefore, changes
in carrier salt can alter deposition directly through transport properties and indirectly by shifting
the particle population into a different size range. This distinction is especially important for CrF2
and heterogeneous particles, where chemistry or mixed aggregation can change the characteristic
particle size before deposition occurs.
Figure 7-9b presents the same wall-transfer behavior in terms of average particle mass rather
than radius. This comparison is useful because the sectional PBE advances mass inventories, and
because deposition removes mass rather than number alone. Cases with larger average particle mass
can produce stronger mass deposition even if the number of particles is not large. For noble metals,
this reflects aggregation among insoluble Mo and Rh particles. For CrF2 , it reflects the balance
between dissolved chromium precipitation, growth, and dissolution. For heterogeneous particles,
it reflects the formation of mixed aggregates that combine noble-metal and chromium-fluoride
material into heavier particles with different wall-transfer behavior.
For the noble-metal class, the combined interpretation of Figs. 7-7 to 7-9 is that the carrier salt
mainly changes deposition efficiency rather than fundamentally changing the final particle-size
regime. The noble-metal population is insoluble, so the model does not need to first create the
particle phase from dissolved precursor. As a result, the fluoride–chloride contrast is expressed
most clearly through 𝑘 eff . Lower-viscosity chloride salts increase mobility and enhance wall
transfer, while higher-viscosity fluoride salts suppress wall approach. Aggregation can still shift
mass toward larger particles, but it does not change the governing interpretation as strongly as the
carrier-property contrast.
Carrier effects are more strongly coupled in the CrF2 class. The same viscosity and transport
arguments apply once particles exist, but the number and size of CrF2 particles also depend on
nucleation, growth, and dissolution. Therefore, CrF2 deposition cannot be interpreted from carrier
viscosity alone. A carrier that increases particle mobility may also correspond to a temperature
and chemistry state that changes the amount of particulate chromium available for deposition. This
produces a combined chemistry–transport response rather than a purely hydrodynamic response.
Figure 7-10 summarizes the effect of temperature across the carrier-salt calculations. Tempera-
ture primarily influences transport by changing carrier properties, especially dynamic viscosity.
Increasing temperature generally lowers viscosity, which increases particle mobility, strengthens
particle–particle encounter rates, and enhances particle–wall transport. For noble metals, this
mainly increases deposition of an existing insoluble inventory. For CrF2 , the temperature response
must also be interpreted through the dissolved–particulate equilibrium because temperature can
change the relative balance of nucleation, growth, and dissolution. For heterogeneous particles,
