 Figure 7-11. Aggregation-efficiency sensitivity of the carrier-salt sweep.

 122

The role of the aggregation-efficiency multiplier 𝛼 is isolated in Fig. 7-11. Increasing 𝛼 increases
the probability that particle encounters lead to permanent attachment, which shifts suspended
material toward larger aggregates. The effect is modest but important for noble metals because it
changes the size distribution of an insoluble particle inventory. It is more complicated for CrF2 ,
where aggregation competes with nucleation, growth, and dissolution. It is most important for the
heterogeneous cases because 𝛼 controls not only like-particle aggregation, but also cross-population
aggregation between Mo, Rh, CrF2 , and mixed-particle classes. Large values of 𝛼 therefore promote
mixed aggregates that are heavier and more susceptible to gravitational and turbulent deposition,
while small values of 𝛼 preserve more separate fine-particle populations.
The uranium-bearing salts should be interpreted in terms of how uranium addition changes the
underlying carrier properties. In the fluoride family, adding UF4 changes both viscosity and
density, thereby modifying viscous drag, particle–fluid density contrast, and kinematic transport
behavior. In the chloride family, adding UCl3 primarily changes density while leaving the viscosity
closer to the binary chloride carrier. Thus, uranium addition is not a uniform perturbation across
all salts; its effect depends on whether it changes viscosity, density, or both.
Overall, the comparison shows that carrier identity affects all three particle classes, but through
different pathways. Noble-metal deposition is controlled primarily by carrier-dependent transport
of an insoluble particle inventory. CrF2 deposition is controlled by both carrier transport and
dissolved–particulate chemistry. Heterogeneous-particle deposition is controlled by the interac-
tion between carrier-dependent transport and wetting-controlled mixed aggregation. The largest
deposition responses occur when low-viscosity carrier properties, elevated temperature, and strong
aggregation act together to produce mobile, coarse, and efficiently deposited particles. The smallest
responses occur when high viscosity and weak aggregation preserve smaller suspended particles
and suppress wall transfer.

7.5.6. Comparison with the Previous Mass-Transfer Study

The salt-sweep calculations in Sections 7.5.2–7.5.4 were intended to connect the sectional-PBE re-
sults with the earlier mass-transfer analysis in SAND2026–12380[60]. That report implemented and
evaluated deposition closures for turbulent deposition, thermophoresis, and gravitational settling
under MSRE/Kedl-type conditions. A central result of that work was that noble-metal deposition
is strongly dependent on particle size and carrier properties. In particular, the mass-transfer coeffi-
cients changed with salt viscosity, density, flow velocity, temperature, and particle diffusivity, with
viscosity and particle size producing especially strong sensitivities. The previous study therefore
showed that deposition cannot be treated as a single salt-independent removal rate; it depends on
both the hydraulic environment and the size of the particulate material being transported.
The present salt-sweep results agree with that main conclusion. Across the noble-metal, chromium-
fluoride, and heterogeneous-particle cases, the lower-viscosity chloride salts generally produced
stronger wall transport than the higher-viscosity fluoride salts. The hotter cases also tended to
increase deposition because increasing temperature reduced the carrier viscosity and increased
particle mobility. These trends are consistent with SAND2026–12380[60], which showed that the
turbulent and particle-transport closures respond directly to carrier viscosity, temperature, and flow

 123

conditions. The present results also support the earlier conclusion that density is important but
usually secondary to viscosity. Changes in density modify the particle–fluid density contrast and
the kinematic response of the carrier, but the strongest carrier-property changes in the sweep occur
when the viscosity changes.
The key difference between the two studies is how particle size is treated. In SAND2026–12380[60],
particle size was an input to the closure evaluation. The previous report could therefore show
directly that smaller particles and larger particles are transported differently, and that gravitational
deposition becomes increasingly important as particle size increases. In the present sectional-PBE
calculations, particle size is not imposed as a fixed parameter. Instead, the size distribution evolves
through aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth, dissolution, heterogeneous aggregation, and
deposition. Thus, the present work agrees that deposition is particle-size dependent, but it extends
the earlier result by showing that the relevant particle size must be predicted dynamically.
The noble-metal sweep provides the most direct comparison with SAND2026–12380[60] because
both analyses focus on inert noble-metal particles. The agreement is strongest for the carrier-
property trends. Hot, low-viscosity salts produced the largest noble-metal deposition rates, while
cooler and more viscous salts produced lower deposition rates. The noble-metal results also agree
with the previous report that turbulent wall transport is the dominant removal pathway under the
high-flow conditions used here. The main addition from the sectional-PBE model is the role
of the wetting or sticking parameter, 𝛼. In the previous report, changing particle size was an
imposed sensitivity. In the present model, increasing 𝛼 produces larger aggregates by increasing
the probability that particle–particle encounters become persistent contacts. The effect of 𝛼 on
deposition is therefore indirect: it changes the particle population, and the deposition closures then
act on that changed population.
The mechanism-level comparison is also consistent with the previous report. SAND2026–
12380[60] found that turbulent deposition is important under high-flow conditions and that gravita-
tional deposition becomes increasingly important for larger particles. The Section 7.5 noble-metal
results follow this same ordering: turbulent deposition is the leading pathway, while gravity is a
secondary but size-dependent contribution. The CrF2 and heterogeneous cases can show a larger
gravitational contribution because their evolving populations place more mass into larger particle
bins. This should not be interpreted as disagreement with SAND2026–12380[60]. Rather, it is
the expected result of applying the same size-dependent transport closures to a particle population
whose size distribution evolves during the calculation.
The uranium-bearing salts provide an additional check on the carrier-property interpretation. In
the fluoride family, adding UF4 increases both density and viscosity. The increased viscosity
suppresses particle mobility and tends to reduce deposition, while the density increase changes the
particle–fluid density contrast. In the chloride family, adding UCl3 primarily changes the density in
the property set used for this sweep, while the viscosity remains closer to the binary chloride case.
The deposition response is therefore stronger when uranium addition changes viscosity than when it
mainly changes density. This behavior agrees with the sensitivity trends in SAND2026–12380[60],
where viscosity was a stronger driver of the evaluated mass-transfer coefficients than density.
Overall, the present Section 7.5 results agree with the major conclusions of SAND2026–12380[60].
Both studies show that solid deposition is strongly dependent on particle size, salt viscosity,

 124

temperature, and flow-driven transport. The present work does not contradict the earlier mass-
transfer study; it generalizes it. SAND2026–12380[60] showed how deposition closures respond
when particle and carrier properties are specified. The present sectional-PBE calculations show
how those same dependencies appear when the particle population evolves dynamically. The added
conclusion is that reliable deposition prediction requires both accurate carrier-property data and
an evolving particle-size distribution. Carrier properties determine how efficiently particles are
transported to the wall, while aggregation, wetting behavior, precipitation, growth, dissolution, and
heterogeneous mixing determine what particles are available for that transport.

7.6. Conclusion

The Chapter 7 calculations demonstrated that the sectional PBE implementation provides a con-
sistent framework for evaluating particle-size evolution, dissolved–particulate exchange, heteroge-
neous aggregation, and wall deposition under coupled liquid-phase conditions. The noble-metal
cases showed that insoluble particle inventories can be strongly redistributed by aggregation,
breakup, turbulent transport, and deposition even in the absence of dissolved-species chemistry.
The CrF2 cases showed that soluble particle populations are controlled not only by transport, but
also by the thermodynamic and kinetic coupling among dissolved chromium, nucleation, growth,
dissolution, and deposition. The heterogeneous aggregation cases combined these effects and
demonstrated that mixed particle formation can change both the evolving size distribution and the
surface-removal response by coupling noble-metal and precipitated-salt inventories. Finally, the
carrier-dynamics studies showed that changes in viscosity, density, temperature, flow rate, wall-
friction velocity, and thermal-gradient forcing do not act as isolated correction factors; instead,
they alter the relative importance of Brownian motion, settling, turbulent delivery, aggregation,
breakup, precipitation, and wall exchange. Taken together, these results show that molten-salt par-
ticle behavior is best treated as a coupled inventory-evolution problem. The model preserved the
intended mass-transfer pathways while identifying the parameters and mechanism combinations
most responsible for suspended mass retention, particle growth, mixed-population formation, and
deposited inventory accumulation.

 125

 126

8. DISCUSSION, EXPERIMENTAL VALIDATION NEEDS, AND
 FUTURE WORK

Chapter 8 interprets the present sectional PBE framework in terms of its technical significance,
validation requirements, and remaining development needs. The discussion first examines why
particle-size evolution, dissolved–particulate exchange, heterogeneous aggregation, carrier-salt
properties, and wall exchange must be treated as coupled inventory processes rather than as inde-
pendent correction factors. It then identifies the experimental data needed to convert the verified
implementation into a predictive model, including measurements of carrier properties, aggregation
and breakup behavior, precipitation and dissolution kinetics, deposition and resuspension rates,
and mixed-particle formation. The chapter concludes by outlining extensions needed for reac-
tor source-term applications, with emphasis on radionuclide decay and transmutation, liquid–gas
transfer, volatile-species release, and solid–gas aerosol dynamics [2, 3, 1, 9, 61].

8.1. Discussion of the Models Presented in this Report and the Results

The sectional population balance equation (PBE) framework developed in this report is important
because it provides a mechanistic bridge between microscale particle processes and system-scale
reactor inventories. In molten salt reactors, particle behavior is not only a materials issue or a
chemistry issue; it directly affects radionuclide transport, source-term retention, corrosion-product
redistribution, heat-transfer-surface fouling, and the location of radioactive material within the
primary system. A model that only tracks a bulk concentration cannot distinguish between fine
particles that remain suspended, larger agglomerates that deposit efficiently, dissolved species that
can reprecipitate elsewhere, and surface-bound inventories that may later resuspend. The sectional
formulation addresses this limitation by tracking how material is distributed over particle size,
composition, dissolved inventory, and deposited inventory [2, 3, 57, 4].
The mass-based sectional discretization in Chapter 3 is important for nuclear reactor model-
ing because conservation of material inventories is a primary requirement for source-term and
activation-product calculations. The model advances sectional masses rather than only number
densities, while reconstructing number concentration when needed for number-based kinetics such
as aggregation. This capability is essential for molten salt systems because particles are rarely
guaranteed to remain chemically pure. Noble metals, corrosion products, precipitated fluorides,
oxides, and mixed aggregates may coexist and interact. A multicomponent sectional representation
allows the model to retain composition information while still predicting size-dependent transport,
deposition, and resuspension [57, 58, 3, 4].
Verification work in Chapter 6 is important because the model contains many coupled mechanisms
that could otherwise produce nonphysical inventory changes. Aggregation should reduce particle

 127

number but conserve mass. Breakup should increase particle number or redistribute size while
conserving mass. Growth and dissolution should transfer material between dissolved and particulate
inventories with correct sign and stoichiometry. Deposition and resuspension should exchange
material between suspended and surface-bound states. The verification tests demonstrated these
properties for the implemented residual. This gives confidence that the trends observed in Chapter 7
arise from the selected closures and parameter choices rather than from numerical inventory errors.
Conservation of mass, volume, or selected moments is a central requirement of sectional and
fixed-pivot PBE methods [40, 57, 58, 2].
The results in Chapter 7 show why this type of model is needed. The noble-metal calculations
demonstrated that an insoluble particle inventory can be redistributed substantially by aggregation
and breakup even when no dissolved-species chemistry is active. The most influential parameters
were not arbitrary numerical factors; they corresponded to mechanisms that control whether particle
collisions occur and whether those collisions lead to persistent attachment. The global aggregation-
efficiency multiplier 𝛼 changes the probability of attachment after encounter, while the aggregation-
kernel multiplier 𝛽 changes the rate at which encounters occur. Their influence on the noble-metal
size distribution is important for reactor analysis because noble-metal fission products and poorly
soluble species can act as mobile or depositing carriers of radioactivity. Whether they remain as fine
suspended particles or grow into larger depositing agglomerates changes the predicted distribution
of radioactive material throughout the loop [5, 6, 7, 61].
Noble-metal deposition sweeps further showed that particle growth alone is not sufficient to deter-
mine surface accumulation. The deposition response depends on the coupled competition among
aggregation, breakup, carrier viscosity, turbulent dissipation, and wall-transfer mechanisms. This
is significant because reactor components do not experience a single isolated perturbation. Tem-
perature, viscosity, turbulence, surface area, salt composition, and particle population all vary
throughout a primary system. The Cartesian-product sweeps therefore provide more reactor-
relevant information than one-at-a-time perturbations because they expose combined regimes,
such as strong aggregation with high viscous damping, or strong turbulent mixing with enhanced
breakup. These regimes determine whether material is transported as a mobile suspension, retained
on walls, or redistributed by resuspension [1, 9, 7, 37].
CrF2 calculations demonstrated a different but equally important point: particulate inventories
in molten salts can be controlled by chemistry before transport becomes dominant. Dissolved
chromium can remain in solution, precipitate into CrF2 , grow existing particles, dissolve back into
the salt, or deposit after entering the particulate phase. Thus, the relevant reactor question is not
only how particles move, but when and where particles are created. The reaction-activity treatment,
solubility-product parameters, activity coefficients, growth and dissolution kinetics, and molecular
diffusivity determine the amount of chromium-bearing material available to the particle popula-
tion. This matters for reactor materials performance because chromium transport is connected to
corrosion, redox state, precipitation, and deposition on surfaces. A model that separates dissolved
chromium from particulate chromium can therefore distinguish chemical retention in the salt from
particulate accumulation on structural surfaces [11, 12, 41, 50].
Heterogeneous-particle calculations represent a more realistic reactor condition than either the
noble-metal or CrF2 cases alone. In a reactor system, insoluble fission products, corrosion products,
precipitated salts, and mixed aggregates can coexist. The heterogeneous model allows these

 128

populations to interact through cross-aggregation while retaining componentwise mass. The results
showed that mixed-particle formation can alter both the particle-size distribution and the deposition
response. This is significant for source-term modeling because a radionuclide that would otherwise
remain mobile as a fine particle may be incorporated into a larger mixed aggregate, while a corrosion
product may provide a surface for heterogeneous nucleation or attachment. The transport behavior
of a radionuclide-bearing particle can therefore depend on other material classes present in the salt
[3, 4, 48, 7].
Carrier-salt comparisons in Chapter 7 further emphasize that salt identity affects particle behavior
through coupled transport and chemistry pathways. Consequently, a change in carrier salt cannot
be interpreted as a single change in “deposition tendency.” Lower viscosity can increase Brownian
mobility, turbulent encounter rates, and settling mobility, while density changes can alter both
the particle–fluid density contrast and the kinematic viscosity. The Chapter 7 carrier results are
therefore important because they show that the same carrier perturbation affects noble metals, CrF2 ,
and heterogeneous particles through different dominant pathways [1, 13, 9, 62].
For noble metals, the carrier mainly changes the transport and wall-transfer efficiency of an existing
insoluble inventory. For CrF2 , the carrier also interacts with dissolved–particulate chemistry and
can change the amount of particulate material available for deposition. For heterogeneous particles,
the carrier affects both mobility and the rate at which separate material classes collide and form
mixed aggregates. This distinction is central for molten salt reactor analysis. It means that a
carrier-property correlation calibrated for one material class cannot automatically be applied to
another without considering chemistry, size distribution, and aggregation state [2, 3, 41, 4].
The broader significance of the results is that particle transport in molten salt reactors should be
treated as a coupled inventory-evolution problem rather than as a prescribed deposition coefficient.
The location and form of radioactive material depend on interactions among salt chemistry, particle
physics, carrier transport, wall exchange, and system geometry. The model developed here provides
a framework for resolving those interactions at a level that is still compatible with reduced-order
reactor-loop calculations. Its present value is strongest as a mechanistic and sensitivity-analysis
tool: it identifies which processes control suspended mass, particle growth, and deposition. Its
future predictive value will depend on experimental validation of the closure parameters that control
aggregation, breakup, precipitation, dissolution, deposition, and resuspension [2, 3, 10, 61].

8.2. Experimental Verification and Validation Needed for the Present Models

The numerical verification in Chapter 6 establishes that the implemented equations preserve the
intended conservation properties and limiting behavior. Experimental validation must address a
different question: whether the selected closures and parameter values reproduce physical molten-
salt behavior. This validation cannot be performed with a single integral experiment because the
model contains several coupled mechanisms that can compensate for one another. A measured
decrease in suspended mass, for example, could result from deposition, aggregation into poorly
sampled large particles, dissolution, wall reaction, gravitational holdup, or sampling loss. A
useful validation program must therefore combine separate-effect measurements with integral loop
experiments.

 129

The validation basis should begin with the carrier liquid because the carrier properties enter nearly
every transport and interaction closure. Particle-size evolution experiments are needed to isolate
aggregation and breakup. These experiments should begin from a known particle-size distribution
and track the time-dependent distribution under controlled temperature, salt composition, particle
loading, flow condition, and turbulence intensity. The model predicts aggregation as a second-order
process in particle number concentration, with a kernel that separates encounter frequency from
collision efficiency.

The collision-efficiency measurements should be supported by interfacial characterization. In
molten salts, attachment probability can depend on wetting, surface chemistry, oxide state, salt
composition, redox potential, and structured interfacial layers. Measurements of contact angle,
work of adhesion, particle–salt wetting behavior, and particle–particle attachment probability would
provide a physical basis for the 𝛼 parameters used in the model. Without these measurements,
aggregation multipliers can be tuned to match a specific experiment but may not extrapolate reliably
to another salt, temperature, or particle composition.

Breakup validation requires experiments in which large particles or aggregates are exposed to known
hydrodynamic stresses. The model uses breakup frequencies and daughter-distribution assumptions
that depend on turbulent dissipation, surface-tension-like resistance, carrier density, and empirical
constants. Validation experiments should measure the onset of fragmentation, breakup rate, and
daughter-size distribution for representative aggregates. These measurements are needed because
a symmetric binary breakup rule is mathematically convenient but may not describe real molten-
salt agglomerates. Real aggregates may fail by erosion, asymmetric fragmentation, compaction
followed by rupture, or composition-dependent fracture. Each of these mechanisms would produce
a different redistribution of mass across the sectional grid.

Reactive precipitation and dissolution models require a validation path that measures solution
chemistry and particle populations simultaneously. Dissolved-species concentrations alone are
insufficient, and final particle-size distributions alone are also insufficient. Experiments should
measure the relevant dissolved reactants and products, the corresponding particulate phases, de-
posited reaction products, and the time-dependent particle-size distribution as functions of temper-
ature, chemical activity, redox condition, initial solute loading, and carrier-salt composition. These
data are needed to constrain the equilibrium or solubility-product references, activity corrections,
interfacial free energies, molecular diffusivities, reaction enthalpies, nucleation parameters, growth
prefactors, and growth or dissolution orders used in the closure models. Time-resolved mea-
surements are especially important because nucleation-dominated transients, growth-dominated
coarsening, dissolution-dominated removal, and reaction-limited conversion can produce similar
final inventories through different physical and chemical pathways.

Wall deposition and resuspension experiments should be performed with explicit control of surface
area, wall material, surface finish, temperature gradient, flow rate, and wall shear. The deposition
model removes suspended material through a size-dependent wall-transfer velocity, while resus-
pension returns deposited material to the suspended inventory. Validation data should therefore
include suspended mass depletion, deposited mass accumulation, deposit composition, deposit size
distribution, and resuspended mass after changes in flow or shear. Total deposited mass is not
sufficient because the model predicts size-dependent wall transfer. A validation experiment should

 130

determine whether the deposited material is enriched in fine particles, large agglomerates, mixed
aggregates, or chemically precipitated particles.
Surface-specific effects need particular attention. Structural materials in molten salt systems may
develop oxide layers, corrosion films, roughness changes, or chemically active surfaces. These
surface states can alter attachment and resuspension even when the hydrodynamic deposition
velocity is unchanged. Validation should therefore include candidate structural alloys and controlled
surface preparations. Tests with smooth, roughened, oxidized, and salt-exposed surfaces would help
separate hydrodynamic transport from adhesion-limited deposition. This distinction is important
for reactor modeling because deposited radioactive material on heat-transfer surfaces or internal
structures may become a long-term holdup inventory or a delayed resuspension source.
Integral loop experiments are needed after separate-effect closures are constrained. A loop exper-
iment should provide known control volumes, flow rates, surface areas, temperature distributions,
salt composition, and injected or generated particle inventories. Sampling at multiple locations
would test the conservative control-volume transport formulation and reveal whether particle re-
distribution is dominated by inter-volume mixing, local deposition, precipitation, aggregation, or
resuspension. For the present two-control-volume model, a useful validation experiment would
impose a controlled temperature or chemistry difference between two regions so that precipita-
tion, growth, deposition, and recirculation can be observed in a geometry analogous to the model
structure used in Chapter 7.
The validation program should preserve the same inventory categories used by the model. Measure-
ments should be organized around suspended particulate mass, dissolved mass, deposited mass,
particle number concentration, size distribution, component composition, and, where possible,
mixed-particle identity. This is essential because the model is component-resolved and mass-
conservative. If an experiment reports only a bulk concentration or only a final deposit mass, many
different model pathways may fit the data. By measuring the same inventories that appear in the
residual, the validation data can identify which closure is responsible when the model disagrees
with experiment.
Uncertainty quantification should be integrated into the validation process rather than added after
calibration. The Chapter 7 sweeps showed that aggregation efficiency, aggregation-kernel strength,
carrier viscosity, turbulent dissipation, breakup resistance, and chemistry parameters can dominate
different outputs. Experimental uncertainty in these quantities should be propagated through the
PBE to determine uncertainty in suspended mass, deposited mass, dissolved concentration, char-
acteristic particle radius, largest-bin mass fraction, and effective wall-transfer coefficient. This
approach would identify whether predictive uncertainty is controlled by salt-property data, interfa-
cial attachment, breakup physics, precipitation chemistry, or wall exchange.

8.3. Future Work

Future work should extend the present liquid-phase sectional PBE in two complementary directions.
The first is to improve the physical specificity of the models already exercised in this report. The
second is to implement the additional physics developed in a broader sectional PBE theory but not

 131

yet included here, such as radionuclide decay and transmutation, liquid–gas exchange, and solid–
gas aerosol dynamics. These extensions are required for reactor source-term applications because
radioactive material can transform by decay, transfer between phases, and evolve as aerosols after
leaving the liquid salt [63, 1, 9, 61].

The most important new physics capability is radionuclide decay and transmutation. For a radionu-
clide 𝑎, the simplest decay loss in section 𝑘 is

where 𝑌𝑏→𝑎 is the branching fraction and T𝑏→𝑎,𝑘 maps parent material into the daughter distribution.
This mapping must be conservative and must allow for cases in which the daughter species has
a different molar volume, solubility class, volatility, or chemical affinity than the parent. The
decay-chain structure is the Bateman transformation problem embedded in a sectional inventory
framework [63, 2, 3].

Decay should be integrated with the dissolved, suspended, deposited, and future gas-phase invento-
ries. A soluble parent may produce an insoluble daughter that nucleates or attaches to particles. An
insoluble parent embedded in a particle may produce a soluble daughter that dissolves into the salt.
A deposited radionuclide may decay on a surface and alter deposit composition or resuspension
behavior. A volatile daughter may transfer to a cover gas. These transformations require a decay
source term that is coupled to chemistry and phase state, not simply a reduction in total activity
[63, 11, 4].

Decay heat should also be coupled to the thermal state. A local heat source can be written
schematically as

Because temperature affects viscosity, diffusivity, solubility, activity coefficients, growth, dissolu-
tion, and thermophoresis, decay heat can feed back into the same mechanisms that controlled the
Chapter 7 results [63, 1, 9, 62].

Liquid–gas exchange is the next major extension. The present main-document implementation
focuses on liquid-phase suspended particles and deposited inventories. Reactor source-term anal-
ysis also requires material transfer across liquid–gas interfaces by entrainment, bubble bursting,
evaporation, aerosol formation, condensation, aerosol deposition, and pool scrubbing. A coupled

The mapping Π 𝑘→𝑚 should not be assumed to be one-to-one because particles or droplets leaving the
liquid may change size and composition through entrainment, evaporation, salt aerosol formation,
or condensation. The reverse process should be represented analogously for gas-to-liquid capture,
dissolution, or scrubbing [39, 64, 9, 65].

Solid–gas aerosol dynamics should be implemented using the same sectional infrastructure but with
gas-appropriate closures. A gas-phase PBE would include advection, diffusion, Brownian coagula-
tion, turbulent coagulation, gravitational settling, thermophoresis, diffusiophoresis, condensation,
evaporation, wall deposition, resuspension, filtration, and radioactive decay:
         𝜕𝑛𝑔                                    (𝑔)     (𝑔)          (𝑔)    (𝑔)       (𝑔)

 𝜕𝑡
Gas-phase closures differ from liquid-phase closures because slip correction, Knudsen-number
effects, gas viscosity, vapor condensation, thermophoresis, diffusiophoresis, and aerosol wall de-
position can dominate the dynamics. This extension is necessary for modeling material that leaves
the salt and enters cover gas, containment, off-gas, or filtration systems [1, 13, 9, 4].

Ultimately, the sectional PBE should be coupled to reactor-scale thermal-hydraulic and chem-
istry calculations. The two-control-volume loop used in Chapter 7 is appropriate for verification
and performance testing, but reactor applications require networks containing core regions, heat
exchangers, pumps, piping, stagnant volumes, cover-gas spaces, filters, and drain tanks. The

 133

control-volume formulation is compatible with such networks because it already expresses parti-
cle and dissolved inventories through conservative face fluxes and local source terms. Coupling
this structure to system codes would allow local temperature, flow, turbulence, surface area, salt
composition, and interfacial area to drive the PBE closures dynamically [3, 2, 10].
The long-term objective is an uncertainty-aware, experimentally validated model for radionuclide
and particulate inventory evolution in molten salt reactors. The present report establishes the
liquid-phase sectional framework, verifies the implementation, and identifies the dominant sensi-
tivities for noble metals, CrF2 , heterogeneous particles, and carrier salts. Future work should use
experimental data to constrain the closure parameters, implement decay and multiphase exchange,
and propagate uncertainties through reactor-scale calculations of suspended inventory, deposited
inventory, dissolved inventory, gas-phase aerosol loading, and radionuclide release [63, 3, 9, 61].

 134

9. CONCLUSION

This report developed, implemented, verified, and exercised a sectional population balance equation
(PBE) framework for modeling particle transport, transformation, and surface exchange in liquid
systems, with emphasis on molten-salt reactor applications. The model provides a mechanistic
treatment of suspended particulate material, dissolved precursor species, and deposited surface
inventories within a common mass-conserving control-volume formulation. This is important
for molten-salt systems because radionuclide-bearing and corrosion-product materials may exist
simultaneously as dissolved species, insoluble particles, precipitated particles, mixed aggregates, or
deposited material on structural surfaces. The location and physical form of that material determine
whether it remains mobile in the circulating salt, accumulates on surfaces, participates in further
chemical exchange, or becomes available for later resuspension.

The formulation begins from a continuous PBE for the particle-size distribution and decomposes the
source term into aggregation, breakup, nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, and resuspen-
sion mechanisms. This decomposition allows each physical process to be represented independently
while still contributing to a coupled inventory balance. The continuous model was converted to
a mass-based sectional discretization so that the particle-size domain is represented by discrete
bins with conservative redistribution rules. This sectional approach preserves the size depen-
dence needed for particle transport and deposition while remaining compatible with reduced-order
reactor-loop and control-volume calculations.

A central outcome of the work is the component-resolved mass formulation. Instead of advancing
only a total number density, the implemented model advances sectional mass inventories and re-
constructs number concentrations as needed for number-based closures such as aggregation. For
multicomponent particles, each size section can carry separate material inventories, allowing effec-
tive density, composition, and componentwise conservation to be maintained as particles aggregate,
break, deposit, resuspend, grow, or dissolve. This capability is essential for heterogeneous molten-
salt particle systems, where noble metals, precipitated corrosion products, and mixed aggregates
can coexist and evolve together.

Verification studies demonstrated that the implemented residual solves the intended discrete equa-
tions. Aggregation and breakup redistribute material among particle-size sections while conserving
total particulate mass. Dissolved–particulate exchange conserves the combined dissolved and par-
ticulate inventory when no external sources or sinks are present. Deposition and resuspension
exchange material between suspended and deposited states with the correct signs and inventory
bookkeeping. Multicomponent tests confirmed that component masses and effective-density recon-
struction remain consistent during particle interactions. These results establish that the observed
trends in the performance studies arise from the selected physical closures and parameter values
rather than from numerical inventory errors.

 135

Noble-metal studies showed that insoluble Mo–Rh particle populations are controlled primarily
by aggregation, breakup, carrier transport properties, and wall exchange. The strongest particle-
growth sensitivities were associated with the global aggregation-efficiency multiplier 𝛼 and the
aggregation-kernel multiplier 𝛽, which respectively control the probability of persistent attachment
and the rate of particle encounters. Breakup parameters, turbulent dissipation, carrier viscos-
ity, and deposition parameters determined whether the larger agglomerates remained suspended,
fragmented, or deposited. The focused Cartesian-product deposition sweep demonstrated that
deposition is not controlled by any single parameter in isolation; it depends on the coupled bal-
ance among aggregation, breakup resistance, turbulent mixing, viscous damping, and wall-transfer
physics.
Studies of CrF2 demonstrated the importance of dissolved–particulate coupling. Unlike the noble-
metal cases, the CrF2 particulate inventory is created and removed through chemistry. Dissolved
Cr2+ can nucleate and grow particulate CrF2 , while dissolution can return particulate material to
the dissolved state. Therefore, the model response depends on both transport and thermochem-
ical driving forces, including dissolved precursor loading, activity corrections, solubility-product
parameters, interfacial free energy, molecular diffusivity, reaction enthalpy, and growth or disso-
lution kinetics. This result is important for reactor applications because corrosion products and
precipitating species cannot be modeled accurately using particle transport alone; the chemical
state determines how much material is available to the particulate phase.
Heterogeneous-particle studies showed that mixed-particle formation can substantially alter both
size evolution and deposition behavior. When noble-metal particles, CrF2 particles, and mixed
populations are allowed to interact, aggregation can transfer material into larger, compositionally
mixed aggregates. These particles may have different effective densities, transport behavior,
breakup resistance, and wall-transfer rates than the original single-material populations. This result
is significant for molten-salt reactor source-term modeling because a radionuclide or corrosion
product may not transport independently. Its retention or mobility may depend on whether it
becomes incorporated into a mixed particle with other material classes.
The carrier-salt studies showed that salt identity affects particle behavior through coupled transport
and chemistry pathways. Lower-viscosity chloride salts generally produced more mobile particles
and larger wall-transfer coefficients, while higher-viscosity fluoride salts suppressed particle motion
and deposition. However, the effect of carrier properties was not universal across particle classes.
For noble metals, carrier properties mainly changed the transport and deposition of an existing
insoluble inventory. For CrF2 , carrier properties also interacted with precipitation and dissolution.
For heterogeneous particles, carrier properties influenced both wall transfer and mixed-aggregate
formation. These results indicate that molten-salt particle deposition should not be represented
by a single salt-independent removal coefficient. It depends on particle size, composition, carrier
viscosity, density, turbulence, chemistry, and wall interaction.
Overall, the report demonstrates that a sectional PBE framework is a useful and extensible basis
for modeling particulate behavior in molten-salt systems and other liquid nuclear systems. The
approach provides a consistent way to track suspended, dissolved, and deposited inventories; to
represent size-dependent transport and surface exchange; and to couple insoluble particles, soluble
precipitates, and heterogeneous aggregates. The model is most valuable as a mechanistic analysis
and sensitivity tool in its current form. It identifies which processes control particle growth, wall

 136

deposition, and inventory partitioning, and it provides a framework for incorporating improved
closure models as experimental data become available.
The principal limitation of the present work is that predictive accuracy depends on closure parame-
ters that remain uncertain for reactor-relevant molten salts, particles, surfaces, and thermal-hydraulic
conditions. Aggregation efficiencies, breakup constants, solubility parameters, and growth or disso-
lution kinetics require experimental validation. The verification studies confirm that the numerical
model is internally consistent, but experimental data are needed to determine whether the physical
closures produce quantitatively accurate predictions.
Future development should therefore proceed along two paths. First, the liquid-phase closures
should be refined using material-specific and salt-specific experimental data, especially for aggre-
gation efficiency, heterogeneous attachment, breakup, precipitation, dissolution, deposition, and
resuspension. Second, the broader physics described in the sectional PBE theory should be imple-
mented, particularly radionuclide decay and transmutation, liquid–gas exchange, volatile-species
transport, and solid–gas aerosol dynamics. These additions are needed to extend the present liquid-
phase particulate model into a more complete framework for radionuclide inventory evolution,
deposition, retention, and release in molten-salt reactor systems.
The work presented here provides the foundation for that development. It establishes a verified,
mass-conserving, component-resolved sectional PBE implementation; demonstrates its behavior for
insoluble noble metals, soluble CrF2 , heterogeneous mixed particles, and carrier-salt variations; and
identifies the dominant sensitivities that should guide future validation and model improvement.
With experimentally constrained closures and additional decay and multiphase capabilities, the
framework can support predictive analysis of particulate and radionuclide transport in advanced
nuclear reactor systems.

 137

 138

APPENDIX A. Control Volume Formulation: Detailed Development

This appendix provides additional detail for the control-volume formulation used to discretize
spatial transport of particle and dissolved inventories. The formulation combines finite-volume
spatial conservation with the sectional population-balance representation used throughout the report
[2, 3, 1, 9].

A.1. Domain Discretization

The spatial domain is divided into control volumes. Each control volume 𝑖 has:
 • volume 𝑉𝑖 ;
 • bounding faces indexed by 𝑓 ;
 • face areas 𝐴 𝑓 ;
 • outward-pointing face normals n̂ 𝑓 .
Neighboring control volumes exchange suspended particle mass and dissolved species through
shared faces. This control-volume representation is useful for coupled transport and source-term
models because fluxes through internal faces can be written as equal-and- opposite inventory
transfers between adjacent cells [3, 2].

APPENDIX B. Detailed Comparison: Aerosol Dynamics vs. Particle
 Dynamics in Liquids

This appendix compares particle dynamics in gases and liquids. The implemented framework is
focused on liquid-phase particle transport, but this comparison is retained to clarify why liquid-
particle models use different closures than aerosol models. Although the mathematical structure
of the population balance equation is similar in both cases, the relevant transport regimes, collision
efficiencies, and force balances can differ substantially [1, 9, 13, 3].

B.1. Knudsen Number and Continuum Validity

The Knudsen number is
 2𝜆
 Kn = ,
 𝑑𝑝
where 𝜆 is the molecular mean free path and 𝑑 𝑝 is particle diameter [13, 1, 9].

In gases, the molecular mean free path can be large enough that small particles enter transition or
free-molecular regimes. At standard conditions in air,

 𝜆 ≈ 65 nm.

Particles smaller than approximately 100 nm may therefore require non-continuum corrections.
The usual regimes are:

 • continuum regime: Kn ≪ 1;

 • transition regime: Kn ∼ 1;

 • free-molecular regime: Kn ≫ 1.

In liquids, molecular length scales are much smaller. For example, a representative molecular
diameter in water is approximately 0.3 nm. Even for 10 nm particles, the continuum approximation
is typically appropriate. Thus liquid-particle models generally use continuum Stokes drag without
gas-slip corrections [1, 13, 7].

 143

B.2. Slip Correction

For aerosols, the Cunningham slip correction factor 𝐶𝑐 modifies Stokes drag:
 h  𝛾 i
                              𝐶𝑐 = 1 + Kn 𝛼 + 𝛽 exp −           ,
 Kn
where, for air,
                              𝛼 ≈ 1.257,       𝛽 ≈ 0.400,             𝛾 ≈ 1.10.

The slip correction can be large for ultrafine aerosols [13, 1, 9].
For particles in liquids,
 𝐶𝑐 = 1,
so no Cunningham slip correction is applied.

B.3. Density Contrast

In gas-particle systems, the particle density is usually much larger than the gas density:

 𝜌𝑝 ≫ 𝜌 𝑓 .

Buoyancy is often negligible, and the settling velocity may be approximated as

 𝜌 𝑝 𝑑 2𝑝 𝑔𝐶𝑐
 𝑢𝑠 = .
 18𝜇

In liquid-particle systems, buoyancy is significant because the density contrast is smaller. The
Stokes settling velocity is
 (𝜌 𝑝 − 𝜌 𝑓 )𝑑 2𝑝 𝑔
 𝑢𝑠 = .
 18𝜇

Thus liquid-particle settling depends directly on the density difference 𝜌 𝑝 − 𝜌 𝑓 , not only on particle
density [1, 9, 13].

B.5. Interparticle Forces and Collision Efficiency

B.5.1. Gas-Phase Systems

In many gas-phase aerosol models, the collision efficiency is often assumed to be near unity.
Interparticle forces may be neglected during most of the approach trajectory, although van der Waals
attraction, electrostatic effects, and particle charge can matter in specialized cases [1, 9, 66].

 145

B.5.2. Liquid-Phase Systems

the total interaction energy as
 𝑉𝑇 (ℎ) = 𝑉vdW (ℎ) + 𝑉elec (ℎ),
where ℎ is the surface-to-surface separation distance [21, 22, 7].

B.6. Breakup

Breakup is often less important in many aerosol applications unless particles experience wall
impact, high shear, or other strong mechanical stresses. In liquids, breakup is often important
because turbulent shear and hydrodynamic stresses can continuously act on aggregates. The steady
particle size distribution may therefore reflect a dynamic balance between aggregation and breakup
[37, 2, 3, 6].

APPENDIX C. Interparticle Force Models: DLVO Theory and Alternatives

This appendix summarizes interparticle force models relevant to collision efficiency and particle
adhesion in liquid systems. These models are useful for interpreting aggregation, deposition, and
resuspension behavior when particle–particle or particle–surface interactions significantly affect
sticking probability [7, 21, 22, 13].

 𝑉𝑇 (ℎ) = 𝑉vdW (ℎ) + 𝑉elec (ℎ),

where ℎ is the surface-to-surface separation distance [21, 22, 7].

C.2. DLVO Stability and Collision Efficiency

The total interaction energy 𝑉𝑇 (ℎ) may contain:

 • a primary minimum at very small separation;

 • an energy barrier at intermediate separation;

 • a secondary minimum at larger separation.

A large positive energy barrier inhibits aggregation. The collision efficiency 𝛼 may be estimated
using a Fuchs stability-ratio approach:
                              ∫ ∞                                 −1
 𝑉𝑇 (ℎ) 2 ℎ
                         𝛼=          exp          (2 + ℎ/𝑟 eff ) 𝑑         .
 0 𝑘 𝐵𝑇 𝑟 eff

For a simple barrier-dominated case,
  
 𝑉max
 𝛼 ≈ exp − ,
 𝑘 𝐵𝑇

where 𝑉max is the maximum interaction energy barrier. The Fuchs stability-ratio approach con-
nects interparticle interaction potentials to aggregation rate reductions relative to diffusion-limited
collision rates [13, 7, 1].

The ionic strength at which the energy barrier vanishes is the critical coagulation concentration. Its
strong dependence on counterion valence is commonly summarized by the Schulze–Hardy rule,

 CCC ∝ 𝑧−6 .

This behavior is a classical consequence of electrolyte-mediated destabilization in DLVO-type
colloid interactions [22, 7].

C.3. Extended DLVO Theory

Classical DLVO theory may be incomplete at short range or for chemically complex surfaces.
Extended DLVO theory adds additional interaction contributions:

 𝑉𝑇xDLVO (ℎ) = 𝑉vdW (ℎ) + 𝑉elec (ℎ) + 𝑉AB (ℎ) + 𝑉steric (ℎ) + 𝑉hyd (ℎ).

These additional terms are often introduced to represent short-range hydration, hydrophobic, steric,
acid–base, or other surface-chemistry- dependent interactions not captured by classical DLVO
theory [7].

 151

C.3.1. Hydration Forces

Hydration forces arise from structured solvent layers near hydrophilic surfaces. A representative
repulsive hydration interaction is

 𝑉hyd (ℎ) = 𝜋𝑟 eff 𝑊0𝜆 0 𝑒 −ℎ/𝜆0 ,

where 𝑊0 is an interaction energy scale and 𝜆0 is a short decay length [7].

C.3.2. Hydrophobic Interaction

Hydrophobic surfaces can experience an additional attractive interaction:

 𝑉hydrophobic (ℎ) = −2𝜋𝑟 eff 𝛾𝑖 𝑒 −ℎ/𝐷 0 ,

where 𝐷 0 is a characteristic decay length [7].

C.3.3. Steric Stabilization

Adsorbed polymer or surface layers can create steric repulsion. For a polymer layer of thickness 𝛿,
a representative steric interaction is
 (

                                                             ℎ + 2𝛿 − 1 , ℎ < 2𝛿,

where Γ is the adsorbed amount and 𝑠 is a polymer segment length [7].

C.3.4. Acid–Base Interactions

The van Oss–Chaudhury–Good approach decomposes surface energy into Lifshitz–van der Waals
and acid–base components:

C.4. Contact Mechanics and Adhesion

Once particles reach contact, adhesion and deformation determine whether they remain attached.
Contact mechanics models provide estimates of contact radius, pull-off force, and adhesion energy
[26, 36, 32].

C.4.1. Hertz Theory

C.4.3. DMT Theory

C.4.4. Tabor Parameter

The Tabor parameter indicates whether JKR or DMT behavior is more appropriate:
 ! 1/3
 𝑅eff 𝑊 2
 𝜇𝑇 = ,
 𝐸 ∗2 𝑧30

where 𝑧0 is an equilibrium separation distance.
Typical interpretation is:
    • 𝜇𝑇 < 0.1: DMT limit;
 • 0.1 < 𝜇𝑇 < 5: transition regime;
 • 𝜇𝑇 > 5: JKR limit.
Contact-mechanics regime selection is important for interpreting particle sticking, rebound, pull-
off, and resuspension thresholds [26, 36, 32, 33].

C.5. Application to Collision Efficiency

For detailed collision-efficiency calculations, particle trajectories may be modeled using
 𝑑u 𝑝
 𝑚𝑝 = Fdrag + FBrownian + FDLVO + Fgravity ,
 𝑑𝑡
where
 𝑑𝑉𝑇
                                           FDLVO (ℎ) = −           n̂.
 𝑑ℎ

In practical sectional PBE calculations, the detailed interaction model is often reduced to a collision
efficiency 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 or stability ratio that modifies the collision-frequency kernel:

 𝛽𝑖 𝑗 = 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 𝛽0,𝑖 𝑗 .

 154

This reduction separates the transport-controlled encounter rate from the probability that an en-
counter produces a persistent aggregate [13, 1, 7, 2].
Depending on available data, 𝛼𝑖 𝑗 may be:
 • computed from DLVO or extended-DLVO parameters;
 • tabulated as a function of chemistry and particle size;
 • fitted to experimental aggregation data;
 • prescribed as a constant for sensitivity studies.
This flexibility is useful in sectional PBE simulations because detailed surface-force data are often
unavailable, whereas measured aggregation rates or sensitivity studies can still constrain effective
collision efficiencies [3, 8, 6].

C.6. Model Selection Guide

 System characteristic Suggested model
 Charged particles in electrolyte Classical DLVO
 Short-range hydrophilic interactions Extended DLVO with hydration forces
 Hydrophobic surfaces Extended DLVO with hydrophobic attraction
 Polymer-coated particles Steric stabilization model
 Hard particles in contact DMT adhesion model
 Soft particles in contact JKR adhesion model
 Intermediate adhesion regime Maugis–Dugdale transition model

APPENDIX D. Analytical Solutions for Verification

This appendix lists analytical and limiting-case solutions that are useful for verification of the
sectional population balance implementation. These cases provide checks on aggregation, breakup,
and conservation behavior.

D.1. Constant Kernel Aggregation

For the constant aggregation kernel
                                            𝛽(𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) = 𝛽0 ,
with no breakup, nucleation, growth, dissolution, deposition, resuspension, or transport, the total
number concentration evolves as
 𝑁0
 𝑁total (𝑡) = ,
 1 + 𝑁0 𝛽0 𝑡/2
where 𝑁0 is the initial total number concentration.
The total particle volume concentration remains constant:

 𝑉total (𝑡) = 𝑉0 .

This case is useful for verifying that aggregation decreases particle number while conserving total
particulate mass.

D.2. Sum Kernel Aggregation

For the sum kernel
                                      𝛽(𝑣 𝑖 , 𝑣 𝑗 ) = 𝛽1 (𝑣 𝑖 + 𝑣 𝑗 ),
the total number concentration evolves as

                                    𝑁total (𝑡) = 𝑁0 exp(−𝛽1𝑉0 𝑡),

where 𝑉0 is the initial total particle volume concentration.
The total particle volume remains conserved:

 𝑉total (𝑡) = 𝑉0 .

 157

This case provides a second analytical aggregation test with a size-dependent kernel.

D.3. Pure Breakup

For constant breakup rate
 𝑎(𝑣) = 𝑎 0 ,
with binary symmetric fragmentation and no aggregation or transport, total particle volume is
conserved:
 𝑉total (𝑡) = 𝑉0 .

For a monodisperse initial condition, repeated symmetric breakup increases the total number of
particles while preserving mass. In an ideal generation-based approximation,

                                          𝑁total (𝑡) ≈ 𝑁0 2𝑎0 𝑡 ,

for large 𝑎 0 𝑡, where 𝑁0 is the initial number concentration.
This case is useful for verifying that breakup redistributes mass to smaller bins without creating or
destroying total particulate mass.

D.4. Self-Preserving Size Distributions

For certain aggregation kernels, the particle size distribution approaches a self-preserving or self-
similar form at long times. The distribution may be written schematically as
  
 𝑁total (𝑡) 𝑣
 𝑛(𝑣, 𝑡) = Φ ,
                                                𝑣¯ (𝑡)       𝑣¯ (𝑡)

where Φ is a time-independent shape function and 𝑣¯ (𝑡) is the mean particle volume.
Although the exact self-preserving form depends on the kernel, this behavior provides a useful
qualitative verification target. At long times, simulations should approach a stable normalized
distribution shape for kernels known to admit self-similar solutions.

D.5. Conservation Checks

For closed systems with no external sources, analytical conservation statements provide the most
important verification targets.
For aggregation-only systems,
 𝑑 ∑︁
 𝑀𝑘 = 0.
 𝑑𝑡 𝑘

 158

For breakup-only systems,
 𝑑 ∑︁
 𝑀𝑘 = 0.
 𝑑𝑡 𝑘

For dissolved-particle exchange,
 𝑑  dissolved 
 𝑀 + 𝑀 particulate = 0.
 𝑑𝑡

For suspended-deposited exchange,
 𝑑  suspended 
 𝑀 + 𝑀 deposited = 0.
 𝑑𝑡

For multicomponent particles, each component should satisfy
 𝑑 ∑︁
 𝑀𝑘,𝑐 = 0
 𝑑𝑡 𝑘

under aggregation and breakup when no component-specific sources or sinks are active.
